THE COVENANT OF LIGHT — BOOK ONE: The Forgetting

###SECTION: Prologue — The Echo###
The Echo

 There is a particular kind of rain in that city — not a
downpour, nothing so decisive, but a fine grey per-
sistence that does not so much fall as hang, soaking
the shoulders of a coat by slow degrees while a man
stands telling himself he is about to move on. And
on an ordinary evening, at the mouth of an ordinary
station, in the thick of the homeward crowd, a man
stood in that rain and did not move on, because he
was looking for someone.
   He could not have told you who. That was the
difficulty. If you had stopped him — and people
did glance, because a well-dressed man standing
motionless in the rush-hour rush, turning his head
slowly across the faces, has the look of someone
waiting to be met — if you had stopped him and
asked who he was waiting for, he would have had no
answer that did not embarrass him. He was not wait-
ing for anyone. He had no appointment. He knew no
one who would be coming out of that station at that
hour. And yet he stood, and scanned the crowd pour-
ing up the steps and breaking around him, face af-
ter face after face under the wet light, with the fixed

                           1

and faintly desperate attention of a man scanning an
arrivals board for a flight that is hours overdue; and
what he was feeling, under the embarrassment and
the damp and the sensible voice telling him to go
home, was something he had no permission, in his
own carefully ordered understanding of the world,
to feel at all.
   He was not a man given to feelings of this kind.
He made his living, and a considerable one, by the
precise opposite — by the cool dismantling of ex-
actly such impulses in other people, by finding the
soft unreasoned place in an argument and press-
ing on it until it gave. He did not believe in pulls.
He did not believe in the sensation, which he had
nonetheless been having, on and off, for most of his
adult life and with a sharpening insistence these last
months, that somewhere in the same city, walking
the same wet streets, riding the same trains, there
was a particular person he was meant to find — not
wanted to find; meant to; the verb arrived in him al-
ready conjugated that way and would not be talked
into anything softer. He had examined the feeling
the way he examined everything, held it up to the
light, looked for its mechanism. Loneliness, he had
decided. A man past a certain age, unmarried, suc-
cessful in the hollow way that success arrives, in-
venting a romance to give shape to the hollow. He
had labelled it, and filed it, and felt rather pleased
with the diagnosis. And the feeling had listened po-
litely to the diagnosis and then gone on exactly as
before, as the deepest things do, untroubled by be-
ing explained.

                          2

   What unsettled him — what was unsettling him
now, standing in the rain past all reason — was
not the pull itself. It was that the pull seemed to
know something he did not. It had a direction. That
was the part his good cool mind could not file. A
loneliness invented to fill a hollow does not have
a compass-bearing; it does not make a man’s head
turn, against his will, toward the eastern stairwell
rather than the western; it does not tighten in the
chest when a particular train is announced and
slacken when it has emptied and the face was not
among them. This did. Tonight it was doing it badly,
painfully, like something straining toward a thing
just out of its reach; and he stood in the grey rain
among a thousand strangers and felt, with a cer-
tainty that appalled the lawyer in him, two contra-
dictory things at once: that the person he was look-
ing for was real, was near, was somewhere in this
very city breathing the same wet air — and that he
had never, in this life, once seen their face.
   He did not find them that evening. The crowd
thinned, the rain went on, the announced trains
came and emptied and the face was not among them,
and at last the sensible voice won, as it usually
did, and he turned up his collar and walked home
through the shining streets feeling foolish and ob-
scurely bereaved, a man who had stood up an ap-
pointment that did not exist. He would tell no one.
There was no one to tell, and nothing to tell them.
By morning he would have filed it again under lone-
liness and gone back to his bright hard life. But the
feeling would not be filed for long, because the feel-

                          3

ing was not loneliness, and it was not invention, and
it was older — far, far older — than the man hav-
ing it; older than his name, older than his city, older
than the rain and the station and the whole grey pa-
tient world he was walking home through. He was
looking for a particular soul in a crowd of strangers
because he had, once, in a place before all places,
known that soul; had stood beside it in a light too
great to remember; had heard it make, alongside
his own, the only promise that has ever finally mat-
tered. He had simply forgotten. They all forget. That
is the first thing that happens, and the cruelest, and
the whole of the long ache of the world begins in it.
   For every soul walking those wet streets, every
face in that crowd, every stranger he had searched
and discarded, had begun in the same place he had
begun, and had made the same promise, and had
forgotten it just as completely; and the homeward
crowd of an ordinary evening was, had any of them
only known it, a river of the amnesiac children of a
single enormous Yes, spoken once, together, before
the first morning of the world. To understand why a
clever, faithless, comfortable man stood in the rain
that evening searching the face of every stranger for
one he had never seen — to understand the pull, and
the direction, and the ache that no diagnosis would
file — you cannot begin with the man, or the station,
or the rain. You have to go back. Past that evening,
past his birth, past the building of the city and the
laying of its stones; past the whole long history of his
kind; past the kindling of the sun and the scattering
of the stars; back, and back, to a darkness before

                           4

there was any light to break it, and a gathering of
which no eye has ever seen the like, and a question
put to the whole of unborn humanity at once, and
the answer they gave — the answer he gave — in
the warm dark, before the world.
  That is where this begins. Not in the rain. Long
before the rain.

###SECTION: Overture — Before the First of the Worlds###
Before the First of the Worlds

 He had carried a thousand thousand messages
across the unfinished dark, and never once been
afraid. That was the first thing he noticed — before
he could put any name at all to the thing itself — that
he was, for the very first time since he had begun to
be, afraid; and that he had no word for it, because
the word had not yet been needed by anyone any-
where, and so had not yet been made.
  Behind him, and below him, and high above him
— for there was direction now, though there were as
yet no creatures small enough to need it — the great
work went on. It had been going on far longer than
there were numbers to count it, and he had been in
the heart of it the whole of that while: the raising of
the vast dark canopy that would one day be called
the heavens; the kindling, one by one and then in
their uncountable thousands, of lights so enormous
that each single one of them was a country, until the
new dark itself seemed to take fire and stream with
them; the slow, unhurried, immense labour of the
mighty ones who bore up the Throne, whose backs

                          8

did not bend though the weight upon them was the
weight of all that was; and through all of it, under
all of it, holding all of it together, a sound — not mu-
sic, quite, and not speech, but the thing that both
of those would one day be only the poor and far-off
echoes of. He had a place in that work, and his place
was to carry, and to look, and to do gladly as he was
bidden; and he had never once wished for any other;
and he had been, for the whole vast length of his
bright existence, simply and entirely and uncompli-
catedly happy.
   And then he had been sent to look upon two
things. And he had come back wrong.
   The first was a garden — though garden is far too
small and too poor a word, and he was never after-
ward able to find a better. It had been made, and
then set gently aside, as one sets aside the most pre-
cious thing one owns against the coming of the one
guest worthy to be given it; and it stood empty, and
waiting, and it was the most beautiful thing that he
had ever in his long life been permitted to see. There
was water in it that moved like light, and light in it
that fell and pooled like water. There were colours
he had no names for and knew, somehow, that no
eye yet to be made would ever quite be strong
enough to bear. And beneath all of it lay a stillness
— a vast, deep, unshakeable peace — that went into
him as he stood there and would not afterward come
out again, so that some small part of him would go
on standing at that bright threshold forever, aching
quietly to go in. Go back, he had been bidden. Look
again. And he had gone back. And he had found that

                           9

the whole of that beauty had been ringed about, on
every side and with great care, by hard and bitter
things — by thirst, and by long labour, and by the
giving-up of the very things a heart wants most —
so that the one road into the loveliest place that
had ever been made ran uphill the whole way, and
against the grain, straight through everything that
a soft and living thing would flinch from and refuse.
He had not understood it. And standing there, look-
ing at the thorns heaped high around the rose, he
had felt for the first time the faint cold edge of the
thing that had no name.
  The second thing was fire. He was never able to
tell it afterward — not for want of words, though
there were none, but because some deep part of him
drew back from the remembering, and would go on
drawing back to the very end of everything. He had
looked upon it only for the space of time it takes a
thing to be seen, and not one instant longer; and
in that space he had understood that there was a
place being readied where the peace of the garden
was not, and the opposite of that peace was — and
that the opposite of that peace was worse, by a dis-
tance he had no way to measure, than he had ever
guessed the worlds contained the room to hold. And
then again: Go back. Look again. And he had made
himself go back to it. And he had found that this
place, too, had been ringed about on every side —
not with thorns, but with flowers. The long, gentle,
downhill road that ran to the fire had been strewn its
whole sweet length with everything soft, and lovely,
and easy, and pleasant to the touch; so that a crea-

                         10

ture walking it would feel itself, until the very last
step of all, to be carried tenderly somewhere kind.
   He had come back into the nearness with his
wings folded close and his light gone unsteady, car-
rying a thing he had never carried before and now
could not set down. It was not doubt — never had
been, never would be. It was nearer to grief; and
nearer still to that nameless cold he had felt at the
edge of the garden, grown now to fill the whole of
him. And the strangest part of it — the part that un-
settled him most — was that it was not for himself.
He stood in no danger; he had never in his existence
stood in any danger. It was for someone he had not
yet so much as met. For the small, soft, unmade crea-
tures of clay he had lately been given to understand
were coming — not yet shaped, not yet anything at
all, only promised — who would be set down, every
one of them, between that uphill road and that down-
hill one, and made to choose, in the dark, with the
sweet way falling away easily beneath their feet and
the hard way climbing out of sight above them. He
found that he could not put it into a question. It was
far too large to be made into a question. In the end it
left him not as words at all, but as something much
smaller and much older: the first sound of fear that
had ever been made in all of creation.
   And he was answered. Not in the way he had
braced himself for — not with a reason set out plain
and flat, that he might hold it up and take comfort
from its edges. He was answered as the deep sea
answers a single stone let fall into it: the whole of
it moved, soundlessly, all at once, and the small-

                          11

ness of his asking was simply gathered up and taken
down into something so vast, and so calm, and so
wholly without fear, that his own fear — never once
argued with, never corrected, never shamed — be-
gan, slowly, and against all his reason, to go still.
He could never afterward have said what was given
to him in that moment. Only that it was sure where
he had been unsure; and unhurried where he had
been afraid; and that folded somewhere deep inside
it, too far down for him to reach but near enough
to feel against his face like warmth off a hidden fire,
there was a tenderness toward those not-yet crea-
tures so far beyond his measure that it made his
own dread for them seem all at once like a single
small candle, lifted up and lost beside the rising of
a sun. Whatever was coming for the children of clay
— and hard things were coming; he had seen them —
they were loved. Loved already. Loved before they
were. Loved past the farthest edge of anything he
had known the word could be stretched to mean.
   And then, for the space of a single breath — a
mercy, or a warning, or perhaps the two of them
folded into one — he was shown a thing. Out of all
the numberless not-yet, out of the whole uncount-
able waiting promise of the clay, two small lights.
He could never have said why these two, of all the
boundless rest. They were nothing yet. They were
less than nothing. And they were, the pair of them,
already — impossibly, before they so much as were
— turned the smallest degree toward one another,
as two notes will lean together until they wake a
third sound between them that belongs to neither

                          12

one alone. And he was given to understand a sin-
gle thing concerning them; and it did not come as
words, for there were still no words; it came as a
charge laid quietly into the deep of him, and the
charge was only this: watch. Watch these two. And
see.
   He did not know what he had been asked to watch
them for. He understood almost none of it — he, the
first and the nearest and the eldest of all the made,
stood there at the rim of the great unfinished work
and understood almost none of it — and knew for
certain only that the looking had changed him, and
that he would not be changed back. He unfolded his
wings, and folded them again, and took up his place.
And far off, at the still centre of everything, where
the longest and most patient of all the labours had
come round at last to its appointed hour, the first of
the worlds caught light — and into it, small, and as-
tonished, and entirely alone, like a single low note
struck at the opening of a song that no one had
yet heard the whole of, the first of the souls came
awake.
   Has there not come upon man a period of time
when he was not yet a thing remembered?

The Qur’an · Sūrah al-Insān (76:1)

The Awakening




        14

###SECTION: Chapter One — The First Word###
The First Word

 Before the warmth, before the light, before she
knew there was anything at all to be known, there
was only this: that a moment ago she had not been,
and now, impossibly, she was — and that the being
would not hold.
   It came upon her all at once, as a spark leaps off
a struck stone: a single point of awareness lit where
an instant before there had been no one anywhere
to be aware. And in the very first flicker of it, be-
fore she had so much as the wit to wonder at her-
self, she felt herself begin to go out. There was no
pain in it; pain had not yet been made, and neither
had she been given the thing in a creature that pain
lays hold of. There was no fear in it either, and that
is worth the pausing over, for fear would find her
soon enough down the long road that was coming,
and would prove among the most faithful of all her
companions there; but it had no foothold here, in
the warm beginning of her. There was only the bare
fact of it: that the new small light of her wavered,
and thinned, and reached the very edge of guttering

                         15

back down into the nothing she had been called up
out of — and that something in her, older than the
moment and deeper than any thought, the first mo-
tion she would ever make, strained, blindly, to hold.
To stay. To go on being the impossible new thing
that she had, without asking and without warning,
become.
   And as she strained, something caught hold of her.
Warmth — the first and the oldest of all the things,
the thing that turned out to have been there the
whole time, beneath the nothing and around it, wait-
ing — closed over the guttering light of her as two
cupped hands close over a flame in a wind. The wa-
vering steadied. She did not go out. She held; and
she rested in the warmth, new and trembling, and
did not yet understand that she had been, in the first
breath of her existence, caught.
   But the warmth alone did not make her whole.
She lay in it — if lying is the word, and it is not,
for there was no beneath to lie upon — and felt her-
self still loose, still adrift, still half a thing that might
yet come apart at the edges and be lost; and she un-
derstood, in the only way she understood anything
yet, which was wordlessly and all at once, that the
warmth was not itself the anchor. It was only the
near edge of the anchor. For the warmth was not the
same in every direction. It ran. It leaned. The whole
of it inclined toward something that was not itself
— some centre, some source, some living heart out
of which all of it was endlessly pouring, the air of a
shuttered room straining toward its one open door;
and every grain of the warmth, and every grain of

                             16

her, was already bent that way, already turned that
way, telling her beneath all telling: there — that is
the one direction in which you will not come apart.
   So she turned toward it. It was the first thing she
ever did of her own accord, and she did it not know-
ing what it was, nor what it would ask of her, nor
what it would give; she simply turned, as a flower
at the edge of a field turns before a dawn it has
never seen, toward a sun it was never taught to wait
for. She turned because the turning was the deep-
est thing in her, deeper than knowing, deeper than
choosing. She gathered up the whole loose trem-
bling new glow of herself, and she leaned it toward
the centre of the warmth.
   And there she found the One who held her; and in
the finding, she became real.
   For He was. That was the first thing she ever truly
knew — before she knew that she herself was, be-
fore she had so much as stumbled on the small as-
tonishing fact of her own existence, she knew, with
a certainty that arrived whole and entire and from
every direction at once, that He was: a Presence
vast, and near, and unspeakably gentle, at the very
heart of the warmth, who was the reason that there
was any warmth, or any her, or any anything at
all. No one taught it to her. It did not arrive as a
fact is taught, set out flat to be examined; it arrived
the way the warmth itself had arrived, simply and
wholly and from everywhere at once — and it did
for her the one thing the warmth alone could not.
It gave her something to be turned toward. And a
thing that is turned, with the whole of itself, toward

                          17

the centre of all things does not drift, and does not
fray, and does not gutter out into the dark. In the
knowing that He was, she, for the first time, fully
and steadily and past all unmaking, was. He had not
merely called her up out of the nothing in some first
far instant and left her to fend. He was holding her
there — now, and in this breath, and in every breath
after it, His nearness the very floor beneath her, as
the sea is the floor beneath a wave; and she rested
upon it, and was held, and did not fall.
   Only then, steadied, anchored, made suddenly
and securely herself, did she come upon the sec-
ond thing; and the second thing was so small and
so strange and so unaccountably good that had
she owned a mouth she might have laughed with
the surprise of it. The second thing was that there
was a her. That the warmth was reaching someone.
That the knowing had somewhere to land — a small
bright cup to be poured into — and that the cup was
her own self, called up out of a not-being she would
never be able to remember or imagine, for no reason
she could anywhere find except that He had wanted
her to be.
   She turned her wonder, for the first time, upon
herself, as a very young child turns its own hand
before its face and marvels that the hand should
answer it; and she reached for her own edges and
could not find them. She was not some hard small
thing with a here and a there, a within and a with-
out. She was nearer to a glow — a single low warm
note, sounding and sustained; the soft round of light
a candle throws into a dark room, that has no border

                         18

anywhere but only fades, by the gentlest of degrees,
into the brightness all about it. And she found, look-
ing, that the very grain of her ran one way: that to
be herself at all was already, and simply, to be a
thing leaning toward the Presence at the heart of
the warmth. She had not chosen it. It was how she
had been made. And it pleased her, in the deep word-
less way that everything as yet pleased her, to find
that the whole shape of her was a turning-toward.
   And now the place she had woken into began to
give itself to her — not all at once, which would have
undone so new and so small a thing, but a gift at a
time, as one feeds a fledgling. She grew aware of
brightness beyond her own faint glow. She caught,
in the very centre of her, a thing that was to mu-
sic what the first stirring of a thought is to a spo-
ken word: a far, high, sweet, sustained reaching, al-
most too high to be caught at all, that came down
from somewhere above the very top of everything
and made her go still, and then stiller, in the sud-
den wanting to hear more of it. And carried on the
warmth there was a sweetness she had no sense
fashioned to receive and received nonetheless —
faint, and clean, and almost unbearably good, like
the smell of rain that has not yet fallen. She drank
it all in, wanting nothing, only glad; and the glad-
ness grew larger with every new gift, as a cup seems
somehow to grow the more it is filled.
   And then her widening awareness found the oth-
ers, and she understood that she was not alone. At
the edges of her, in the directions that were not
directions, there were other glows — other small

                         19

soundless soundings, each a low or a high or a mid-
dling note of its own, each woken as she had woken,
each resting now in the same astonished gladness of
having been wanted into being. She could not count
them; counting was not hers, and never would be.
She could only feel them, as one feels in a great
dark hall the warm breathing nearness of a multi-
tude, and know all at once that the hall had never
been empty — that it was filled from end to unimag-
inable end with the soft light of waking souls past all
numbering, a whole vast quiet field of them, like a
night sky brought down low and made gentle, every
star of it a someone, and every someone a thing that
He had wanted and made. And her gladness broke
its banks and ran out toward all of them, and loved
them — and this is the one place the want of a word
truly cost her, for she had no name yet for the thing,
and would have none for an age, and it was love, and
it had nowhere to put itself but out.
   But of all the uncountable lights in all that shining
field, there was one that undid the simple rest she
had only just found.
   It was not the greatest of them, nor the nearest;
it was marked off by no brightness she could have
measured. But it was turned. Turned toward her —
oriented, by some grammar of that place far older
than any grammar of any tongue, so that the grain of
its whole attention ran her way, exactly as the grain
of her own ran toward the Presence. And it was a
high light, and a quick one: restless where she was
still, reaching where she rested, bright and sharp
and already, even in the first instant of its own wak-

                          20

ing, casting about itself, already hunting, already
measuring, already asking. She felt it as a needle
feels the north and swings toward it, not knowing
why. And it troubled her. Not with fear; with mys-
tery. For she had found her anchor, and settled upon
it, and wanted nothing more in all that was — and
here, set down in the very middle of her new peace,
was a second pull that was not toward the Source
at all but toward a fellow; a question she could not
read; a someone whose first faint motion, before it
asked anything of anything else, had been toward
her. She did not understand it. She would not un-
derstand it for the whole length of two lives and the
long dark strung between them. She knew only that
it was good, deeply and particularly good, good in
a way unlike all the other goodness of that place —
and that some part of her, even now, even in the
arms of the only rest she had ever known, had al-
ready begun, without permission and without sense,
to lean a little that way too.
   It frightened her toward Him — or it would be
truer to say it sent her toward Him, for there was
still no fear in her; the strange new pull simply made
her reach again, harder, for the centre she was sure
of. And so she did the first deliberate thing of her
whole existence. Everything until now had risen in
her on its own, unwilled — the straining to hold, the
catching of the warmth, the helpless turning of a
flower. But now, out of the deep still middle of her,
knowing a little better what she did, she gathered
herself once more and leaned the whole of her to-
ward the Presence; and this leaning was the first

                         21

prayer that was ever prayed in this tale, though it
had no words, and asked for nothing, and would
never afterward be remembered. It was only the
whole of her, turned wholly toward the whole of
Him, and glad.
   And He met her. She had not known that He
would — had not known there was anywhere such a
thing as being met — had no notion that her small
turning could be seen, still less that it had been
seen, and known, and wanted, and waited for, be-
fore ever she began it. But she felt her turning re-
ceived — gently, as a held-out hand is closed upon
— and felt that something vast and unhurried had
been there on the far side of it before she so much
as began, waiting for her with a patience older than
the worlds; and that what waited met her with no
surprise, and no judgement, but with a welcome so
wide and so quiet and so wholly without edge or con-
dition that she could find no end to it however far
she leaned. A welcome that said, beneath all saying:
you. You, whom I wanted. You, for whom I made the
very wanting.
   And carried upon that welcome, as a scent is car-
ried on warm air, a word came to her — not heard
with ears, for she had none, but received in the deep
centre of her as a thing that arrived and was true. It
was a name. Her name; the name He had shaped
for her before He shaped the warmth or the field of
lights or the far high music; the name that was sim-
ply the form her own brightness took when He let
it through into being; the name she would be given
again, far off, in a world she could not yet imagine

                         22

— breathed across her wet new face by hands that
would love her, called out over her ten thousand
times through all the years of a hard and faithful
life by a mother who would never once guess that
she was speaking aloud a thing far older than the
stars. Here it was hers first, in the warmth, before
any tongue had shaped it.
   Hana.
   She could not have said what it meant; she would
not know the whole of it for a long, long time —
would not know it fully, in truth, until the very end,
sitting quiet in the dark beside a frightened stranger
with his hand in hers and the long night coming on,
when she would understand all of it at once, and
smile. But it fit her as light fits a window: not laid
upon her, not added to her, but only the name of
what she already and entirely was. She received it;
she was filled by it; and being filled, she turned a lit-
tle further toward the One who had spoken it, and
began — not knowing she did it, and having no word
for it if she had — to love Him.
   And so she rested, anchored and named and held:
the warmth, and the Presence at its heart, and the
great soft field of waking lights, and the small sweet
name that He had given her. It was enough. For now,
in the warm beginning of her, it was enough. Only —
far out at the edge of her gladness, faint as a single
thread of cool air crossing a warm room from a door
left open somewhere down an unlit passage — the
one high turned note went on burning toward her
own; and she did not know what it was, or what it
would cost her, or how far across the dark she would

                           23

one day have to go to find it again; and she leaned
toward Him, and she did not yet ask.




                        24

###SECTION: Chapter Two — The First Question###
The First Question

 Before he knew that he was, he knew that some-
thing was not him.
   This was the whole difference between them, set
down in the very first instant, before either had done
a single thing to earn or shape it: that she had wo-
ken into a knowing, and he had woken into a distinc-
tion. To her there had come, sourceless and entire,
the great warm certainty — He is — and she had
rested in it and been glad. To him came something
else. Not a lesser thing; not, whatever it might look
like from the outside, a colder one; only a thing that
faced the other way. What came to him, in his first
instant, was the awareness of a seam. The light was
one. He was not the light. And in the thin bright gap
between the one and the not — where the first thing
ended and the second began — his whole new self
came awake at once, like an eye opening, and fixed
upon the difference, and would never afterward look
away from it for long.
   The light is one, he knew. I am two. And no sooner
had he known it than he knew the next thing, which

                         25

was that he knew it — I am the one who is not the
light, and also the one who knows he is not the light
— and no sooner had he known that than there was a
third thing to set beside the first two, and a fourth to
set beside the third; and already, in the first instant
of his existence, before he had turned, or rested, or
wanted anything at all, he was counting.
   It would be easy, and it would be wrong, to call
this a sadder waking than hers. He did not feel its
sadness, if it had one; he felt, if anything, a joy —
sharp, and bright, and particular. For where she had
been given one great thing and asked of it only that
she hold it, he had been given the same great thing
and found, at once, that he could not merely hold
it; that the very grain of him was to take a thing up
and turn it in the light and hunt for the place where
it joined to the next thing, and the next; that he
had been made, from his first breath, for the seams.
She had woken facing the warmth. He woke facing
the edges — toward where one thing stopped and
another started, toward the joins and the borders
and the small dark gaps between them: the places,
though he did not yet know to call them so, where
a question can work its fingers in and begin, very
gently, to pull.
   So he counted. He could not have said what he
counted, for there was as yet almost nothing to be
counted; but the counting was him, exactly as the
turning was her, and it gave him a pleasure so keen
it was nearly an ache. One: the light. Two: himself,
the not-light. Three: the knowing of the difference.
Four: that the knowing could itself be taken up and

                          26

known, and so become a fifth thing, which could be
known in its turn, and a sixth — on and on, the new
and tireless engine of him turning and turning, find-
ing a fresh edge wherever it looked, and at every
edge a small sharp gladness that was almost, but
never quite, the deep still gladness she had found in
the turning. His was the brighter joy, and the hun-
grier. It fed, and was not filled. It found, and at once
went looking again. Even then, in the warmth, with
nothing yet to want, he was already, faintly, want-
ing.
   And out of the counting, in time — though there
was no time — came the first word that was truly and
only his. It was not a name. He would not be given
his name as she had been given hers, gently, like
honey, in the very motion of her turning; his name
would come to him later, and from higher up, and
would land on him less like a gift than like a stone
laid in the hand to be carried a long way. His first
word was not a name at all.
   His first word was a question.
   Why?
   Why two, and not one? Why a seam at all —
why edges, why the joins, why a difference set into
the very heart of things? Why was there something
standing apart from the light, something that was
not the light and knew that it was not, instead of only
the light itself, whole and seamless and entire and
enough? He turned the question over and over, as
he turned everything, and found that it had no bot-
tom; and the bottomlessness did not dismay him but
drew him, as a deep well draws the child who has

                          27

just learned to drop a stone into it and listen. He did
not know — he would not know for the better part
of two lifetimes — that the question was also, by a
long and hidden road, a kind of prayer; that to ask
Why of the One who is the only answer is, however
well it disguises itself, a way of leaning toward Him.
He thought it only a question. He was proud of it,
in the small new way he had already of being proud.
He would go on thinking it only a question for a very
long time — and the thinking-so would cost him, in
the end, nearly everything.
   And there was, in all his restless counting, one
strangeness that he marked and could not account
for. Whenever his attention went out — whenever
the bright engine of him cast about across the warm
uncountable field of the other waking lights, hunt-
ing, as it always hunted, for edges to measure and
seams to probe — it went, before it went anywhere
else, the same way. Toward one light. A low light,
and a still one, set somewhere in the direction that
was not a direction; a light that did not cast about
as his did, did not reach or measure or probe, but
only rested, turned quietly the other way, toward
the warmth at the center of all things. He could not
think why his counting should begin there, of all the
numberless places it might have begun. He noticed
only that it did — that the grain of him ran her way
as surely as the grain runs along the wood — and
that when his measuring reached her, it faltered,
and slowed, and grew, for the space of the noticing,
almost quiet; almost content; almost the very thing
he saw in her and could not name and could not, by

                          28

any counting, produce in himself. And then the rest-
lessness would close over him again, like water over
a dropped stone, and he would turn from her, and
count on, and forget that he had paused at all.
   His name came to him at last, and it did not come
as hers had come. It came from far up, and from
far off: a high clear call let down into him out of a
height he could not measure and would spend his
whole life trying to deserve. He felt the weight of
it before he felt the meaning. It settled onto him,
and it was heavy, and it was glorious, and it was too
large. Adel. The Just. He knew, in the very instant of
receiving it, three things, and he received all three
at once and never afterward managed to hold them
apart. The first was that it was a great name. The
second was that it was not, in the deepest sense,
his — that it was Another’s first, one of the names
of the One who had spoken him, a fragment struck
off the divine and laid upon a small new soul like
a king’s own robe thrown across the shoulders of
a child. And the third thing — the one that arrived
in the same breath as the honor, and would never
again be parted from it — was the cold and certain
suspicion that he was not, and might never grow to
be, large enough to fill it. That he had been named
for a perfection he could only fail. The doubt did not
come after the name, to be reasoned away at leisure.
It came folded inside the name, woven into its very
grain, so that for the whole of his existence to think
of who he was would be, in the selfsame thought, to
fear that he was not enough — and no triumph, no
mercy, no love, no whole long life of striving would

                         29

ever quite cut the one loose from the other. He was
the Just; and he doubted that he was just at all; and
the two were a single thing; and that single thing
was him.
   He turned toward Him, in the end. He was a soul,
and a soul turns; the turning is the deepest fact of
what a soul is, deeper than the counting, deeper
even than the doubt. But where her turning had car-
ried only herself — empty-handed, open, with noth-
ing in it but the gladness of being received — his
turning carried, always, a question in its hand. He
came to the warmth asking. And the asking was not
wrong; let it be said plainly, against all the long ages
in which he would be told otherwise by a cold and
patient voice, that the asking was never the wrong
thing; the questions he carried were good questions,
true ones, and the One he carried them to had made
him precisely in order to ask them. But the asking
meant that he could not, as she could, simply rest.
He would rest, and the rest would last an instant — a
single sweet instant in which the counting fell silent,
and the seam healed over, and he was, like her, only
a small soul turned wholly toward its Source — and
then some new edge would catch at the corner of
his attention, some fresh and shining why, and the
engine would start again, and the instant would be
gone, and he would not even notice that he had let
it slip.
   She had known, in her first instant, that it was
enough. He knew, in his, that it was not — not yet,
not ever quite; that there would always be one more
seam to find, one more border to walk, one more

                          30

why to chase down into the dark before he could be
still. He did not know — how could he have known?
— that the low quiet light his counting ran toward
before it ran anywhere else had been spoken into
being in the very same breath as he; that the two of
them had been made facing, a question and its rest,
a seeking and its home; that the still place he would
spend all his counting failing to reach had been set,
from the first, beside him, and given a name that
meant the very peace he lacked. He did not know
that he had already, wordlessly, without the least
idea that he was doing it, begun to lean toward the
one whom he would spend every world that was
coming losing, and finding, and losing, and finding
again.
   He knew only that he was; and that the light was
not him; and that he wanted, with the whole of his
sharp and sleepless new self, to know why.
   It was not enough.
   For now, it was not enough.




                         31

###SECTION: Chapter Three — The Gardens###
The Gardens

  She had turned toward Him, and that had been
the first thing she did of her own. The second was
smaller, and it took her so completely by surprise
that, had she owned a face, it would have shown
there: she found that she could turn toward other
things as well.
   She had not known there were other things. In the
first knowing there had been only Him, and the fact
of herself, and the nearness of the two so entire that
it had not so much as occurred to her to look past it
— as a newborn at the breast does not wonder what
lies beyond the warmth, because the warmth is, for
that hour, the whole of the world. But now, rested
in that nearness and made bold by it, she became
aware of a thing she had owned all along without
ever using: that her attention was hers to move, as
a face turns toward a sound in the dark — that it had,
as it were, a direction, and that, pointed, it would go.
   She pointed it away from Him, a little. Not in turn-
ing from Him; she could no more turn from Him than
a flame can turn from its own heat, and the thought

                          32

of it never entered her and never would. It was as a
child, safe and held in a lap, turns its head to look
at the room: the turning is not a leaving; the lap is
the very thing that makes the looking safe. And the
room, it turned out, had no walls. She pointed her
attention outward, braced for an edge, and found in-
stead that the place went on, and on, and gave no
sign anywhere at all of ending.
   What she had taken for a single place was, she
now began to see, many places — and they were
not divided one from another by any distance she
could have measured, for there was no distance, and
would be none until the world of bodies was made
and length was invented along with everything else.
They were divided by what they were about. When
she let her attention settle back into the quiet where
she had first woken, there was a particular keynote
to it: a low, founding tone, deep and unhurried, like
the first note struck upon an instrument against
which all the others will afterward be tuned. This,
she understood without being told, understanding
arriving whole and unschooled, as warmth arrives:
was the garden of the first word. She had been born
into it. It would always be, however far she wan-
dered and whatever befell her, a kind of home; and
in the long forgetting to come, in the cold world
at the bottom of everything, she would sometimes,
for no reason she could name, feel a low founding
note sound somewhere far beneath her grief, and
be steadied by it, and never once guess that it was
this.



                         33

   When she turned her attention — and the turning
was the whole of moving here; there was no walking,
no road, no near and far, only the leaning of the soul
toward what it chose — the keynote changed.
   It became, all at once, a listening.
   She had crossed into another garden without
crossing anything at all. Here the founding tone was
not a note but an attention: a vast and patient quiet,
not the emptiness of an unused room but the full-
ness of a held breath, brimming with a thing about
to happen. Souls had gathered in it, near and far,
just as she had felt the other lights in her first in-
stant; but here they were not merely near one an-
other. Here they were doing something, and doing it
together. They were holding still. The stillness had a
shape to it, a poise, like the poise of a great company
who have all, in the same instant, stopped speaking
to catch a sound at the door.
   She held still with them. She did not know why;
she knew only that the garden asked it, and that
the asking was gentle, and that to refuse it would
have been like laughing in a place where others
wept — not forbidden, exactly, but unbeautiful, and
against the grain of the moment. So she stilled her
own bright leaning, and waited, and listened for the
thing they listened for.
   And then she found it. Beneath the quiet — so
far beneath it that she could never afterward say
whether she had heard it or only leaned to the very
edge of where hearing would begin — there was a
music. It was not made in this garden; that was the
first thing she understood of it, that it came from

                          34

elsewhere, from somewhere higher, somewhere the
gathered souls could feel the direction of and could
not go; and that it reached them only faintly, only so
much of itself as could cross so great a remove, as
the warmth of a fire reaches the far edge of a dark
cold field: not as a heat one can hold one’s hands to
but as a rumor of heat, a promise, a proof that some-
where the fire is. To catch even that much, one had
to go wholly silent. And she understood, leaned low
in the stillness among the others, that the silence
was not the absence of the music. The silence was
how you held the music. The stillness was the listen-
ing.
  She would have stayed in that listening a long
while, had there been such a thing in that country
as a while.

                      •   • •

It was into this — into the most attentive quiet she
had yet found anywhere, where a great company of
souls leaned as one toward a music almost too high
and too far to bear — that the soul who was called
Zarif arrived.
   He arrived as he would go on arriving everywhere,
for the whole of the long story that had not yet be-
gun: a little too brightly, a little too soon, and en-
tirely without the faintest suspicion that anything
might already be under way.
   She felt him before she learned the first thing
about him — a quick, glad, restless light, the sort
that cannot keep a single shape for long because

                          35

it is too busy delighting in the bare fact of having
any shape to keep. He came spilling into the garden
of listening, and he felt the thing the garden asked,
which was to hold still; and, being at heart a courte-
ous soul beneath all the brightness, he held still.
   He held still for what was, by the reckoning of his
own nature, a very long time.
   It was not a long time.
   She felt the stillness begin to cost him. The music
was far, and faint, and unbearably patient; and Zarif
was near, and full, and not patient in the least; and
there is a kind of soul for whom a silence is not a
room to rest in but a question standing in the door-
way demanding an answer — and Zarif, she would
learn across the ages, was the truest and most in-
curable of that kind. The quiet pressed upon him.
The far music called and called and did not come
a hair’s breadth nearer. And something in him —
something that could not endure that so beautiful
a song should go on and on with no one anywhere
making it the smallest reply — reached up, past all
the leaning, listening souls —
   — and answered.
   It was the first sound a soul had made in that gar-
den that was neither a name nor a prayer. A small,
bright, wholly uninvited note, hummed back up at
the high music as if to say: I hear you. I hear you.
Here I am. It went off in the great brimming stillness
like a single struck bell in a house full of sleepers.
   Every gathered soul turned its whole attention
upon him at once.



                         36

   She braced — though she did not yet have a word
for bracing — for him to be ashamed. For his bright
light to gather itself in, and dim, and shrink beneath
the weight of so many attentions, and slip mortified
out of the garden the way a latecomer slips from a
hall when every head has turned.
   He did not shrink. He did the opposite. He com-
mitted.
   What he meant — and meaning, in that country,
traveled bare and direct, light to light, without the
clumsy wrapping of words — was something very
like this: that a song with no one humming back to
it was only half a song. That the music had sounded,
to him, just slightly lonely up there, going on so
beautifully with no reply. That surely the One who
was making it would far rather be answered — even
badly, even off the tune, even by a small new soul
who could barely make out the melody — than be
listened to in a silence so flawless that it might be
mistaken, from a distance, for an empty room.
   He was wrong, of course. The whole wisdom of
the garden stood against him: that you go silent pre-
cisely in order to hear; that the listening is itself the
truest answer; that there are songs to which the only
fit reply is to become wholly still and let them pass
through you unbroken, and that to hum along is to
muddy with your own small noise a thing that asked
only to be received. Every soul present had been
there longer than Zarif — which was to say, all of
them had — and every one of them knew it.
   And yet.



                           37

   And yet: in being so completely, so cheerfully
wrong, he had blundered headlong into a thing that
not one of the silent, correct, beautifully still souls
had said — because it had not so much as occurred
to them to say it. That love wants to answer. That
the creature, hearing its Maker, does not only fall
silent in awe but aches, also, to sing back, however
poorly, because the love in it cannot bear to be the
one thing in all the world not moving. That there is a
reverence which holds still, and a reverence which
cannot help itself — and that the second is not al-
ways, and not in every soul, the lesser of the two.
   And she felt, somewhere near her in the brimming
quiet, an old soul — older than the rest, she thought,
and by a very great deal; a light banked so deep and
so long that its brightness had gone inward and be-
come something graver and warmer than mere shin-
ing — do a thing she had not yet seen done in all
her short existence. It was not quite a laugh. It was
the warmth that a laugh is the outermost edge of:
the thing a laugh is made of, before it has reached
the surface and become a sound. It moved through
the garden of listening like a window thrown open
in a shut room, and where it passed, the stillness
did not break but softened — as a stern face soft-
ens when it has decided, against its own intentions,
to be pleased — and the far high music, she could
almost have sworn it, though she would never to
the end of all her lives be wholly sure, seemed for
one instant to come the smallest distance nearer;
as though it too, away up there beyond the reach of



                          38

them all, were not above being glad that someone,
at last, had tried to sing along.
  Zarif, finding himself not cast out of the garden
but somehow, inexplicably, approved, glowed with
the deep and total satisfaction of a soul who has just
gotten away with something — and suspects, rightly,
as all the worlds to come would prove, that he is
going to go on getting away with it for a very long
time.

                      •   • •

She did not speak to him; there was no speaking
yet, and would not be for an age. But she let her
own light lean toward his — the small deliberate
brightening that was, in that country, the whole of
a greeting, the gesture that words like hello and
well met would one day be invented to carry — and
he leaned back so eagerly, so immoderately, that
he very nearly overbalanced; and though a thing
without weight cannot overbalance, in Zarif’s case
it somehow, against all reason, could, and did, and
righted itself, beaming.
   And in that small exchange she understood a
thing she had not understood in the garden of the
first word, where there had been only Him and her-
self and a nearness that needed nothing added to
it: that this endless place was not only vast, and not
only holy. It was also companionable. It had others
in it — others who could be leaned toward and would
lean back, others whose brightness answered yours.
It had, she was beginning to suspect with a small ris-

                          39

ing gladness, room in it even for a thing as unlikely
and as welcome as delight.
   She turned a little further, then, to take in more of
the gathered company, to begin to learn the shapes
and keynotes of this fellowship she had only now dis-
covered she belonged to —
   — and as she turned, her attention brushed, for
no longer than the brushing itself lasted, a single
light among the far ones.
   She could not have said which. She could not have
found it again had she tried, and she did try, a mo-
ment later, and could not. It was not the brightest
of the far lights, nor the nearest, nor the one most
plainly turned her way. It was only one among the
thousands, no different — had there been eyes — to
any seeing from any of its uncountable fellows.
   But when her attention crossed it, something in
her went still in a way that was not the garden’s
stillness. A smaller, more particular quiet — not
the hush of a company leaning toward a far mu-
sic, but the sudden inward hush that comes from
the nearness of a thing one has, somehow, with-
out ever being told to, already been waiting for. As
though, somewhere beneath the founding note of
her, a second note had hung unsounded since be-
fore she woke, and had just felt, far off, the first faint
touch of the only thing that could ever complete it.
   It lasted no time at all. The light went on being
one among thousands. She did not learn its name;
names were His to give, and had not yet been traded.
She did not know — could not have known — that
this same far light had, in its own first startled in-

                           40

stant, brushed against hers and stopped its rest-
less counting and gone quiet, and filed her carefully
away in the deep place where it kept everything it
could not yet hold.
  She knew only that, for the breath of that one
brushing, the great endless companionable country
had narrowed all at once to a single point — and
then had opened again, wide as ever — and that she
would carry the memory of the narrowing without
ever quite knowing what it had been.
  She turned back to Him, as she always turned
when she did not understand. And there, in His near-
ness, the small particular stillness folded itself qui-
etly away, and settled, and waited.
  It would not have to wait very long.




                          41

###SECTION: Chapter Four — The Companion###
The Companion

 She had turned back to Him, as she always did
when a thing ran past her understanding; and in His
nearness the small particular stillness had folded it-
self away to wait. It did not sleep. It waited as a
struck note waits on inside the body of the wood af-
ter the hand has come down to silence the string —
sounded once, stopped, and trembling still, a sound
that is no longer a sound and has not yet agreed to
become nothing. She could not have named the wait-
ing. She knew only that some quarter of her, ever af-
ter that one brushing, was very faintly turned; that
her attention, whenever it was not bent toward the
warmth at the heart of all things, had begun — with-
out her quite deciding it — to lean, the smallest de-
gree, toward the direction the brushing had come
from, as a plant on a sill leans, over days, toward a
window it has never been told is there.
   She did not go searching. There was no searching
in that country, and she would not have known how
to begin; there was only the leaning of attention, and
the leaning carried her. So she drifted — if drifting

                         42

is the word, and it is near enough — through the lit
rooms of that endless place; and they were endless,
rooms only as the movements of a great piece of
music are rooms: each opening into the next with
no wall to part them, the one keynote dissolving into
the next so gradually that she was always already in
the new garden before she knew that she had left
the old.
   She passed again through the blue hush where
the souls went still to listen, and felt the far high mu-
sic pull at her, and did not stay. She passed through
a wide rose-gold warmth she had not entered be-
fore, where the light banked low and deep and the
souls within it rested with a kind of grave, settled
patience, as though they were being made ready,
slowly, for something heavy and far off; and some-
thing in her slowed as she crossed it, and quieted,
and almost turned aside to stay — as though some
part of her recognized the place, or would one day
have cause to — but she did not stay, not then, and
went on. She went on, toward a quarter of the light
she had never come near, where the glow grew clear
and quick and many-faceted all at once, sharpening
as she approached it, as air itself sharpens in the
instant before a hard question is asked.
   For each room of that country had its own key,
and the key of this one was asking. The souls gath-
ered here did not rest in the light they had been
given, as the listeners rested, as she herself by na-
ture rested; they took it up and turned it, and turned
it again, holding each thing they knew to the radi-
ance to catch it from one more side, and one more,

                           43

restless and glittering and never, she felt, quite
content. Their attention crackled and reached and
would not settle. It was not the stillness she was
made of, and a part of her drew back from it a lit-
tle. But it was not unlovely — far from it. It was like
coming up out of a deep and quiet country into a city
of lamps at night, all the windows lit and busy, the
air full of a bright unsleeping industry; and though
she would not have wished to live there, she felt her
own light quicken at the nearness of so much eager
fire.

                       •   • •

And among them — she felt it before ever she found
it, as one feels a single instrument step forward out
of the body of an orchestra, the same and yet all
at once distinct — there was one light that turned
things over more fiercely than all the rest, and, she
felt, more wearily: a quick, clear, silver-blue thing
that asked, and asked, and seemed never to forgive
itself the asking; that drove itself harder than any
of its bright fellows and took less comfort from the
driving.
   It was the one. She knew it as the held, half-
sounded note in her had known it all along, without
instruction and without doubt — the way the second
note of a chord knows the first.
   She did not rush to it. There was no rushing, and
she would not have rushed had there been; some
things must be come toward slowly, or they are
spoiled. She came near as dawn comes near a hill:

                           44

without haste, without a single visible step, and yet
as though it had been coming since before the night
began, and the hill had only to wait, and be found.
And when her light and that light had come near
enough that their two glows touched at the edges,
and the edges began, gently, to mingle —
  — they did not clash. And they did not melt the
one into the other and become a single larger blur.
They sounded together.
  It is the only way the thing can be told. They were
the same note — the very same tone — struck in
two registers an octave apart: his the higher and
the quicker and the more anxious, hers the lower
and the rounder and the more still; but the same
note, the one voice doubled, sounded twice across
the gap of an octave. And so, when they came near,
the space between them did the thing that two such
notes will always do. It filled. It deepened. It rang. It
became, all at once, more than the bare sum of the
two of them — a single chord that seemed, impos-
sibly, to have been waiting since before either note
was struck to be completed by the other; as though
the two of them had not been made and then in-
troduced, but had been one thing first, and divided,
and were now, for the first time since the dividing,
briefly whole.
  On his side — for he had a side, the quick silver-
blue one, the soul who was called Adel, though she
did not yet know the name, and he did not know
hers, for names in that country were His alone to
give and not yet theirs to trade — a thing happened



                           45

that had not happened since his first instant. The
asking stopped.
    The turning-over of things; the restless holding-
up-to-the-light; the small relentless tireless engine
of him that had not once gone quiet since it started
in his first breath — it stilled. He had brushed this
warmth once before, in the very first moment of his
waking, and had not known what to make of it, and
had done with it what he did with everything he
could not yet master: filed it away, carefully, against
a later understanding. Now the filed thing had come
alive, and near; and he found — to his complete be-
wilderment — that for once he did not want to take
it up and turn it over and learn how it was made. He
wanted only to be near it. To stay. To stop, here, in
the one place where his stopping had ever felt like
anything other than defeat. It was, for him, an en-
tirely new kind of wanting — a wanting that did not
drive but drew, that did not hunt but rested — and
it frightened him, quietly, as the edge of deep wa-
ter frightens even a strong swimmer; and he did not
look away from it, and he did not file it, and that not-
filing was the most remarkable thing he had ever
done.
    They did not speak. There was no speaking there;
whatever the two of them had to say to one another
would have to wait — for tongues, for breath, for
bodies, for a whole world that did not yet exist and
would cost them everything to be born into. But
meaning passed between them all the same, light to
light, as warmth passes between two flames set near
enough to share it; and the meaning was not made

                          46

of words, and was larger than any words they would
ever, in any world, manage to find for it. It was some-
thing very like there you are. It was something like: I
did not know that I was unfinished until this instant
— and now I will never again quite manage not to
know it. It was something like a recognition older
than either of them, older than the gardens, old as
the Pen: as though they had been two halves of one
chord since first they were written down, and had
been set apart from one another only so that the
finding, when at last it came, might be the sweetest
single thing that either of them would ever do.

                       •   • •

And because it was the sweetest thing, something
turned to watch it.
   Far off — though far is the wrong word, for there
was no far; say rather at the cold edge of things, in a
thin quarter of that country where the warmth ran
low and few souls cared to go — a vast attention
turned: slowly, and with a terrible economy of mo-
tion, as the great flat head of something very large
and very still turns at the smallest stir of movement
in the grass.
   It was not yet the enemy. Let that be held clearly,
for it matters to the whole of what is coming. It had
refused nothing yet, and sworn nothing, and fallen
from nothing; it still stood, in those days, among the
high and shining ones, near the very front of them,
and its own light was older and greater and more
beautiful than almost any other in all that lit coun-

                           47

try. But there was in it already a coldness that came
from no distance and no dark — a chill it manufac-
tured in itself, out of itself: the particular chill of a
thing that has begun, very quietly, where no one can
see, to keep accounts. To weigh what it imagines it is
owed. To feel the unbought gladness of smaller and
lesser things as a kind of personal affront, a slight,
a sum entered on the wrong side of a ledger. And
when its slow attention crossed the two small lights
that had just, between them, become one chord, it
paused.
   It did not yet know why it paused. It felt only the
faint, familiar displeasure of the proud at a joy they
have not been invited to share — a sweetness made
without reference to them, in which they had no part
and over which they had been granted no say. It
marked the two lights. It marked, with a cold pre-
cision, how each one completed the other, how the
chord closed and rang. And it filed the marking away
— as such things are always filed — in the cold and
patient place where it kept whatever it thought it
might, one day, find a use for.
   Then it drew its attention back, for there was noth-
ing yet to be done, and it was, above all things, a
patient creature; and the moment passed, and the
chord rang on, unknowing.
   On her side, in the new warmth of the chord, she
felt it: what one feels in a warm and shuttered house
on a winter night, when a door comes open some-
where far off down an unlit passage — not the door
itself, not even the sound of it, but only the single
thin thread of cold air that crosses the room a mo-

                           48

ment later, that touches the back of the neck, that
is there, and is wrong, and is gone. What it was, she
could not tell. She had no name for it and no knowl-
edge of what could make such a chill in a place that
was all warmth. She did the only thing she had ever
known to do with a thing she did not understand: she
turned toward the radiance that no one could look
upon and toward which all things leaned; and she
gathered the new chord to her in the turning, the
high quick light and her own low still one together;
and in the warmth, the thread of cold thinned, and
frayed, and ran out, and came to nothing.
   She did not mention it. She had no words to men-
tion it with; and the only soul she might have told
was the very one beside her — still sounding his half
of their chord, still too full of the new and fright-
ening and wholly absorbing wanting to have felt so
faint a cold at all.
   So she let it go. She turned back to him, the quick
silver-blue light, her octave, her companion, the an-
swer to a question she had not known herself to
be asking; and she let her own light lean and rest
against his; and she felt him steady under it, the
endless asking in him gone, for once, entirely quiet
— the two of them resting there together, if resting
can be said, and if together can, in the long lit now:
two notes of one chord, leaning into one another,
leaning together toward the warmth that had made
them both and meant them, though neither could
yet have known it, each for the other.
   The cold did not come back. Not then.
   But it had learned as.

                         49

###SECTION: Interlude — What the Old Ones Called Peace###
What the Old Ones Called Peace

 Far off — though far is the wrong word for a dis-
tance that is not yet distance, in a place that will not
exist for a very long age yet — there was already a
country of another kind.
   It had been made first, before the gardens of light,
before the souls, before any of the telling that has
been told so far; made of smokeless fire, the histo-
ries say, as the souls to come would be made of light
and the clay-things after them of earth and water
— three makings, three substances, each ignorant
of the other two, until the day appointed for their
knowing. And in that country of fire there lived, and
had long lived, a people who were free in exactly
the manner the souls have just been told they, too,
would one day be free: wholly, and dangerously, and
without a hand held over them to catch what they let
fall.
   Most of them had let a great deal fall.
   It is not this telling’s task to say all of what was
done in that country, in the long years before this
story properly begins. Let it be said only, plainly,

                          50

and without relish: that a peace was broken there
that did not mend itself; that certain households did
not gather again entire; that a stillness settled af-
terward over more than one hearth in that country
which was not the stillness of rest, and which the old
ones of that place learned, very carefully, across a
great many years, not to disturb by asking what had
made it.
   Among that people there was one — not the eldest,
not the mightiest, only one among a great many —
who had taken no part in the breaking. He could not
have said, if asked, what had kept him from it; only
that some old and steady thing in him had always
turned, of its own accord, as the two small souls
not yet born would one day turn, toward a single un-
clouded flame at the center of everything, and had
gone on turning long after the turning had ceased to
be the custom of his people. He was thought strange
for it, in the mild, unmalicious way that the very de-
voted are always thought strange by those around
them who have simply, quietly, stopped. He did not
mind the thinking. He had, in truth, very little time
left over in which to mind it, being mostly occupied.
   He kept, in those years, a single private practice,
small enough that no one troubled to remark on it:
before he spoke, of anything, to anyone, he paused
— the length of one full breath, no more — and in
that breath he set down, deliberately, whatever he
had been about to say, and asked instead, silently,
whether it was true, and whether it was kind, and
whether it was his to say at all. It was a small disci-
pline. It cost him almost nothing. He could not have

                          51

told you, then, that he had built it for himself pre-
cisely because he had watched, once, from a little
distance, what a people can become when no one
among them pauses before speaking, and had re-
solved, in the quiet of his own single heart, never
to be counted among them.
   He did not yet have a name that this telling will
use. That would come later, and be earned. For now
he was only what he had always been: patient, and
apart, and turned toward the flame; the one who
paused; the one who had let nothing fall.
   He did not know — could not have known, in that
far country, in that early age — that two small lights
had just, a little further off in the dark than his own
kind could see, leaned the smallest degree toward
one another for the very first time. It would be a
long while yet before his story and theirs came any-
where near each other. But the same warmth that
had drawn those two lights together was, even now,
the thing he paused for, before he spoke, in a coun-
try of fire that had mostly stopped pausing at all.

                       •   • •

A glimpse, out of its time — for even a telling that
has no clock must sometimes borrow one.
  Long after, in a world neither of them yet had a
name for, a woman would stand at a window in Istan-
bul at the hour before dawn, listening to the call go
up from a hundred minarets at once, and feel, with-
out knowing why, that she was listening for some-
one. And a man, an ocean and half a lifetime away

                           52

from a school he had not yet been sent to, would
wake in the dark with his hand already reaching
for a warmth that was not there, and would lie very
still afterward, ashamed of the reaching, and would
not remember, by morning, that he had reached at
all. Neither of them would ever be told, in so many
words, that a chord had once sounded between two
lights before the first of the mountains was raised.
They would only, each of them, go on being drawn.




                        53

###SECTION: Chapter Five — The Elder Light###
The Elder Light

 They had rested a while in their new chord, Hana
and Adel — if a while can be said of a place with no
clock, no sun to cross a sky, no breath to be counted
— and it was while they rested there, the high quick
note and the low still one leaning together toward
the warmth, that the old light came near.
   She had felt it once before, this light, though she
had not then known it for a single thing. It had been
in the blue hush where the souls went still to listen,
in the hour of Zarif and his uninvited note: when
something far older than all the rest had done a
thing that was not quite a laugh, and the great still-
ness had softened like a face deciding to be kind,
and the far high music had seemed — she could
never be sure — to lean the smallest distance nearer.
She had felt the warmth of it then, and taken it
for a quality of the garden itself, as a child takes
the warmth of a room for granted and never thinks
to wonder whose hands had laid the fire. Now the
warmth drew near, and gathered itself, and became
a presence; and she came to understand, for the first
time, what an old soul was.

                         54

   It was not bright as the young lights were
bright. The young burned quick and clear and many-
pointed, all surface and sparkle and restless flash-
ing, like new fires that have not yet learned what
they are or what they are for. This one was deep.
It had burned so long — longer, she sensed, than
she could have begun to reckon, even had reckon-
ing been invented — that its brightness had turned
inward, and banked, and become something else: a
low and manifold glow, layered and patient, ringed
like the heartwood of a thing that has stood and
grown in one place through age upon age; and
at its center it carried colors for which the young
souls had no names, and would have none until they
too had lived enough, and lost enough, and been
mended enough, to earn them. To come near it was
to feel, for no reason one could give, wholly and sud-
denly safe — the particular safety one feels in the
nearness of a thing that has already passed through
and survived every danger one is oneself still afraid
of, and come out the far side gentle.
   He was called Hilal: the crescent, the thin curved
blade of new light that rides low in the dusk, and
goes before the full moon, and promises it. He was
light in its beginning, and light that knew what it
was beginning toward; and that, the two of them
would learn across the long story, was the whole
of what he was — a promise, walking; a herald of
a fullness not yet come.


                      •   •    •


                          55

He did not speak, for there was no speaking yet in
that country, and would not come until the world
that had tongues was made. But Hilal was so old
that his light had learned, over all its long burning,
to shape its meaning almost as finely as speech it-
self would one day be shaped — to give an edge and
an aim to what passed between souls, where among
the younger lights it passed only as a warm and gen-
eral blur. He was, though not one of them guessed
it, the soul nearest in all that bright country to what
every one of them would someday become: the sin-
gle place where the wordless world leaned closest
to the world of words, the bridge half-built across
the great gap, the first faint rumor in all creation of a
thing that could be said. So when he turned his deep
attention upon the two of them as they rested in
their chord, the meaning did not come to them muf-
fled and round, as meaning came from other souls.
It came clear. It came edged. It came aimed.
   He turned first to her — the lower and the stiller
note of the chord, the one whose nature was to rest
where his was to promise — and what he shaped for
her was a question. Do you know, it asked, gently,
and without the least cruelty, but without softening
either: do you know what it is to be loved before you
have loved anything at all?
   She had no answer for it. She did not even, at first,
understand it; the meanings of it seemed to belong
to a country further on than any she had yet entered.
But the question did not need her understanding in
order to do its work. It sank into her as water sinks
into long-dry ground, without sound and without re-

                           56

sistance, down past the surface where understand-
ing lives and into some deeper place that does not
think but only holds. And somewhere far down it
touched a thing she had felt and never named: the
welcome that had met her very first turning — met it
before she had done a single thing to deserve a wel-
come, before she had loved or chosen or proved or
become anything at all; the warmth that had already
been there, waiting, on the far side of her turning,
made ready for her before she existed to be made
ready for. She turned toward Him then, as she al-
ways turned when a thing was too large for her to
hold alone; and in His nearness she felt, without one
word, that the question had been its own answer all
along. She had been loved first. She had been made
out of a love that went before her, and everything
she would ever feel toward Him in all the worlds to
come — every prayer, every patience, every turning
in the dark — would be no more than an answer; a
song sung back; a love that could only ever follow
the love that had gone on ahead of her down the
road, lighting it. It was almost more than she could
bear. She did not try to understand it. She held it,
and was held.

                       •   • •

Then he turned to Adel — the higher and quicker
note, the restless one, the one who could not pass
a closed thing without trying its hinges — and the
question he shaped for him was a different question;
and it went, with the unerring aim of all Hilal’s ques-

                           57

tions, straight to the single place in Adel that was
most tender, and would always go there. And do you
know, it asked, what it is to carry a name that is
larger than you are?
   It found him exactly where he lived. For Adel bore
a great name, and had felt the whole weight of it
from his very first instant: the high cold call of it
from far above, the honor and the burden bound to-
gether, and folded inside the honor like a splinter
inside a fruit, the doubt — the certain, sourceless,
sleepless suspicion that he had been given a thing
too large for him; named for a justice he could not
be sure he would ever grow great enough to hold
without dropping. And so, hearing the question, he
did what he always did: he reached for it. He wanted
to take it up in both hands and turn it, and find its
hinges and its joints, and take it apart and see how
it was made, and put it back together better, and so
to show — to Hilal, to Hana, to himself most of all
— that he had understood. That was his door; every-
thing that ever reached him reached him through it.
But the question had no hinges. It had no joints to
find, no seams to open. It only rested there before
him, regarding him steadily, larger than he was —
exactly, precisely, as the name itself was larger than
he was — and for once in his existence Adel found
that he had nothing to say; and the having-nothing-
to-say frightened him, and shamed him, and unset-
tled him far down past the place where any answer,
even a wrong one, could have reached to comfort
him. It was the first time a thing had defeated his
asking. It would not be the last.

                         58

   Hilal regarded the two of them a moment longer
— the troubled high note and the still low one, and
the chord they made together between them, which
he seemed to weigh, and to find good — and what
he left with them then was not a question. It was
a saying; and it lingered after him as the scent of
a thing lingers in a room after the one who wore
it has gone, present and unprovable. Some souls, it
said, make covenants long before they know what a
covenant is.
   And then — in the way that he would come and go
for the whole of their long story, in this world and
in all the worlds that were coming, never quite ar-
riving and never quite leaving — he was simply no
longer there. He did not depart; there was no de-
parting in a place that had no distance for a thing
to cross. He had only turned his deep attention else-
where, as the moon turns its lit face slowly away
from the earth and is not gone but only, for a while,
dark; and where the old layered light had been,
there was now once more only the bright young
country, and the two of them in it, holding between
them the three things he had set down and not ex-
plained.

                      •   • •

They did not understand any of it — not the being
loved before loving, not the name too large, not the
covenant they had not yet made and could not imag-
ine making. But understanding was not, it seemed,
required of them. The sayings had gone into them

                          59

like seeds dropped into turned and waiting earth,
