and a seed does not need to comprehend the sum-
mer in order to grow toward it. Hana took the first
of them — loved before loving — and turned it, as
she turned all things, toward Him, until it stopped
being a question and became a kind of prayer, and
then stopped being even a prayer and became sim-
ply a part of how she stood in the world. And Adel
took the second — the name too large — and turned
it over, and over, and over, in the restless engine of
him, and could not once make it lie flat. And some-
where far ahead of them both, though neither could
have seen so far, a day was coming — a great day,
the greatest of all the days that ever were or would
be — on which they would learn at last, and not from
any telling but by the doing of it, what a covenant
was: by making one, between the two of them and
Him, in words that were not yet words; and would
spend all the long and broken road that came after
trying, against everything, to keep it.




                         60

###SECTION: Chapter Six — Sami###
Sami

 The name would not lie flat.
   Adel carried Hilal’s question away with him — do
you know what it is to carry a name that is larger
than you are? — and it would not lie flat, and it would
not be solved, and it would not be set down; it only
sat in him and chafed, as a small hard thing sits and
chafes in a shoe on a long road, too slight to stop for
and too constant to ignore. He did the only thing he
had ever known to do with an unease he could not
name: he went looking for something to turn over,
some other thing to take apart, in the hope that the
noise of solving it might drown out the question he
could not solve. He drifted back into the bright quick
quarter where the asking souls burned — the city of
lamps, all eager fire and crackling attention — hop-
ing that the company of a thousand other questions
might quiet his own.
   It did not quiet his own. But it gave him, instead,
a thing he had not known until that moment that he
had been missing the whole of his short existence:
a friend.


                         61

                        •   • •


The soul was called Sami, and his was the most
beautiful light that Adel had yet come near among
the young ones. Not beautiful as the great veiled
lights were said to be beautiful — that was a beauty
of another order, and would have struck him down
rather than drawn him in. Sami’s was a nearer and
a warmer beauty: the beauty of a flame at the in-
stant it leaps highest in a fire, the one tongue of light
among all the others that the eye goes to without be-
ing asked, that seems for a moment to be what the
whole fire is for. He was bright as Adel was bright,
and quick as Adel was quick, and a questioner as
Adel was a questioner; in all these things they were
so alike that to come near him was, for Adel, like
catching sight of himself in still water for the first
time. But there was one great difference between
them, and it was the difference that would in the end
decide everything, and neither of them could see
it then. Adel’s asking turned inward, and doubted
itself, and gnawed; it was forever asking whether
Adel was enough. Sami’s asking turned upward, and
reached, and never doubted at all; it asked only how
high, and how soon, and took it wholly for granted
that whatever it reached for would prove worth the
having, and would, in the fullness of things, be his.
   They knew each other at once — as two of a sin-
gle kind know one another across a crowded room
of strangers, by a sign that needs no explaining be-
cause it is older than explaining. And near Sami,
Adel felt a thing he had not felt since his waking:

                            62

his own endless, grinding doubts went quiet. Not
answered — Sami answered nothing, and was not
asked to — but outshone, as a candle’s small flame
is not put out by the sun but simply ceases, for a
while, to matter. Sami’s certainty was a warm thing
to stand beside, a fire on a cold and questioning
night; and Adel, who was so often left shivering by
the cold of his own relentless asking, leaned toward
that warmth, and was grateful for it, and did not look
too closely at what it was he warmed himself upon.
   They played, in the way that lights could play in
that country — which is to say that they chased
one another’s meanings through the bright quar-
ters, and finished one another’s wonderings before
they were half shaped, and dared each other out to-
ward the dangerous edges of the lit country to see
how near the high veiled brightness they could lean
before the sheer force of it turned them, laughing,
back. Adel laughed more in those hours than he had
in the whole of his short existence before. For the
first time the great name on him — the name too
large, the name that would not lie flat — felt a lit-
tle lighter; for here, at last, was someone to carry
the weight of the questions alongside him, someone
bright and unafraid who made the carrying feel less
like a burden and more like a game played by two.
He thought — and he did think it, in just these terms,
and would remember, far off and too late, having
thought it — that he could not imagine the whole
long story to come without Sami somewhere near
the heart of it. He was right. He was only, and en-
tirely, wrong about how.

                         63

                      •   • •


It came in the middle of their play, lightly, almost
carelessly, as the most dangerous things so often
do — not announced, not professed gravely as a
creed, but tossed off, in passing, as a joke. Sami
had reached, as he so loved to reach, out toward the
high veiled quarter where the great lights were; and
he had been turned gently back by the brightness,
as they always were, as every young soul always
was. But where another might have been humbled
by the turning-back, Sami only laughed — a bright,
unbothered laugh — and what he shaped for Adel,
still laughing, leaning close, was this:
   Do you ever think — you and I — that we were
meant first? That when He began the whole of it,
it was the two of us He had in His mind; and that
all the rest of them, all these thousands, came after,
and only to fill the room?
   He did not mean it. Or — and this is the truer and
the worse way to say it — he half meant it, as a soul
half means a boast let slip among friends, where the
laugh is only a coat thrown hastily over a thing that
would be shameful to be caught saying plainly, and
the laugh and the coat are there precisely so that,
if the thing is ever challenged, one can always say
I was only joking, and so never have to look at it
straight.
   Adel laughed too. Of course he laughed; it was
funny, or near enough; and it was the kind of bright
foolish thing one says and laughs at with a friend
in the first warm flush of a friendship, when every-

                          64

thing the other does seems fine and clever and dear.
But somewhere underneath the laugh, in the deep
place where his nature did its quiet ceaseless work,
a small thing turned over that he could not name and
did not like: a faint wrongness, a single note very
slightly out of true in an otherwise faultless chord
— the kind of thing the ear catches a half-instant
before the mind does, and cannot quite say what it
caught. And here — here was the moment, though it
looked like nothing, though it was the smallest mo-
ment imaginable — Adel did the one thing that was
utterly against the whole grain of his nature. He did
not examine it. He, who took everything apart, who
could not let a closed thing stay closed, who turned
every passing notion over three times to study its
undersides — he let this one pass, unexamined, un-
weighed. The friendship was too new, and too warm,
and he found that he did not want to take it apart
and risk finding, down in its workings, some small
flaw that he would afterward be unable to stop see-
ing. So he let it lie. And the letting-it-lie was nothing
at all — the easiest, the most natural, the most for-
givable thing in all the world. That is always, always,
how it begins.

                        •   • •

And at the cold edge of the bright country, where
the warmth ran thin and few souls cared to go, the
vast patient attention that had once turned to mark
two lights becoming a chord turned again. But it did
not turn, this time, drawn by sweetness, which it

                            65

neither understood nor wanted. It turned drawn by
something it knew far more intimately than it had
ever known sweetness — something it knew, in fact,
as one knows the sound of one’s own breathing.
   It had heard the joke. And in the small bright
boast of the soul called Sami it had felt, unmistak-
able, the faint familiar shape of a thing that it had
itself begun, very lately and very quietly, to feel —
and had not yet dared to speak aloud, even alone,
even in the locked cold privacy of its own keeping.
I was meant first. I am more than these others. The
room is full of them, yes, but I — it had never fin-
ished the thought, even to itself. It did not need to
now. Here was the thought, finished, and laughing,
in the mouth of a small bright soul who did not even
know that he had spoken it.
   It recognized the shape of it as one recognizes
one’s own voice, heard suddenly and unexpectedly
from another room — with a start, and then with a
cold and dawning interest.
   And where, at the chord of Hana and Adel, the
cold had felt only the old affront, the displeasure of
the proud at a gladness they have no share in, here it
felt something altogether better. Something useful.
For here, in a bright and gifted and beautiful light,
was a crack — a flaw, a fault running down into the
grain — shaped exactly, precisely, lovingly like its
own. A door, in another soul, standing the smallest
possible distance ajar.
   It did not go in. It was far, far too patient to go in.
A door that has once begun to open will open all the
rest of as by itself, in time, if only one is willing to

                           66

wait — and waiting was the single thing the cold had
been granted ages enough to do. So it did not even
breathe upon the door. It only marked it: marked the
crack, and the bright light the crack ran through,
and the name of the one it belonged to. Sami. The
elevated one. The one who already wished, just a
very little, just enough, to be lifted up above the rest.
Then it drew its attention back to the cold edge of
things, and folded itself down, and bided.

                        •   • •

Adel knew none of this. He had a friend now — the
first friend of his whole existence, bright and cer-
tain and beautiful — and the great name on him felt
lighter for the sharing of its weight, and the long lit
now stretched away ahead of them full of play, and
questions, and the simple unlooked-for warmth of
company. He was happy. And it matters — it mat-
ters more than almost anything else in this telling
— to say plainly that he was happy, and that the
happiness was real and good and no illusion; and
that Sami’s love for him was real too, then and for
a long, long time after. Because what is coming will
tempt you, one day, when you have seen where it all
goes, to look back and decide that it had been false
from the very first — that the friendship was always
a trap, the warmth always a lie, the laughter always
a mask drawn over the crack. It was not. That is the
worst of it, and the truest of it. The friendship was
real. The crack was also real. Both of them were en-
tirely real, at the very same time, in the very same

                            67

soul — and the whole long story that was coming
would be, more than it was anything else, the slow
and terrible working-out of which of the two would,
in the end, win.
   But all of that was still ahead, still mercifully
unimaginable. For now there were only two bright
lights in a bright country, glad of one another, rac-
ing each other out toward the shining edges of the
light and being turned, laughing, back. The cold
bided at the rim of things. And far off, the old deep
light that was called Hilal turned his attention to-
ward the two friends for a moment, and saw the
crack, and saw the door that it made, and saw the
cold and patient One that the door stood ajar to-
ward — and did a thing that Hilal almost never did,
and that cost him more than the young could have
known. He said nothing at all. For some seeds even
an elder cannot tear from the ground before their
season without tearing up, along with them, the
good wheat that has rooted alongside; and a warn-
ing given too soon, to a soul not ready to hear it,
becomes only a map to the very thing it warned
against. He would watch. He would be near, later,
when it came to matter, if there were any nearness
then that Sami would still allow. For now he only
grieved — in the deep and quiet way of the very old,
who have seen this happen before, and will see it
again — over a brightness that did not yet know that
it stood in any danger at all; and he turned his face
away, and let the two friends play.
   There is no soul but that it has a guardian over it.



                          68

                     •     • •


The Qur’an · Sūrah aṭ-Ṭāriq (86:4)



                         ACT II
                      • • •




                     • • •


                 The Watchers




                          69

###SECTION: Chapter Seven — The Angels Pass###
The Angels Pass

 Until then they had seen only one another — souls,
and the lights of souls, and beyond all of it the
warmth toward which every last thing in the country
leaned. It had been enough to fill them, and they had
not thought to wonder whether there was anything
else. Then the angels passed, and they learned that
there was.
   The angels crossed the lit country the way
weather crosses a sky — purposeful, unhurried, en-
tirely without question. Where a soul’s glow flick-
ered and wondered and doubted, an angel’s burned
level and changeless, like a star that has never once
paused to consider going out. They were upon er-
rands the souls could not have named, only felt to
be grave and vast. The souls watched, hushed. Even
Sami was hushed — for a while.
   Then, because he was Sami, the hush did not hold.
They cannot grow, you know, he said, watching the
great level lights pass. They are precisely what they
were made to be, forever. But we — we can become.
Give us time enough, and I think we will be more
than they are.

                         70

   It was not quite true, and Adel, hearing it, knew at
once that it was not. He could feel, watching the an-
gels burn so sure, that their changelessness was not
a poverty but a different wealth — a perfection the
souls would have to climb a long and broken road
even to glimpse. The correction formed in him whole
and clean, asking only to be spoken. And he did not
speak it. Sami was bright and glad beside him, and
it was easier, just this once, to let the brightness be
right. So Adel held his peace — and that holding was
the first time in all his existence that he let a truth
he could have spoken go by him unspoken. It was a
very small first time. That is exactly how they pass
unnoticed.

                       •   •    •

Zarif, meanwhile, had picked out among the cross-
ing host one young angel flying his errand with
fierce, tongue-between-the-teeth concentration —
and did the thing no soul in the bright country had
ever once thought to do to a member of the angelic
host.
   He waved.
   The young angel, whose name was Nuri, was so
astonished at being waved at mid-errand that he for-
got, for one catastrophic instant, to watch where
he was going, and flew, with tremendous solemnity,
squarely into the side of an angel the size of a moun-
tain range. The mountain range did not appear to no-
tice; it went on about its errand as a mountain goes
on being a mountain when a bird strikes its flank in

                           71

fog. Nuri rebounded, gathered up his dignity, and
proceeded on his way with the composure of one
who had always intended to touch precisely that
spot and had now, thank you, satisfactorily done so.
He did not look back at Zarif a second time. The tips
of his light, however, had gone a furious and unmis-
takable shade of embarrassed.
   Zarif was delighted beyond words. And Hana,
watching, felt the laugh come up from the very bot-
tom of herself — the real kind, that cannot be rea-
soned with, the kind that is half a prayer. For one
bright moment the awe and the absurdity stood side
by side and did not quarrel: the mountain-sized ho-
liness on its grave errand, and the small mortified
angel, and the grinning fool who had caused it all
and regretted nothing. It seemed to her — though
she could not have said why for the better part of
two lifetimes — that a country with room for both
the awe and the small ridiculous tenderness was a
truer country, and a kinder one, and far more like
the One who had made it.

                      •   • •

The host passed on, and the bright country was only
the souls again — though not, any of them, quite the
same souls as before. Sami carried his small bright
certainty a little higher, having said a daring thing
and been allowed to say it. Adel carried a silence
where a truth should have been, and did not yet feel
its weight, as one does not at first feel the weight
of a stone dropped into a deep pocket. Hana carried

                          72

the laugh, and beneath it the awe, and turned both
toward Him. And somewhere out among the host, a
young angel named Nuri carried, all the way to the
end of his errand and well past it, the warm and se-
cretly cherished memory of having been, just once,
waved at by someone — and found, turning it over
with great confusion, that he did not entirely wish it
had never happened.




                         73

###SECTION: Chapter Eight — The Question Adel Should Not Ask###
The Question Adel Should Not Ask

 The silence Adel had kept began, very soon, to itch.
   It was not guilt. He would not have known to call
it guilt, had the word yet existed, and besides it was
far too small a thing for so large a word — a discom-
fort, only; a faint and nagging sense of a thing left
crooked. He had allowed a falsehood to be spoken,
and had let it stand by saying nothing; and he was
a soul made for the setting-right of things, named
for it, built for it down to the grain; and so the un-
set thing lodged in him and would not dissolve, as a
single grain of grit lodges in a shoe — too small to
stop the road for, too constant to be forgotten. He
could not take it back to Sami now; the moment was
long past, and to dig it up again would be to build
a mountain out of a grain, and to lay over nothing
the very chill on the friendship that his silence had
been meant to spare. So he did instead what he had
always done with anything that would not lie flat. He
carried it to the old light.
   But the carrying did not, this once, quite empty
his hands. He had always before been able to set a

                         74

thing down wholly in that light and walk away light-
ened; and he turned to do it now in the old way, the
trusting way, and found — he could not have said
how — that a small remainder stayed behind in him
after the rest was given up, a single grain that would
not pour out with the others, lodged somewhere be-
low where his looking reached. It did not hurt. It
was far too small to hurt. It only sat, the faintest
possible unevenness in a soul that had till now lain
perfectly flat to its Lord, and he noticed it as one no-
tices, walking, that something has got into one\{}’s
shoe — not enough to stop for, not yet, not nearly;
only enough that some part of the mind now keeps
a thread of its attention forever on the sole of one
foot. He did not stop. He told himself it was noth-
ing, and went on down the bright road he was, of
his own will, still gladly walking; and behind him, at
the cold edge of the country, something noted that
he had not stopped, and was content.
   And when, some while later, he went to the old
light again — for he went often; it was the deepest
habit of him — he noticed the grain a second time,
and noticed, with a small cold surprise he did not
let himself examine, that it had not stayed the size
it was. It had not grown loud. It had done something
quieter and worse: it had changed from a discomfort
he carried into a grievance he nursed, so that where
the first time he had brought it to the light to be rid
of it, this time some new part of him brought it to
the light almost to justify it, to lay it down and have
it agreed with rather than dissolved. He did not get
the agreement. The light does not take sides in a

                          75

soul against itself. But he noticed — and this was
the thing he stepped quickly past, as one steps past
a face in a crowd one does not wish to have seen —
that he had gone to the light, this time, already half
hoping it would not ask him to set the thing down;
and that the hoping had been comfortable; and that
comfort, in that particular place, was the first truly
dangerous thing that had ever happened to him.
   He found Hilal in the deep rose quiet where the
patient lights rested, banked and grave; and he did
not ask about Sami, for he could not find as to begin,
and would have had to confess his own small silence
in order to do it. He asked instead the question that
lay beneath the question about Sami — the one that
had truly been forming in him, slow and unnoticed,
since the first time a thread of cold had crossed the
warmth of his joy and he had not known what it was,
or whence it came. Why, he shaped, are there shad-
ows in this world?
   The old light regarded him a long moment before
it answered — a moment in which Adel felt himself
weighed, not unkindly, as one weighs not a thing’s
worth but its readiness. There are no shadows here,
it said at last. There is no sun to cast them; there
is no place that the light does not reach. What you
have felt, and taken for a shadow, is no shadow at
all. It is a depth — a place where the light goes down
further than your seeing is yet able to follow it; and
your seeing, finding its own limit, mistakes the limit
for a darkness. The two are not the same thing. They
are not even close. And a very great deal will de-
pend, for you, child, on your never coming to con-

                         76

fuse the one for the other. And then, more gently,
and lower, the old light gave him a saying — the
saying that Adel would carry all the rest of his days,
and resist, and turn against, and wrestle with, and
come to understand only at the very last, at the end
of the end of all things: Do not mistake a secret for
a debt.
   Adel did not understand it. And — this was
the deeper trouble, the one beneath the not-
understanding — he did not want a thing he could
not understand. He had no use for it; it gave him
nothing he could hold. He wanted an answer with
edges to it, a thing he could take up and turn and
verify and set in its place, the kind of answer that
closes a question and lets the mind move on. He had
brought a real question, an honest and a sharp one,
asked in good faith — and the old light had handed
him back, in its stead, a riddle wrapped around a
kindness; and the riddle would not come apart, and
the kindness he did not know what to do with. Some-
thing in him rose against it, hot and quick and quite
new. Why would the old one not simply say? Why
did the great ones, all of them, speak forever in
these closed and curving sentences, these things
that turned back upon themselves and would not
open — as though plainness were beneath them; as
though a straight answer were a thing too good to
give; as though he, Adel, who wished only to under-
stand, could not be trusted with the bare truth of
the thing itself?
   The old light saw the heat rise in him. It saw it
plainly — it saw most things plainly — and for a mo-

                         77

ment it seemed about to say more: the deep glow
gathered and deepened, as a thing gathers itself to
give a fuller answer, and Adel felt, for one instant,
that a whole and satisfying truth waited just behind
the riddle, near enough to touch, ready to be poured
out into a soul that was ready to receive it. And then
the old light did not pour it. For Adel was not ready;
and a great truth poured into an unready soul does
not nourish it but scalds it, the way water poured too
soon into cold glass cracks the glass; and besides —
and this the old light knew as it knew its own long
age — there are some roads a soul must walk to the
end upon its own feet, alone, or the place it comes
to at the last is not truly its own, and means nothing,
and saves no one. So Hilal let the riddle stand where
it was. He grieved a little, behind the gentleness, for
the heat he could see kindling and could not, with-
out harm, quench. And he said only this: In time.
What is kept from you is not kept to wound you. It
is kept until you have grown strong enough to carry
it without its breaking you. And Adel, of all that the
old light gave him in that hour, heard clearly only
the one thing the heat in him was already certain of,
and bent everything else to fit it: that he was being
made to wait. That a thing was being kept from him.
That somewhere there was an answer he had a right
to, and it was being withheld.

                       •   • •

And at the cold edge of the country, in the thin places
where the warmth ran low, something that had been

                           78

watching and waiting a very long while felt the small
hot feeling kindle and catch in the bright quick soul
it had marked since the hour of his waking — and,
for the first time in all the long ages of its watching,
it smiled.
   It did not stir. It did not speak. It did not need to do
either; that was the beauty of it; that was the whole
of the art it had been perfecting in itself across the
long patience of the cold. The grain was in the shoe
now. The foot would go on walking. The soreness
would grow of its own accord, with no further help
from anyone, a little more with every step the bright
soul took down the road he was already, of his own
free will, choosing to walk. There was nothing yet
that needed doing. It only noted — with a cold satis-
faction it had not felt in a long age, the satisfaction
of a patient craftsman who sees the first true sign
that his patience will be repaid — that the bright
clever light had begun, entirely on his own, with no
whisper from anyone, to resent the very mercy that
was even then sheltering him; to chafe against the
very gentleness that was keeping a truth from him
because the truth would have scalded him; to mis-
take, precisely and exactly as it had always hoped
some bright clever soul one day would, a kindness
for a cruelty, and a depth for a shadow, and a se-
cret for a debt. Then it folded itself back down into
the cold, and settled in to wait — as it had settled
in to wait over the crack in Sami, as it would settle
in to wait a thousand thousand times across all the
ages that were coming: unhurried, untroubled, and
serenely certain that whatever has once begun to

                            79

lean will, if only one is patient enough, in the end,
and of its own weight, fall.
   And the bright soul, who would have laughed
aloud at the suggestion that anything but his own
clear reason moved in him, felt nothing he could
have named. He felt only that the old light, when he
turned to it that night as he always did, stood a half-
step further off than it had the night before — not
withdrawn, never that, the withdrawing was all on
his side, but as though seen now across a hair\{}’s
breadth of cooler air; and that the small kept thing,
the secret he had let lie, had quietly changed its
weight in the dark of him while he was not looking,
so that it no longer sat like a kindness he was spar-
ing a friend but like a debt a friend was owing him.
He could not have said when the one became the
other. He would have sworn no one had touched it.
And in that — in his unshakable certainty that the
lean was wholly his own, that no hand had tilted him
— the cold at the edge of the country found the only
proof it ever needed that the work was going well.
   Adel turned away from the old light still itching,
still unsatisfied, still unanswered — and went to
find Sami, whose bright unclouded certainty was so
much warmer to stand beside than Hilal’s cool and
curving riddles, and asked so much less of him. He
did not notice — for there was, as yet, truly nothing
to notice, no single thing one could have pointed to
and named — that he had begun, by the very small-
est of degrees, by a fraction too fine to be measured,
to choose the brightness that flattered him over the
depth that did not.

                          80

###SECTION: Chapter Nine — The Music of the Higher Place###
The Music of the Higher Place

 While Adel chased his answers through the bright
quick quarter, Hana heard a sound.
   It came to her in the blue hush, where she had
grown used to the far high music that all the listen-
ing souls leaned toward together. But this was not
that music. This came from beneath it — so far un-
der that she could not at first be sure she heard it at
all, rather than felt it: a sound so low and vast it was
less a note than the very foundation a note might be
built upon, held without wavering by something so
unthinkably large that the holding had been going
on since before the bright country was ever lit.
   No one else caught it. The other souls leaned, as
they always leaned, toward the high sweet melody
above; not one stirred toward the low enormous
note beneath. Hana alone had heard it, and be-
cause the one thing she did better than any other
in all the world was to lean, without fear, toward
exactly what she did not understand, she followed
it — leaning past the listening-place, past the gar-
dens whose keynotes she knew, out toward the high

                          81

veiled quarter where the young souls were always,
always turned back.
  This time she was not turned back. Not all the
way. She came to the trembling edge of what a soul
could bear, and was permitted to remain there; and
across a remove that was not a distance, she saw
one of them.

                       •   •    •

There is no telling how large it was. It did not loom,
as a mountain looms over a traveler, for to loom a
thing must share your sky; this shared nothing with
her at all. It simply, overwhelmingly, was: vast past
the meaning of vast, still with a stillness that had
a kind of violence in it — the held stillness of an
unimaginable strain perfectly borne. It was bear-
ing something she could not see, could not have
survived seeing; she understood only that its whole
measureless being was poured out entirely into the
holding-up of a weight beyond her — and that the
low ceaseless note was the very sound of that bear-
ing: a thing that carried, forever, without tiring, the
very floor of everything that was.
   It did not see her. She was less to it than a mote
of dust to a mountain, and had no fraction of its at-
tention to spare. And this was the strange thing she
would carry ever after: she was not wounded by be-
ing nothing to it. Some last inmost room in her, one
she had not known was still latched, came quietly
open; and she felt her own smallness not as a wound
but as a mercy — the particular mercy of standing

                           82

at the foot of a thing so far beyond you that you are
released, all at once, from the long exhausting labor
of being large.
   She was small. It was great. And that vast bent
bearer of the floor of everything — that, she under-
stood with a lurch that was almost joy and almost
terror, and was, in truth, the very beginning of wor-
ship — was itself only a servant, pouring out the
whole of its being in the holding-up of a thing it had
been given to hold. And if even that was a servant:
then what was the One that it served?
   The thought had no end at which to finish; it ran
on past her, out into a brightness she could not fol-
low. She only turned — toward Him, deeper than
she had ever turned — as water that has wandered
far turns at the very last downhill, and goes gladly,
because down is where it was always meant to go.
And the great low note went on beneath everything,
holding, and holding; and she let it hold her too, and
did not try to understand; and in the not-trying she
came nearer to understanding than all her trying
could ever have brought her.

                      •   • •

She told no one. Not because it was a secret — she
did not yet know how to keep one; that grief be-
longed to another, and was already, elsewhere, qui-
etly beginning. She told no one because there were
no words for it anywhere in any world, and because
some sights are given to a soul to be carried, not
spoken. So she carried it back down with her into

                          83

the bright young country, and it changed her, qui-
etly, in the deep way that only the deepest things
ever changed her: not into someone new, but more
wholly, more unmistakably, into the one she had al-
ready, from her very first instant, always been.




                        84

###SECTION: Chapter Ten — Zarif’s Theology###
Zarif’s Theology

 It was Zarif who lightened them again, as in the end
it would always be Zarif.
   He had been thinking. This was, in and of it-
self, a circumstance to beware of, for Zarif think-
ing was rather like weather forming over a still sea:
you could not say in advance what it would pro-
duce, only that it would be remarkable, that it would
arrive without warning, and that someone, some-
where, was going to get wet. He had been turning
his bright restless attention upon the shape of the
whole bright country — how the gardens lay one
against another, how the keynotes shifted as one
crossed between them, where the warmth welled
up from and where it went — and he had arrived,
by a road known only to himself and very likely un-
traceable by anyone else, at a complete, final, and
entirely satisfactory understanding of the structure
of the whole cosmos; which he now, generously, pro-
posed to share with everyone, whether they wished
to receive it or not.
   The country, Zarif explained — settling in with the
deep contentment of a soul about to hold forth at

                         85

length — was shaped, when one really came down
to it, like an enormous fruit. They were all of them
living inside it, snug and small, somewhere just un-
der the rind. The warmth that they all leaned toward
— the radiance in the direction that was not a direc-
tion — was, plainly, the seed at the very center of
the fruit; and the reason that no one, however hard
they leaned, could ever quite reach it was that the
fruit was, regrettably, infinite, so that the center re-
mained always exactly as far off as it had been a
moment before, no nearer and no farther — which
was to say, when one thought about it properly, no
distance away at all, and also the whole of all the dis-
tance there was, both together, forever. The angels,
he went on, warming to his theme, were obviously
the bees — forever crossing the fruit on their grave
important errands, pollinating who could say what.
The great veiled high souls were the parts of the
fruit that had ripened earliest, and sweetest. And
the reason that souls could grow while angels could
not was that the souls were the living seeds of the
fruit, full of becoming, while the angels were merely
its faithful, unchanging skin — here Sami bright-
ened visibly, much taken with the notion of being a
seed full of glorious becoming while the angels were
demoted to mere rind — until Zarif added, blandly,
that the skin, of course, is the part of any fruit that
actually touches the great Outside; so that the an-
gels, being the skin, were in fact nearer to that Out-
side than any seed buried deep in the middle could
ever hope to be; which rather thoroughly spoiled the
point that Sami had just been settling down to enjoy.

                          86

   It was wrong. It was gloriously, comprehensively,
magnificently wrong; there was not one single par-
ticular of the whole grand edifice that was not
wrong; the country was not a fruit, and the warmth
was not a seed, and the veiled souls were not early-
ripened pulp, and the angels were most emphati-
cally not bees. Adel, listening, felt the wrongness
of it pile up in him error upon error, each separate
falsehood lodging like a small unpaid debt, each one
rising up in him and demanding, in the very name
of the truth, to be corrected; and the pressure of all
of them together grew until he could no longer hold
it, and he opened himself to speak, to set the whole
teetering nonsense right —
   — and was forestalled, before a single correction
could leave him, by a sound that not one of them
had ever heard before in all their short existence.
The old light was laughing.

                      •   • •

Not the almost-laugh of the listening-place — not
the warmth that a laugh is only the outermost edge
of. The thing itself, whole and helpless and entire.
Hilal — the deep banked ancient light, the layered
grave glow, the bridge between the wordless and
the word, the oldest soul that any of them had ever
come near — was laughing like the very youngest
soul among them, helplessly, openly, the whole deep
manifold glow of him shaking and brightening with
it; and the bright country seemed to draw in around
the sound and lean toward it the way a cold room

                          87

leans, all at once, toward a fire just kindled in its
hearth. It was, Hana thought, one of the most beau-
tiful things she had yet seen in all that country of
beautiful things: that so old and so deep a light could
still, after everything it must surely have seen, laugh
like that. Zarif, you are wrong, said the old light at
last, when at last it could shape anything at all. You
are wrong in every part of it. The country is not a
fruit, and you know perfectly well that it is not a fruit.
And yet — the center that cannot be reached by any
leaning, that is always no distance away and all the
distance there is, both at once — child, where did
you get that? Where did you find it? You have taken a
true thing, one of the truest and the strangest there
is, and wrapped it round in a hundred false ones,
and handed it to us like a gift buried in far, far too
much bright paper. Tell me. Was it on purpose?
   Always, said Zarif, who was by now beaming so
broadly that he had quite given up any pretense of
having merely theorized about fruit.
   And Hana understood. She understood the whole
of it at once and complete, in the wordless way
she understood the things that could not be said
in words. She saw that Zarif had not, for one in-
stant, been explaining the cosmos at all. He had
been smuggling. He had taken up the one truth that
was too vast and too strange ever to be set down
plainly — that the Nearest of all cannot be reached
by the crossing of any distance, because He is not in
distance at all; that He is closer than the leaning of
the heart and farther than the farthest rim of every-
thing, both at the very same time, and forever, and

                           88

without the least contradiction — and he had hid-
den that unspeakable truth down inside a ridiculous
fruit, precisely so that it might slip past the watch-
ful part of them that demanded edges and proofs
and plain straight answers, and lodge itself instead
in the deeper part that was able to hold a mystery
in its hands without crushing the life out of it. The
fruit was the paper. The whole absurd and glorious
cosmology was the paper. And the paper had been
made funny, deliberately, lovingly, so that none of
them — not even the most anxious among them, not
even the most demanding — would be afraid of the
gift inside.
   Adel did not understand. Or rather — and this
was the harder and the sadder thing, the thing that
would tell in the long account — he understood the
truth itself perfectly well, grasped it cleanly, could
have restated it in plain terms had anyone asked him
to; and he could not forgive the wrapping. Why bury
it? he thought, with the old hot impatience rising in
him again. Why hide a true thing inside a false one?
Why not simply say it, cleanly and plainly and once,
and be understood, and be done? The fruit annoyed
him exactly as the riddle had annoyed him, and the
held-back answer before that; it felt to him like one
more door shut politely in his face, one more plain-
ness withheld on purpose, one more invitation to be
charmed and beguiled and made to smile when all
that he had ever asked was to be told. He laughed
with the others — the laugh could not be helped;
not even Adel could hold out for long against the
sound of Hilal laughing. But underneath the laugh,

                         89

in the deep cold place that was so slowly, so invisibly
growing in him, the small hot grain turned over once
again; and he did not notice — there was nothing
yet to notice — that he had begun, somewhere be-
low his own awareness, to keep a quiet and careful
ledger: an accounting of every truth that had ever
come to him dressed in something other than the
plain, every kindness that had worn the coat of a
riddle, every gift that had been wrapped instead of
simply handed over. The ledger was very short, as
yet. It would grow.
   Zarif, for his part, was thinking of none of this.
Zarif had already moved on, and was busy explain-
ing, to a wholly baffled soul who had made the error
of drifting too near, that the far high music — the
sweet unreachable melody of the listening-place —
was nothing more than the great fruit humming qui-
etly to itself, away down at its infinite center, out of
sheer and overflowing contentment. Which was, of
course, also wrong. And which was also, in a way
that not one of them, then or ever after, could quite
find the means to argue against, not entirely.




                          90

###SECTION: Interlude — The One Who Paused###
The One Who Paused

 He was older now, by whatever the word means in
a country with no years to count — old enough that
the pausing before speech had become no longer
a discipline but simply the shape of him, as a path
worn long enough into a hillside stops looking like a
choice and starts looking like the hill’s own idea.
  He had watched his people, in that time, drift fur-
ther from what they had been before the breaking —
not all at once, for nothing in that long history had
ever happened all at once, but by the same fine, un-
measured degrees that a soul not far off, in a bright
country he did not yet know existed, was even now
beginning to prefer a flattering brightness to a truth-
ful depth. He did not despise them for it. This, more
than his devotion, more than the pausing, was the
thing that set him furthest apart from the rest of his
kind: that he had watched his own people go wrong,
one careless small kindness withheld at a time, and
had felt, instead of contempt, only a grief he did not
know how to put down.
  He asked, once — of one of the fire-bright elders
who still remembered the old breaking first-hand —

                          91

whether it could be mended. Whether a household,
once emptied, could ever be filled again as it had
been.
    The elder did not answer him kindly. Some things,
the elder said, are not mended. They are only, if a
mercy comes, no longer required to be carried by
everyone who was ever near them. And even that —
even the laying-down of what cannot be mended —
is not a thing our own kind has ever yet been shown
how to do.
    He carried the answer a long while, turning it as a
soul not far off would one day turn a riddle from an
old cold light at the edge of everything. He did not
know, and could not yet know, that the laying- down
the elder had named as beyond his people’s reach
was, at that very hour, being prepared — carefully,
patiently, and by a hand neither he nor any of his
kind had troubled yet to look for — for a people not
yet made at all, out of clay, out of water, out of noth-
ing his own kind would have thought fit to build a
soul from.
    He only kept pausing. He only kept turning to-
ward the flame. It was the one thing that was, un-
mistakably, his to do; and he had long since stopped
needing to be told that it mattered, to go on doing
it.




                          92

###SECTION: Chapter Eleven — The Vision of Adam###
The Vision of Adam

 They were still full of the lights — the purity of
Maryam, the beauty of Yusuf, the wellspring of all
brightness, the small fierce voice that loved for noth-
ing and asked them to do the same — when a new
thing began to move through the bright country.
It was not a sight, and it was not a sound. It was
an announcement, in the way that the turning of
a season is an announcement: not declared any-
where, not spoken by anyone, but felt — a sense that
spread, soul to soul, across the whole of the lit coun-
try at once, like the first change in the air before
a great weather, that something was about to be
made. Something new. Something that had never,
in all the ages, been made before.
   And they were given, all of them, every soul in the
country, a small vision of it. Not the thing itself —
for the thing did not yet exist, was not yet so much
as begun — but its substance; its raw and waiting
beginning. They saw clay.
   It was being gathered. From everywhere. They
saw — in the way that one sees within a vision,

                         93

which is not as that one sees with eyes — handfuls
of the very stuff of a world that did not yet exist:
clay of every shade and tone, red and white and
black and brown, smooth and coarse, sweet earth
and bitter earth, drawn from every quarter and cor-
ner of it. And all of it was gathered into one place,
and kneaded into one substance; and not one hand-
ful of it was set above another. For it was all, at the
root, the one same earth; and the One who gathered
it gathered it from everywhere, on purpose, so that
the thing made from it would carry the whole world
folded up inside itself, and so that no part of that
world could ever look upon another and name itself
the better clay.
   They did not understand what they were being
shown. But each one of them felt, with a strange
and a sudden and a wholly personal certainty, that
it concerned him — that this making, whatever it
was to be, was not some far and lofty matter of the
great ones and the veiled lights, to be watched from
a respectful distance and then forgotten; but some-
thing that reached, somehow, all the way down to
small and ordinary souls like themselves, and that
would, in some way that they could not yet begin to
guess at, become the whole of their own story.
   Hilal gathered up the meaning of their unease,
and answered it. You are about to learn, he told
them, what it is to be small — smaller than you can,
here, in the warmth, so much as imagine being. You
are about to be folded into clay, and set down into
time, and made to forget, and sent down very low.
It will frighten you, when at the last you come to un-

                          94

derstand it. But hear me now, and keep this above
all the rest: it is in the smallness that the greatness
hides. The angels, who are great, were never made
small, and so there are heights they can never climb,
having never had the deep place to climb up out of.
You will be made small precisely so that you may
rise — so that the rising, when it comes, will be your
own, chosen and fought for and won in the dark,
and worth immeasurably more than any greatness
handed down for free. So do not fear the clay. The
clay is the beginning of your honor — though it will
feel, for a long while, like the very end of it.
   They did not fully understand that either. But they
held it, as they had learned by now to hold all the
things Hilal said — not as answers, but as seeds:
kept, and carried, against the far-off season in which
they would at last be needed. And the announce-
ment went on spreading, quietly, through the whole
bright country, soul to soul to soul — the sense,
grown now almost to a certainty, of a great making
about to begin. And the four of them turned, with
all the uncountable rest, toward the warmth at the
heart of everything, and waited. They did not know
— how could they have known? — that what they
were waiting for was the gathering together of every
soul that had ever been or would ever be, the whole
of humankind from the first of them to the very last,
into one single assembly outside of time; and the
asking, there, of the one question upon which every
other question, in every world, would forever after
turn.



                          95

  ”Am I not your Lord?” They said: ”Yes — we bear
witness.”


                      •   •    •


The Qur’an · Sūrah al-Aʿrāf (7:172)



                      ACT IV
                      • •      •




                      • •      •


               The Day of Alastu




                          96

###SECTION: Chapter Twelve — The Gathering###
The Gathering

 And then the call came.
   It was not a sound. Nothing in that country had
ever been quite a sound, and this least of all. It was
a turning — a single vast turning of attention, as
though the whole bright country, every soul in it and
every garden and the warmth at the heart of all of
it, had been asked, in one wordless and irresistible
instant, to face one way. And it faced. They all faced.
There was no choosing in it, no more than the tide
chooses the moon that lifts it; the call did not re-
quest their attention but simply took it, gathered it,
drew it round as a hand draws back a curtain, and
they were turned before they had so much as under-
stood that any turning had begun.
   They were being gathered.
   Hana had thought, until that hour, that she had
begun to learn how large the country was. She
had wandered through its gardens, and crossed be-
tween its keynotes; she had stood at its furthest bor-
der and looked upon the Tree; she had seen the mul-
titudes of the listening-place and called them, in her

                         97

own small reckoning, many. She had known nothing
at all. For now, as the great turning drew them all
in toward one place that was not a place, she saw
— all at once, in a single overwhelming widening of
her seeing, a widening that frightened her even as
it filled her — how many the souls truly were. They
were beyond number. Not many; not a great many;
but past the very reach of counting altogether, so
far past it that the failure of the counting was itself
a kind of vertigo — light upon light upon light upon
light, ranged and gathered and pressed close, ev-
ery soul that had ever been spoken and every soul
that ever would be, the whole of humankind from
the very first of them to the very last, drawn in to-
gether into one single assembly that no eye in all
creation but His alone could ever have held entire.
It was a terror. It was also, in the very same breath,
a glory; and she could not have said, then or ever
after, where the one of them left off and the other
began.
   In all that endlessness she found Adel — or, more
truly, was turned to him: the chord in her sounded,
low and sure, and she leaned toward the sounding,
and there he was, the high quick note, exactly where
the chord had always promised that he would be.
And beside him stood Sami, bright and reaching
even now, even here. And near them Zarif, for once
in all his existence wholly and uncharacteristically
hushed. And folded close about them all, the deep
old layered glow of Hilal, who had come to stand
among them as though he had always, from the very
first, meant to be there for precisely this. The five of

                          98

them together: one small warm knot of the known
and the familiar, in the midst of an ocean of the un-
known.
   They saw the others: not as faces — there were
no faces yet — but as what each would one day be,
for the knowing was given them so they might un-
derstand the weight of what was about to happen.
They saw the lights of those who would be prophets,
blazing among the rest. They saw the lights of those
who would be tyrants, and shuddered without under-
standing why. They saw small lights that would burn
in the world below for only a breath and then go
out — children who would die in cradles, in famines,
in the cold — and these, Hana saw with a catch in
her, were not dim, as she had braced herself for;
they were among the clearest and steadiest of all
the lights, as though He had given the briefest vis-
its the purest flames. They saw the lights of those
who would die for Him, and the lights of those who
would forget Him utterly; and all of them were here
now, gathered together, for the one thing about to
be asked of every soul that would ever live.
   And among them, unmarked by any of the souls
there, stood the cold thing too. Not at the edge, now.
Not out in the thin places where the warmth ran
low. Here: here in the very midst of the gathering,
ranged among the high and the shining ones, near
the front of them, in all the splendor that it still wore
in those days before the fall. For it had been sum-
moned with the rest, and it had come with the rest,
and it would answer, in a moment, with the rest.
It stood among them unfallen, ungrieved-for, unsus-

                           99

pected by any — and only the One who had gathered
them all, and the old light called Hilal, knew what it
was, or what, before very long, it would become.
   And then everything went still. It was the deepest
stillness that any of them had ever known — deeper
than the blue hush of the listening-place, deeper
than the wake of Maryam, deeper even than the
long fall before the Crown Light. The far high music
ceased. The leaning of all things ceased. Even the
warmth at the heart of everything seemed to gather
itself inward, and hold, and wait. The whole of all
the creation that could be gathered had been gath-
ered; and now it held its breath — every soul that
had ever been or would ever be, the numberless and
the named, hushed together into one assembly out-
side of time — and waited for the Voice.




                         100

###SECTION: Chapter Thirteen — The Witnessing###
The Witnessing

 The silence held.
   It held as the silence before rain holds — when
the whole world has gone deliberately, expectantly
quiet, because something is about to be given that
will require its undivided attention. The cosmos
waited. The bright country waited. Every soul that
had ever been spoken, and every soul that ever
would be spoken — for they were all of them present
now, the past and the future alike folded together
into the one timeless instant of His attention —
waited.
   Then the Voice.
   There is no telling what the Voice was like. Any
telling diminishes it; any image set beside it fails
it, and falls away. We can say only that it was; and
that it was His; and that when it came, it came from
no place, because it was itself the source and the
ground of all places — from the One whose near-
ness is nearer than nearness, whose word is the
very floor that holds up every other word that would
ever be spoken in any tongue in any world. It did

                       101

not come down to them, for there was no down; it
did not arrive, for it had never once been absent; it
simply was, suddenly and totally, the one fact in all
of being, and every soul in the numberless assem-
bly knew it for what it was, and could not not have
known.
   It asked one question. Am I not your Lord?
   It was not asked because He did not know the an-
swer. He knew. He had known the answer before
the Pen was made, before the first light was lit, be-
fore the question itself had any need to be framed.
It was asked because the souls needed to answer
— because the answering was the whole purpose of
the gathering, the very reason that every soul that
would ever be had been drawn together here out-
side of time: the one foundational answering, the
first and the deepest Yes, that every later answering,
in every soul, in every hour, in every world that was
coming, would forever after be either a turning-back
toward, or a turning-away from. Everything that any
of them would ever do was about to be given its root.
   And every soul answered. Yes. We bear witness.
   It came from every light at once, in the single
now of the asking. From the lights that would be
prophets, and from the lights that would be tyrants.
From the small clear lights that would burn only a
breath. From the light of the cold thing itself, stand-
ing splendid among the high ones — who bore wit-
ness too, that day, with all the rest, and who could
never afterward, in all the long ruin that was com-
ing, truthfully say that he had not. From every light,
without one exception anywhere in all the endless

                         102

assembly. There was no withholding. There was no
soul that hung back, no voice that fell silent, no cor-
ner of the gathering where the Yes did not sound.
The witness was complete, and it was universal, and
it would stand — forever, and against everything,
and past the very end of all the worlds.
   Hana’s answer was a tear — if tears had been in-
vented, and they had not, but there is no other word
for it: the whole of her joy at simply being asked,
poured out of her in a single brimming Yes. She had
been waiting for this question without ever know-
ing that she waited for it; all her turning, from her
first instant, had been a turning toward exactly this;
and when at last it came, she answered it with the
entire whole of herself, gladly, without an instant’s
pause, as a flower answers the sun it has been turn-
ing toward all the long morning without ever know-
ing why.
   Adel’s answer was the stopping of his counting.
For one perfect instant the restless tireless engine
of him — the asking, the measuring, the turning-
over that never ceased — went wholly, blessedly
still; and his whole vast asking turned, all at once,
into answering; and he said Yes with the full and
undivided force of the very mind that was forever
questioning, so that his questioning itself was, for
that one instant, not silenced but fulfilled — healed
— made finally and perfectly whole. He would chase
the memory of that instant, without ever once be-
ing able to remember it, for the entire rest of his
life; and never find it; and never stop looking.



                         103

   Sami’s answer came a beat late. He said Yes — of
course he said Yes; every soul that was or would ever
be said Yes; there was no other answer, and he made
no other — but in him, before the Yes, there came
the smallest hesitation. Not doubt; never doubt; he
no more doubted the Lordship than the cold thing
did. It was pride: a flicker, faint and swift and almost
nothing, of a wish to have been asked something
other than what all the rest were asked, something
finer, something his alone; a flicker of resentment
at being merely one voice, indistinguishable, in a
boundless ocean of voices. Then the flicker passed,
and the Yes came, and it was real, and it was sealed
with all the others. But it had come a beat late. And
the cold thing, listening, in the splendor of its own
unfallen ranks, marked even that. Marked it, and
kept it.
   And Zarif’s answer was a laugh. A single bright
helpless peal of a laugh, that held the entire whole of
his holy foolishness folded up inside it — the laugh of
a soul simply too glad, too overbrimming, to manage
to be solemn even here, even now, even before the
Voice itself. And Hilal heard it: heard the one laugh
in all that vast ocean of grave and trembling yeses,
ringing out clear and unembarrassed and entirely
unrepentant — and was, for the first time in a very
long age of the world, moved past the telling of it.
   And when all the answers had been given —
though there was no before and no after to them; all
the uncountable yeses arrived together, in the one
single now of the asking — the gathering breathed
again. Something had been made, in that instant,

                         104

that would never in all the worlds be unmade. From
that moment on, no soul could ever truthfully say
that it had not known Him; every soul now car-
ried the witness, sealed in deep beneath everything
else that it was, beneath even the great forgetting
that was coming. The covenant of the whole of hu-
mankind was struck, and struck forever. And the
Voice did not speak again. It did not need to. The
greatest of all the works had been done. And now,
in the vast hush that came after it, two small lights
among the numberless turned, of their own quiet ac-
cord, toward each other.




                        105

###SECTION: Chapter Fourteen — The Pledge Between Two###
The Pledge Between Two

 Hana and Adel found each other.
  That is not quite as to say it, though there is no
truer way that the words allow. They had not been
searching; there was no searching to be done in that
place, no distance anywhere between them to be
crossed, no losing that a finding could undo. It is
nearer the truth to say that they were turned toward
each other — turned by the very same turning that
had, a moment before, turned the whole of them to-
ward Him; as though, in the great witnessing, He
had reached into each of them and opened a small
hidden door that neither had known was there, and
on the far side of both those doors, when they swung
wide, lay one and the same room.
  They did not speak. The gift of speech was still far
ahead of them, waiting for them down in the bodies
and the tongues of a world that did not yet exist.
Here there was only the standing-toward: she stood
toward him, and he stood toward her, and the stand-
ing was the whole of it. And all around them the
great assembly had already begun to loosen, and to

                        106

stir, and to disperse, each soul carrying away into
itself the new and permanent fact of the witnessing;
and some of them, passing near the two who stood
so turned toward one another, felt in the passing a
small unaccountable warmth, and did not know its
source, as one feels the heat of a hidden fire through
a wall and cannot anywhere find the hearth.
   Hilal stood a little apart from them, and watched,
and was glad — in the deep and silent way of the
very old, who have learned that the gladdest things
are often best not spoken. He had known that this
was coming. He had not known its shape; even he
had not been shown the shape of it; but now he
saw the shape, watching the two small lights turn
toward each other in the loosening crowd, and he
found it good.
   What passed between them then was not a
speech, and it was not even a vow, in the way that
the word would later come to be used — not formal
words, set down and witnessed and sworn. It was a
pledge in the older and the deeper sense, the sense
that comes before all speaking and underlies it: the
placing of one’s whole self, freely and entirely, at the
disposal of something. And they placed themselves,
the two of them together, at the disposal of three
things.
   The first was Him. And this was easy; this was, in
truth, already done. Every soul in the whole gather-
ing had just placed itself at His disposal in the great
universal Yes, and the two of them, in pledging it
now again, were doing nothing new — only making
private and particular what had been universal, as

                         107

a great public vow is made dear, and one’s own, by
being whispered, once more, alone, in the dark, to
no one. We will remember Him, they pledged. What-
ever else falls away from us — and so much, so very
much, will fall away — this, this one thing, will re-
main.
   The second was each other. And this was harder;
for they did not yet truly know what each other
meant; they had only just, in the gathering, begun to
know themselves. But in the very not-knowing there
was already a recognition older than any knowledge:
that they had been sounded in the same key, an oc-
tave apart; that their two lights had been made to
go on answering one another across whatever dis-
tances were coming, however vast; that something
in each of them had been shaped, from the very first,
for the other. We will find each other, they pledged.
Wherever the descent takes us; however far apart
we are flung; however deep, however total, the for-
getting. We will find each other again. Some part
of us will remember. Some part of us will not, will
never, let go.
   And the third pledge — and here something hap-
pened in that bright country that not even Hilal,
watching, had foreseen — was not for themselves at
all. They did not know what dying was. They had no
notion of the dunya, no notion of fear, no notion of a
body failing in the dark or a soul slipping alone and
untended out of the world. But in the great witness-
ing, only a moment before, they had been opened
— for one single impossible instant — to the whole
of every soul that would ever be. And in that whole,

                         108

among the uncountable lights, they had seen some-
thing particular, and could not now unsee it. They
had seen the small lights that would go out alone
at the very end. The lights that would forget Him
in their last dark, and have no one beside them to
remember on their behalf. The lights that would die
afraid.
   No one had told them to do anything at all about
those lights. The witnessing had not commanded it;
it had laid no charge upon them; it had only shown.
But in the seeing they had recognized, together, in
the silent communion of their two near lights, that
they could not unsee it — that the sight had laid a
weight into them that would not lift; and that to be
given such a sight, and then to do nothing with it,
is itself a way of refusing the sight. So they pledged
it. Not in words; they had no words. In the shape
of their turning toward each other, and in the small
joined motion of their two lights toward the far place
where the lost and frightened ones would one day
be: We will go to where a light is about to go out in
fear. We will go to the ones who die alone. And we
will be — together — the hands that hold them, until
the dark becomes Light.
   They did not know what they were promis-
ing. They could not possibly have known. But the
promise was real, and it was sealed into them in the
very making of it, without their seeing it sealed; and
very near to them, in the loosening and dispersing
crowd, the cold thing paused in its passing, and lis-
tened. And what it heard, it did not like. And what it



                        109

did not like, it did not — it never did, it never once
in all the ages would — forget.
   They held the pledge a long moment, the three
of them — Hana, and Adel, and the One who had
heard it made. Then, gently, they turned again to-
ward Him, because that was what they always did,
what she had always done and what he was learn-
ing to do; and the great gathering went on dispers-
ing all around them; and the work that had been
done in that one small bright corner of the vast as-
sembly began, quietly, and seen by none but Hilal
and the One who sees all things, to make its long
way toward a world that did not yet exist — where
it would, in the fullness of the time that was about
to begin, become flesh, and become hands, and be-
come — though they would not know it, would not
remember it, for the whole long length of both their
lives — the living answer to a question that the uni-
verse had asked aloud long before either of the two
of them was ever spoken.




                         110

###SECTION: Chapter Fifteen — The Listener in the Shadow###
The Listener in the Shadow

 It had answered the great Yes with all the rest, and
meant the witness it bore — for it did not doubt, had
never doubted, and would never doubt that He was
the Lord. Unbelief was never its ruin. It believed
everything, and believed it perfectly, more clearly
than any trembling soul in the gathering. It simply
bore its witness as a man signs a contract — a thing
owed, rendered, and thereby squared. It had given
its yes, and considered that the yes had bought it
something, and had already begun to reckon up pre-
cisely what.
   So it moved through the loosening assembly, still
wearing the brightness it wore before the fall, keep-
ing its quiet and ceaseless accounts — for that was
what it had become somewhere in the long ages no
one had witnessed: a keeper of ledgers, unable any
longer to look upon a glory without calculating what
it was worth, and to whom, and what was owed. Its
accounting carried it near to two small lights that
had only just become a chord.
   It paused, and caught the shape of what was pass-
ing between them: the placing of two whole selves at

                        111

the disposal of something, and the faint gold tracery
of a Seal beginning to form upon their joined light. It
stopped to read the pledge, as a keeper of accounts
can never quite stop himself reading a ledger left
open before him.
   The first two terms did not trouble it. To remem-
ber Him — every fool in the gathering had pledged
as much an hour since. To find each other again —
sentiment, beneath its notice. But the third term it
read twice, and on the second reading something
moved in it that it had not felt in longer than it could
remember.
   For it too had stood in that gathering. It too had
been shown the whole of every soul that would ever
be, and had looked upon that whole with a slow, ris-
ing, private satisfaction: the ones it would whisper
into ruin, the ones it would teach to forget, the ones
who would die at the last alone and far from Him.
And these two — not yet born, not yet anything at
all — had just pledged, knowing nothing of what it
would cost them, to go to exactly those. To seek out
the very ones it most intended to lose, and turn them
back toward Him at the one hour it would have la-
bored a whole life to win.
   The thing that moved in the cold thing was not
hatred. For the space of a single breath it looked
upon that third pledge and felt something perilously
close to wonder — the faint far ghost of a thing that,
in a creature not yet ruined, would have been the
beginning of love. And it could not bear it. So it did
what it had long ago learned to do with everything:
it turned the wonder inside out, in the same breath,

                         112

into a cold and exact and wholly personal hatred. If
these two were beautiful, then it was not itself the
most beautiful of all created things — and that it
would never allow, though the not-allowing should
cost it everything, as in the end it would.
   It marked them. It filed the chord of them, and the
Seal, and the shape of the pledge, away in the cold
and orderly place where it kept the names of those
it would one day answer for. There were not many
names there yet. There would soon be two more.
   It did not swear anything, not yet. But the mark-
ing was done, and the hatred was lit, and would not
go out. And the one small crack in the cold thing —
the flicker of wonder it had strangled in its own cra-
dle — was lit too, in that same instant, though it did
not know it, and would have died sooner than con-
fess it. That crack would still be there, unhealed and
unconfessed, on the day that mercy came at the very
last and found, as it always found, the door bolted
fast from within.

                      •    • •

They did not see the sealing. They felt only that
something settled: that the pledge, a moment before
a motion between them, became, all at once, a fact.
  What was set upon them, they could not see — it
was set in the one place a soul can never turn to
look upon, in the deep grain of what they most fun-
damentally were. But it was there: two arcs of pale
gold, facing each other across a small bright space
and not quite touching — like the two halves of a sin-

                          113

gle crescent turned to regard one another; like two
hands reaching, not yet joined. It would warm when
they remembered; it would cool when they drifted;
and it would be, after all the worlds and all the for-
getting, the one thing by which they would be known
to each other again.
   They could not see their own, but each, looking
at the other, saw a faint new brightness he could
not name, and did the only thing one can do with
a wordless and precious thing: he kept it, stored it
away within himself, as though some part of him al-
ready knew that this faint gold tracery would be the
only thread left to follow home.
   Three saw the sealing who were not themselves
sealed. Hilal saw it, and his old deep light grew gen-
tle, and he said nothing at all — for some things are
spoiled by being spoken, even in blessing.
   The cold thing saw it too, and studied the shape of
the Seal — the two arcs, the small open space, the
turn of the ancient script — and learned it by heart,
as one memorizes the face of a thing one means to
hunt, so that it would know that mark again wher-
ever in all the worlds it might come upon it.
   And a young angel saw it: one passing on some
bright errand, who slowed, and stopped, and looked,
and did not know what it was or why it should be set
upon two such small lights among so many. He felt
only, with the sudden certainty of the very young,
that it mattered; and he carried the puzzle of it away,
unsolved. His name was Nuri. And a long age hence,
when he was given at last a small soul of his own to
guard, and bent close to her light for the first time,

                         114

and found upon it this very same tracery of pale gold,
he would understand in a rush what he had seen on
the day the worlds were gathered — and from that
moment on he would guard her with everything he
had, and never once tell her why.


                      •    • •


A glimpse, out of its time.
  There would come a night, in a small bright room
above a canal, when a grown man would turn a
scar-shaped stone over and over in his fingers with-
out knowing why he could not put it down, and
would feel, for no reason he could name, that he had
promised someone something once, and had not yet
paid it. And there would come a morning, in a poor
quarter of an old city, when a young woman kneel-
ing over a dying man’s hand would feel a warmth
pass from her into him that was not entirely her own
giving — as though some part of her had done this
before, in some country she could not place, and
knew exactly how it was done. The Seal does not
speak. It only warms, and cools, and waits to be fol-
lowed.




                          115

###SECTION: Chapter Sixteen — The Calm Before###
The Calm Before

 The great assembly dispersed, at the last, all the
way; and the bright country was only itself again —
the gardens, and the warmth, and the numberless
wandering souls returned to their wandering. But
nothing was the same, nor would ever be again. The
witness was in every soul now, sealed in beneath ev-
erything else that a soul might be or do. And in two
small lights, besides, a covenant was sealed that the
rest of them did not carry.
  Hana and Adel found each other once more — the
chord sounding low and sure, the leaning, the sim-
ple being-there — and without choosing it, without
deciding anything at all, they drifted together into
a garden that neither of them had ever entered be-
fore. It was a deep and a quiet place; warm, but with
a grave and a slow and a patient warmth, not the
bright quick warmth of the gardens where the new
souls played; and the rose-gold light in it banked
low, and burned steady, and seemed somehow to
be waiting for something with an endless and an un-
troubled patience. It was the garden, though they

                        116

did not know it, where those souls rested who were
bound for the long hard lives — the ones who would
be given much to carry, and a great while in which
to carry it, and roads to walk that would ask of them
very nearly more than they had to give. They never
guessed what the garden truly was. They knew only
that it received them gently, and without a word, as
a place receives those it has long been expecting;
and that they were content, for once in all their rest-
less wandering, simply to stay.
   They did not speak; there was still no speaking,
and would be none until the world that had the
tongues for it was made. They only rested there
together, the two of them — two notes of a single
chord, struck, and let ring, and left to hang and to
mingle in the warm grave air. And it was, of all the
uncountable things that had happened to them in
that bright country — the Tree, and the lights, and
the Voice, and the great Yes — the very simplest;
and it was the one that Hana would reach back for,
without ever once knowing that she reached, in ev-
ery hard and frightened hour of all the worlds that
were coming: not the wonders, and not the glories,
but only this. The being-beside. The not-alone. The
quiet.
   Hilal came to them there. He did not stay long,
and he did not say very much; he had never been a
one for saying much, and least of all now, at the very
threshold, with so much already said and so little of
it any use at all against what was coming. He only
looked at the two of them, resting in the patient light
with their new Seal warm upon them, turned a little

                         117

toward each other and a little toward Him; and what
he left with them was very small, and very quiet, and
they would not understand it for the whole length of
two lifetimes. Hold this, he said. Hold this exactly as
it is — this hour, this quiet, the warmth of it, and the
being-beside. It will be the only time.
    They did not understand him. How could they
have? They were together; they would always be
together; the chord had only just been struck, and
the covenant only just sealed, and the Seal was still
warm and new upon them — what could he possibly
have meant, the only time? But they had learned, by
now, to hold the things that Hilal said even when
they could not yet understand them — to hold them
the way one holds a sealed letter, unopened, against
the far-off day on which one will at last be given
leave to break the seal and read. So they held it. And
they did not know — they could not have borne to
know — that they were holding, even as he gave it to
them, the memory of the last hour they would have
like this one for the whole of two long lifetimes, and
the whole of the long dark that lay stretched out be-
tween.
    Sami was not with them. He had drawn a little
apart, of late — not far, and not pointedly, nothing
that anyone could have named or reproached him
for; only a little, as a soul draws quietly apart when
the gladness of others has begun, by some degree
too small to measure, to chafe at it. He told himself
that he was giving the two of them their peace. It
was a generous thought, and he was pleased with
himself for thinking it; and he did not look at all

                         118

closely — he was learning, by now, the great and ru-
inous art of not looking closely — at why the giving
of that peace should cost him nothing whatever that
he wished to keep. The coal in him was patient, too.
It had learned its patience from a master, though it
did not know that yet. It could wait through a long
calm quite as easily as through a storm; more easily,
perhaps; for in a calm, no one is watching.
   At the garden’s edge, Zarif had found the young
angel Nuri again — or Nuri had failed, once again,
to make good his escape from Zarif — and was ex-
plaining to him, with an enormous and an unshake-
able seriousness, an entirely new and an entirely
wrong theory of where exactly the warmth at the
heart of all things actually came from. Nuri, who
knew — being an angel, and a conscientious one —
