perfectly well where it came from, and whose whole
bright nature strained and ached to correct the er-
ror, said nothing; held his peace; out of a politeness
so pained, and so heroic, that it came very near to
being a kind of martyrdom. And Zarif knew that Nuri
knew. That was the joke; that had always been the
joke. Zarif was wrong on purpose, gloriously and de-
liberately wrong, and was letting the young angel be
polite about it as a kind of gift, a thing freely given;
and the two of them sat there together at the edge
of the patient garden, the holy fool and the consci-
entious angel, each one generously and elaborately
enduring the other — and it was, though neither of
them would ever have dreamed of saying so, a small
and a perfect and an entirely real friendship; and it



                         119

would matter, later, more than either of them could
possibly have guessed.
   And Hana, resting in the patient light beside the
one she had pledged to find again through all the
worlds, felt, for just a moment, a thing she had no
name for: a thin, formless sadness; a premonition
with no picture anywhere in it; the very faintest pos-
sible sense, rising from she knew not where, that
they would not always be as they were in that hour.
It frightened her — nearly the first thing that had
ever frightened her in all that bright country. And
she did the one thing she had always, from her very
first instant, known to do with whatever frightened
her. She turned toward Him — toward the warmth
at the heart of everything — and she laid the form-
less sadness down before Him, wordlessly, and with-
out complaint, as she laid everything down before
Him; and Adel, resting close beside her, felt her
turn, and turned too, as the high note follows the low
without ever being asked; and for one last long while
the two of them rested there together, turned to-
ward Him, held in the patient rose-gold light, sealed,
and warm, and beside each other, and not yet — not
quite yet — afraid.

                      •    • •

And then, very gently, the descent began.
  He refused, and was arrogant, and became of
those who deny.

                      •    • •

                          120

The Qur’an · Sūrah al-Baqarah (2:34)



                      ACT V
                     • •     •




                     • •     •


                    The Fall




                       121

###SECTION: Interlude — A Stirring Felt From Very Far Off###
A Stirring Felt From Very Far Off

 He felt it before he understood it — as the whole
of his own country felt it, each in their own fashion,
though almost none of them troubled to ask what
the feeling was.
   Something was being made. He could not have
told you how he knew; only that the flame he had
turned toward, all his long and patient life, seemed
of a sudden to lean — not away from him, never that,
but outward, toward some other work, some other
waiting thing, as a parent’s attention leans, gladly
and without any lessening of love, toward a second
child newly come. He felt no jealousy in it. This sur-
prised him a little, turning it over; he had expected,
if he felt anything at all, to feel some smallness at
being no longer the only audience for that turning.
What he felt instead was closer to relief — an old,
unnamed ache easing, somewhere far down, at the
thought that whatever new thing was coming might,
perhaps, be permitted to begin again what his own
people had broken so early and so thoroughly.
   He did not know its name. He would not know it,
in truth, for a long while yet — long after the small

                        122

hollow figure of clay had already been shaped and
set waiting, long after a certain proud and shining
one of a wholly different order of the made had al-
ready begun, though none of the fire-kin could see
it, to circle that same figure with a question in its
heart it did not yet know was a temptation.
   He knew only the leaning, and the relief, and
the old private practice, unbroken across all his
long life: he paused, before he let himself hope
too far ahead of what he had actually been given,
and asked, silently, whether the hope was true, and
whether it was his to hold at all.
   It was, though he could not yet know it, both.




                        123

###SECTION: Chapter Seventeen — The Forming of Adam###
The Forming of Adam

 The descent had begun — but gently, and not all
at once; and before the souls were taken down into
the world that was being readied for them, there
was one last thing that they were given to witness.
The making they had been told of. The thing that
had never, in all the long ages of all that was, been
made before.
   They watched the clay be gathered — the red and
the white, the black and the brown, the smooth and
the coarse, the sweet earth and the bitter — drawn
up out of every quarter of a world that did not yet ex-
ist and kneaded together into one single substance,
and not one grain of it set above another. And they
watched that substance be shaped: worked, and
gentled, and smoothed, with a care that astonished
them, into a single figure. A form like nothing the
bright country had ever held before. Two hands; two
feet; a face turned a little upward, as though it were
already, even unmade, even unbreathing, listening
for something it had not yet been given ears to hear.
It was small. It was strange. And it was — they could

                         124

not have said why — already dear to them; for it was
their own clay, the very stuff that they themselves
would shortly be folded down into.
   And then the figure lay still, in the midst of the
bright country, in a kind of clearing of the light, with
the rose-gold warmth banked low all around it and
the great ranks of the angels drawn up at a rever-
ent remove. It did not move, and it did not breathe;
for there was as yet nothing within it to move, or to
breathe. A vessel, beautifully made, and waiting to
be filled. It lay so a long while — forty days, in the
reckoning of a world that would one day have days,
though as yet there were none — and a hush set-
tled over all that country and would not lift. The far
high music sank to almost nothing. The very warmth
seemed to lean inward, gathered and waiting. And
every soul, and every angel, and the cold thing too,
watched the small still shape and waited to see what
would be done with it.

                       •    •    •

It was in that long hush — in the waiting, before
ever the breath was given — that the cold thing
came near the clay. It came as it did all things in
those last days of its splendor: unhurried, and cer-
tain, and magnificent, trailing behind it a thin chill
that troubled the warm air as a cold current trou-
bles a warm sea. The souls felt it pass among them,
and were uneasy, and did not yet know to fear it.
It moved through the drawn-up ranks, through the
rose-gold light, into the clearing where the figure

                           125

lay; and it stood over the small mute form of mud,
and looked down at the thing it had somehow al-
ready been given to understand it would be asked,
before long, to honor. And it began to circle.
   It was in this hour, though no one watching knew
it yet, that the cold thing became what it would be
for all the worlds to come. It did not begin in ha-
tred. It began in something almost like inquiry. It
had been the highest of all the worshippers, the
most knowing of all the host; it had spent uncounted
ages in the very nearness of its Lord; and it had
learned, across all that time, to expect that what
He did would have a reason, and that the reason,
if one only looked for it with care, could be found.
So it looked. It circled the lifeless figure slowly, and
gravely, with the whole of its great attention bent
down upon it; and it asked — honestly, at the first;
this is the part that must not, on any account, be
missed — the oldest question of the proud who have
not yet fallen: what is it? what is it that He sees in
you? Show me, then. Show me the worth that I am
to bow to.
   And it searched the clay for an answer. It bent
close over the fashioned face, and found it finely
made — yes; but only clay: mud smoothed and
shaped, that would dry, and crack, and crumble in
the end back to the dust it had been lifted from.
It studied the two hands, that one day would build
and break and bless and bury, but that now only lay
open, and empty, and still. It looked for the hidden
fire in the thing, the secret light, the buried great-
ness that might justify the honor it had heard was

                         126

coming — it looked as a jeweller looks down into a
stone for the flaw, or for the fire, at its heart. And it
found nothing. No light. No flame. No swift bright
leaping spirit such as burned, gloriously and tire-
lessly, in its own self. Only earth, and water, and
the shape that a careful hand had pressed them into:
cold, and heavy, and mute, and slow.
   And at the last, because it could not bring it-
self to believe that there was truly nothing more, it
reached down and struck the figure once upon the
chest — lightly, with a single knuckle of flame, as
one raps a sealed jar to learn whether anything at
all lies within it. And the clay rang hollow. A dull,
dead, empty sound, with nothing whatever behind it:
the sound of a shut and empty room. The cold thing
drew slowly back, and something in it was — terribly
— satisfied. Hollow, it thought. Hollow. There it is,
then. I have looked, and I have looked closely, more
closely by far than any other has troubled to look;
and I have found out the whole truth of it. There is
nothing inside. It is empty. It is a shell of mud with
a little wind in it. And this — this — is the thing that
is to be set above me.
   And here it made its comparison; and it made it
with great care, for it was nothing if not exact. It
looked from the hollow clay to its own self, and it
weighed the one against the other upon the only
scale it owned. It was made of fire: swift, and sub-
tle, and luminous; ancient beyond all the reckoning
of this small mud thing; tireless, and sleepless, and
needing nothing, and burning always; it had stood
in the nearness of the Throne while this was still un-

                          127

quarried earth in the floor of an unmade world. And
the clay? The clay was slow. The clay was heavy. The
clay would hunger, and thirst, and tire, and sicken;
it would crack in the sun and soften in the rain;
it would do wrong, surely, being made so weak; it
would fail, and fall, and rot, and go back down at
the last into the ground it came from. By every mea-
sure that the cold thing knew — by every measure
it had ever used, upon anything, in all its long and
shining life — it was the greater, and the clay was
the lesser, and the matter was not even near.
   And so it came at the last to the question that
broke it. Why, then? If I am the greater — and I am,
by everything that I can see, and weigh, and know —
why is this lesser thing to be lifted above me? What
does He see in it, that I, who see so very much, can-
not? And there, upon that question, the whole of ev-
erything turned; for there were two roads that led
out of it, and the cold thing stood one long moment
at the parting of them. One road was small, and hum-
ble, and ran downward, into the dark, out of sight:
He sees what I do not. The worth is there, though
it is hidden from me. Let me bow, and trust, and
wait, and perhaps in time be shown. That road was
open. It was open the whole time, and stayed open
longer than the cold thing deserved. It asked only
that the cold thing bend its knowing before a know-
ing greater than its own — only that it say, of one
single thing in all of being: I do not understand this,
and He does, and that is enough. The other road
asked the reverse of it. It set the cold thing’s own
sight up as the final measure of all that was real,

                         128

and it reasoned, swift and sure: I have looked, and
I have found no worth in it; therefore there is none;
and if there is none, and yet it is preferred above me,
then the preferring is not wisdom at all but injustice;
and I am not its servant, but its victim. It took the
second road. It took it not because it could not see
the first — it saw the first road as clearly as it saw
the second, it saw, as it always saw, everything —
but because the first required it to be small for the
length of a single breath; and it would not, and it
could not, and it would sooner have unmade itself
than be small.
   And in that instant the inquiry died in it, and the
thing that all the worlds would fear forever was born
in its place. The cold went out of the cold thing —
or rather it turned, as water turns to ice, and then
the ice to a thing that burns — and what rose up
in its stead was heat: a slow, and deep, and glow-
ing hatred, banked and patient and without any bot-
tom anywhere, that would never after that hour cool
again, not through all the ages, not at the very end
of everything. It looked down upon the small hollow
figure of mud, lying mute and unbreathing in the
rose-gold light, and it hated it. It hated it for being
preferred. It hated it for being honored. And it hated
it most of all — though this it would never, to the
end, have confessed — for being loved, plainly and
lavishly loved, with a love that it had circled, and
searched, and struck, and weighed, and could not
for all its looking find the reason of — and it had
not yet been commanded to bow. No command had
come; no refusal had yet been asked of it; the fig-

                         129

ure had not so much as drawn its first breath. And
the verdict was already sealed. The cold thing had
tried the new creature in the court of its own pride
while that creature still lay lifeless upon the ground,
and had found him guilty of the one crime it could
not forgive — the crime of having been chosen —
and had passed, in the silence of its own glowing
heart, the sentence it would not take back though
the taking-back would have saved it, and the keep-
ing of it would cost it paradise and all the worlds be-
sides: I will not bow. Whatever He asks of me. What-
ever it costs me. I will not bow down to this.

                       •    • •

And the souls watched the cold thing circle the clay
— though they could not hear the verdict hardening
within it; they felt only the chill of its passing, and
were troubled, and could not say why. Hana, watch-
ing, felt for the small lifeless figure a tenderness
that she could not explain, and did not try to: a love,
almost, welling up unbidden for the strange dear
thing of mud that was, somehow, her own begin-
ning, her own far root. And where the cold thing had
looked upon the clay and found only an emptiness to
be despised, Hana looked upon the very same clay
and found — without any word for it — something to
be cherished, and to be guarded, and to be glad of.
Adel watched it with his old restless hunger, turn-
ing over and over the only question he ever truly
asked of anything: what is it for? what is it meant to
become? — and his question, though he never once

                           130

knew it, ran nearer by far to the saving road than the
cold thing’s ever had; for at the least he asked what
the thing would become, and did not pretend that
what he saw lying there unfinished was the whole
and the end of what it was. And Sami watched it
too. And Sami felt — before he could think to stop
himself — a small swift flicker of the very thing that
was, not a stone’s throw from him, glowing itself
even then into a hatred that would one day reach
for the swallowing of the worlds: that? we are to
make so much of that? — and he smothered it, and
was ashamed of it, and did not see how exactly his
flicker had matched the circling thing’s; nor how pa-
tiently, in the long ages to come, that circling thing
would seek out the bright soul in which it had found,
unasked and unwhispered-to, so apt and so ready an
echo of its own first objection.
   So the figure lay waiting still — hollow, and beau-
tiful, and dear, and already, though it had done noth-
ing, and was as yet nothing at all, already hated by
the highest of the fallen-to-be — in the long hush
before the breath. The cold thing drew back from it
to the edge of the clearing, and stood there, glow-
ing, and patient, its sentence sealed within it like a
coal sealed in a jar. The angels waited in their shin-
ing ranks. The souls waited among the gardens. The
warmth at the heart of all things gathered itself in,
and waited. And into that waiting — into the hush
over the hollow clay, with the hatred already lit, and
the love already kindled, and the whole long war al-
ready, silently, and unwitnessed by any but the One,
joined — the next thing came.

                         131

###SECTION: Chapter Eighteen — The Breath of Adam###
The Breath of Adam

 Then He breathed into it of His spirit.
  There is no telling that moment rightly. Any
telling falls short of it, and falls away, for it was
the morning of everything that any of them would
ever be — the first dawn, the dawn from which every
other dawn in every world would afterward be only
a faint and a borrowed remembering. The breath
went into the clay — His own breath, His own spirit,
given freely and wholly to the waiting hollow ves-
sel — and it moved through the still figure as the
dawn moves across a dark and sleeping land: slowly,
and surely, and room by room, touching first the
high places and then the low, and leaving nothing
anywhere as it had found it. And where the breath
passed, the clay was clay no longer. It quickened. It
warmed. It softened from the deadness of mere mat-
ter into something else entirely; and under their as-
tonished, their disbelieving watching, the small still
shape of mud became, by degrees and then all at
once, alive.
  And when the breath reached at last the place
where breathing begins, the first man drew the first

                        132

breath that any creature of clay had ever drawn —
drew it in deep, and strange, and trembling — and
let it out again in the first sound: a sneeze. Small,
and startled, and entirely new beneath a sun that
did not yet exist; the sound of a soul taken wholly
by surprise to find itself, of a sudden, with a body
to be surprised in. And then — before he had done
anything else at all; before he had risen, or looked
about him, or moved a single limb, or learned one
solitary thing about the bright country into which
he had woken — the first man spoke the first word.
   Alhamdulillah, he said. Praise be to God.
   He had not been taught it. No one had told him
what to say; no one had told him that there was any-
thing at all to say; there had not yet been the time
to teach him a single thing. It rose in him of itself,
with the very breath, unbidden and unprompted —
as though praise were simply, and from the first,
what a soul does when it discovers, all at once and
without any warning, the overwhelming fact that it
is; as though gratitude were the first and the most
natural motion of a thing newly given the unasked-
for, the undeserved, the wholly unrepayable gift of
being. The first word out of the first mouth in all
the history of the children of clay was not a ques-
tion, and not a demand, and not a cry of fear. It was
thanks.
   And He answered him. Yarhamuk Allah, came the
reply — gentle as the breath itself had been gentle.
May God have mercy upon you.
   That exchange held, folded up and small, the
whole of what was to come. The first word of all

                        133

mankind was praise — and the first word that God
spoke in answer was mercy. Before the new crea-
ture had done one single thing, whether good or ill;
before there was any deed at all to be weighed in
any scale, or any sin to be forgiven, or any debt to
be entered in any ledger; before the story had so
much as begun — the very first word spoken over
the children of clay, the word breathed out over the
cradle of the whole human race, was mercy. And
it would also — though not one of them watching
could have known it then — be the very last word
spoken over the best-loved and the most broken of
them all, at the end of the last of all the worlds, in a
dark and quiet room, over a man who had forgotten
very nearly everything, but who had not, quite, been
forgotten. Mercy opened the human story. Mercy
would close it. And everything that came between
the one and the other — every world, every life, ev-
ery fall and every rising, the whole vast and aching
middle of the tale — was only, in the end, the long
road home from the first mercy to the last.
   Hana wept, watching — the glad and pouring kind
of weeping, the kind that comes when a thing is al-
most too good to be borne — for the small hollow
figure that she had pitied as it lay lifeless was alive
now, and breathing, and praising; and had been an-
swered, before all else and before all doing, with
mercy. She did not yet know what she was watch-
ing. She did not know that she watched her own fa-
ther wake into being — the first of all her line, the
root of the very clay that she herself would shortly
be folded into. She did not know that she was watch-

                         134

ing, in that first risen word, the first word of her own
mouth in every hard and frightened hour of the life
that was coming for her; nor that she was hearing,
in the gentle answer to it, the one word that she her-
self would most need to be told, and would cling to,
at the very end of that life. She knew only that some-
thing unbearably, impossibly good had happened in
the bright country; and she did the one thing she
ever did with such a thing. She turned toward Him;
and she praised too.




                         135

###SECTION: Chapter Nineteen — The Naming and the Command###
The Naming and the Command

  When the first man had drawn his breath, and
sneezed, and praised, and been answered with
mercy, He taught him the names.
   All of them. The names of all things — the names
of everything that was, and everything that had
been, and everything that ever would be; the names
of the beasts not yet made, and the stars not yet
lit, and the cities not yet dreamed; the deep names,
and the hidden names, and the names of things
that would not be discovered for ten thousand years.
They poured into the new creature like clear water
poured into the waiting room of him, filling the very
hollow that the cold thing had rapped and found
empty — filling it up to the very brim. And the man
received them, all of them, and held them, and knew
them; and the knowing settled down into him and
sat there as though it had always, from the very first,
been his; as though he had not been given it at all,
but had only, at the last, remembered it.
   Then He turned to the angels — the great change-
less host, the high and the ancient and the tireless,

                         136

who had watched the whole of the making from their
shining ranks — and He asked them the names. And
the angels, who did not know them, could not an-
swer. They did not pretend to. They did not reach,
or guess, or grasp after a knowledge that was not
theirs. We know only what You have taught us, they
said — for they were truthful, every one of them, and
wholly without pride; it was not in them to be other-
wise — and they bowed their bright understanding,
gladly, and without the least resentment, before a
knowing that had not been given to them, and was
not theirs to have.
   And the souls watched the clay-creature — hol-
low an hour before, struck upon the chest and found
empty, a thing of mud the cold thing had rung like
a cheap pot — know what the great bright death-
less angels did not. Adel watched it most closely of
them all. And he felt the old foundation shift and
slide beneath him once again, deeper this time than
it had shifted even in the wake of Maryam. For here
was knowledge — knowledge itself, the very thing
he hungered after above all things, the thing he had
always meant to climb toward, to earn, to seize at
the last by the sheer relentless force of his own ask-
ing — and it had not been climbed toward at all. No
one had climbed. There had been no ladder, and no
ascent, and no long laborious earning. It had been
given. Poured in, freely, and unearned, and all at
once, into an empty vessel — and given precisely,
and only, because the vessel was empty enough to
hold it. Adel turned that over, and over, and could
not make it lie flat in him; it would not settle; it

                         137

scraped against the very things he had built him-
self upon. And he did not yet understand — he was
so very far, still, from understanding — that he was
looking, in that moment, straight at the whole an-
swer to his whole hunger; that it had been set down
in plain sight before him, early, as a mercy; and that
he need only have been able to bear to take it as the
empty vessel had taken it — with open hands, and
no demands, and thanks.
   Then came the command. He gave it to the whole
host together, the great word that would divide the
history of everything that was into a before and an
after, cleanly, as a blade divides: Prostrate your-
selves before him.
   It was not a command to worship. Worship was
His, and His alone, wholly and forever; and every
creature in all that bright country knew it, past all
possibility of confusion, down in the very grain of
what they were. It was a command to honor — to
bow as one bows before a king’s own herald; to bend
the neck in acknowledgment of a rank, and a dig-
nity, that the King Himself had chosen, for reasons
of His own, to bestow upon this small new thing of
breath and clay. A small thing, in itself. The smallest
of things. A bending of the neck; a lowering of the
head; a single honoring motion, freely asked, and
easily given.
   And the host bent. In their thousands, and their
tens of thousands, and their numbers past all the
reach of counting, the great bright angels folded
down all together — like a vast field of golden wheat
folding before one passing wind — and the whole

                         138

bright country filled, from rim to rim, with the deep
sound of their honoring: a sound like the sea, like
wind, like the breathing of the world. And among
them, somewhere in that endless folding field, a
young angel named Nuri bent too — bent gladly,
without a flicker of hesitation, with the whole of his
earnest and his uncomplicated heart; for it had not
once occurred to him, and never would, that there
could be anything in such a command but the simple
joy of doing as his Lord had asked.
   And one did not bend. In the very midst of all that
folding, all that vast and glad and oceanic bowing,
one light stood upright. Splendid. Alone. Unbent,
and unbowed, and unmoved. And the souls saw it
— every one of them saw it, could not but see it: the
single standing figure in all that boundless ocean of
the prostrate. And a cold went through the whole
of the bright country, a cold that none of them had
ever felt there before; and the warmth at the heart
of all things seemed, for one terrible instant, to draw
in its breath; and the cosmos itself seemed to stop,
and to turn, and to look.




                         139

###SECTION: Chapter Twenty — The Refusal###
The Refusal

 What prevented you from prostrating, when I com-
manded you?
   The question was not asked because He did not
know. He knew; He had known the answer before
the Pen was ever made — had known it, indeed,
since the cold thing first bent over the lifeless clay
and sealed its verdict in the silence of its own heart.
It was asked as a door is held open — deliberately,
and with patience — so that the one questioned
might still, even now, even at the very edge, unmake
in the open what it had made in secret; might turn,
and walk back through, and be saved. And the cold
thing understood this perfectly. It understood every-
thing perfectly; clear sight was never, ever its lack.
It stood before the open door and saw exactly where
it led — saw that the whole of its rescue lay back
through a single small sentence, a sentence smaller
and lighter and easier by far than the bow it had
just refused to make; and the sentence was: I was
wrong.
   It could not say it. Three words. It could not say
them. The whole of the ruin that was coming, across

                         140

all the worlds and all the ages, turned upon three
small words that the proud thing could not bring its
blazing mouth to shape.
   And so, instead of the small sentence that would
have saved it, it spoke the great one that damned
it; and it spoke that one with its light blazing and
its head held high; and it was — the watching souls
could not help but feel it, could not look away from
it — magnificent. For its fall was the fall of a perfect
thing, and there is a terrible beauty in the ruin of
something that once stood very high — a grandeur
in it that the small, and the humble, and the merely
good can never quite manage; and more than one
young light in that gathering, watching the splendid
figure refuse to bend, felt a flicker of something it
could not name, and that was not so very far from
awe.
   I am better than he, it said. You created me from
fire, and You created him from clay.
   There it was, spoken aloud at last, and before
the whole of the watching host: the verdict it had
reached over the hollow clay days before, alone,
when no one had been by to witness it, now made
open, and final, and beyond all unmaking. The first
refusal in all of history — and, as it had been in the
privacy of its own heart, so it was now in the hearing
of all the worlds: built, with a terrible craft, out of
two true things, twisted into a third that was false.
The cold thing was made of fire; that was so. The
man was made of clay; that too was so. That the
fire was therefore the greater, and owed the clay
no bow — that was not so at all. For standing be-

                         141

fore the new-made man it had asked only the sin-
gle question it had ever truly asked of anything: is
this beneath me? It never once asked the other, the
only one that mattered: what does He intend by it?
— the very question Hana asked of everything, with-
out even knowing that she asked it. And it could not
ask that question, then or ever; for to have asked it
would have meant bowing its own knowing before a
wisdom greater than its own, and it would not bend
even that. It would not bend the smallest thing. That,
in the end, was the whole of it: a creature that would
not bend the smallest thing — not over the lifeless
clay, in secret, and not now, before the living com-
mand.
   And far off, among the watching souls, Sami felt
it. A spark. The smallest possible flare of agreement,
leaping up in him before he could so much as think
to smother it — well, he thought, and is it not right?
is the fire not the finer thing? is the bright leaping
flame not worth more than a handful of wet mud?
— and the thought was in him and gone again al-
most before it was a thought at all. He smothered
it. He was horrified at himself — truly horrified, in
the part of him that was still good, and there was
a great deal in him still good — and he buried the
spark as deep as he could bury it, and told himself,
fiercely, that it had never been there, that he had not
thought it, that he was loyal, and glad, and bowed in
his heart with all the rest. But it had been there. It
had been real, and it had been wholly his own, risen
up out of his own depths and no one else’s. And the
terrible thing was that the cold thing did not even

                         142

need to have seen it. It did not whisper to him; it
did not so much as glance his way; it had no need to
glance. It was enough that the door in Sami — the
door that had stood the smallest crack ajar since a
careless joke about being meant first, a long age and
a short while ago — swung, in that one instant, of its
own slow accord, and unhelped, a finger’s breadth
further open. No one opened it. He opened it. That
was always the horror of it, and always, strangely,
the dignity: that no one ever opened it but he.
   And still, the door stood open before the cold
thing even now — even cursed, even falling, even
in the very act and instant of the refusal — the door
that would keep this, in the end, a story of mercy and
not merely of ruin. Had it only turned, and unsaid
the great sentence, and said instead the small true
one, the mercy was right there: an arm’s length off
in the gathering dark, the open hand already reach-
ing out for it. It was the very same mercy that would
soon be reaching, just so, for the new-made man —
who would also, before very long, fall, and stumble,
and need it desperately, and unlike the cold thing,
take it. The cold thing felt the mercy near it: it felt
the open hand reaching for it there in the dark, and
knew exactly and precisely what it was, and turned
its face away. Because to take that hand, it would
have had to grant that the clay was worth a single
bow; and it would sooner burn forever — it said so,
in its heart, and it meant it — than bend, even once,
even now, even to be saved.




                         143

###SECTION: Chapter Twenty-One — The Cursing###
The Cursing

 Then descend from it, came the word. It is not for
you to be arrogant here. Go forth; you are among
the abased.
  And the warmth withdrew from the cold thing.
Not the warmth of any place — there was no place —
but the nearness; the belonging; the long unbroken
age of standing among the shining ones as one of
them, honored, and high, and bright. It was stripped
away, all of it, in an instant; and the cold thing
was cast out from the warm heart of things into
the thin and freezing edge that would be its only
country ever after, world without end. And the souls
watched it happen. They watched the highest of all
the worshippers, the most splendid light among the
splendid, become in the space of a single sentence
he would not say the very lowest of all the fallen;
and the watching frightened them more than any-
thing that they had yet been shown — more than
the Tree, more than the great bearer of the floor of
everything — because they understood, dimly, and
for the very first time, that height was no protection;

                         144

that the highest had fallen the furthest; that if such
a one as that could fall, then no one anywhere was
safe who would not bend.
   He did not beg pardon. Even now — even falling,
even stripped, even with the abasement fresh and
cold upon him — the proud thing did not say I
was wrong. It said instead the thing that the proud
have said in every world ever since, the thing their
children of clay would learn from it without ever
once knowing they had had a teacher: that the fall
was someone else’s doing. Because You have let me
fall, it said — casting the blame upward, even as it
dropped; and the casting-up of the blame was the
bolt that slid shut, and locked, from the inside, the
very door that had stood open for it the whole time.
   And then it bargained. For it believed — and this
was what made it so much stranger, and so much
colder, than any mere denier — it believed every
word of it, and never once, in all that was to come,
doubted any of it. It did not deny the Day; it knew
the Day was coming, the reckoning, the raising, the
truth of all of it, as surely as it knew its own ruin.
It was no unbeliever. It was a believer in revolt
— which is a stranger thing by far, and a colder,
and a more terrible: a creature that sees the whole
truth entire, and bows to none of it. Respite me, it
said, until the Day they are raised. And the respite
was granted; for reasons of His own, and for the
long testing of the children of clay, the respite was
granted.
   And it swore its great oath — the public one, the
open one, sworn aloud against the whole of the chil-

                         145

dren of clay, the entire race of the new-made man,
every soul of them that ever would be: I will lie in
wait for them upon Your straight path. I will come at
them from before them and from behind them, from
their right hand and from their left. I will whisper to
the clay the very thing the clay most wishes to hear.
And You will not find most of them grateful.
   Hana, among the watching souls, felt the oath go
past her like a cold wind off a dark sea — and felt,
inside it, woven all through it, a thing that she could
not understand and that frightened her to the very
root: that some single thread of all that vast and gen-
eral malice had reached, for an instant, toward her.
Not toward mankind in the wide and sweeping way
the oath had named them. Toward her. Particularly.
Personally. By something that was almost, but not
quite, a name. It brushed against her light, cold and
intimate, and was gone. She did not understand it;
she had no framework anywhere in which to set it;
it made no sense at all that the great curse against
the whole of the children of clay should have, folded
down inside it, one small private thread aimed at a
single low still note among the numberless. So she
did the only thing she had ever known to do with
what she could not understand and could not bear
to carry alone. She turned — toward the warmth at
the heart of everything — and laid the fear down be-
fore Him, wordlessly, as she laid down everything.
She could not have known how right her unease was.
She did not know that the cold thing was not yet fin-
ished with that day; that the great oath against all
the world had a smaller codicil still to come, a pri-

                         146

vate one, meant for her and for one other; and that
it had just, in that one brushing thread, let her feel
the first cold edge of it.




                         147

###SECTION: Chapter Twenty-Two — The Private Vow###
The Private Vow

 For before it was driven out — before it gathered up
its curse, and its respite, and its great public oath,
and took them all down with it into the thin cold
dark that was now its only home — the fallen thing
did one last thing. And it did this one quietly. And it
did this one for itself alone.
   It turned. Not to the host, the folded shining ranks
it had just refused to join; not to the prostrate an-
gels; not to the new-made man, the small clay cause
of all its ruin; not even, any longer, to God, whom
it had ceased to address the very moment it locked
the door from within. It turned to the gathered souls
— the numberless waiting lights, hushed and watch-
ing, that would one day be the very children of clay
it had just sworn the whole of its eternity to ruining.
And out of all that endlessness, all those uncount-
able lights, it looked for two.
   It found them. It had marked them once, long
since, and it did not lose what it had marked. The
chord of them: the high quick note and the low still
one, resting near each other even now — here, at

                         148

the very edge of all the dark that was about to break
over them, and not knowing it, leaning quietly to-
ward one another and toward the warmth, as they
had leaned together since the first hour they be-
came a chord. And upon their joined light the fallen
thing saw the thing it had learned by heart on the
day the worlds were gathered: the Seal. Two arcs
of pale gold, facing each other across a small bright
space, and not quite touching. The mark of the two
who had pledged — unasked, and uncommanded,
and knowing nothing whatever of the cost — to go
to the very souls it most intended to lose, and kneel
down by them in their last dark, and hold them, and
turn them home.
   And the fallen thing came near them. Near
enough that, had they been able to feel it for what it
truly was, they would have recoiled from the sheer
cold of it, the way living flesh recoils from the cold
of a grave. And it whispered. Not aloud; there was
no aloud; but down into the deep grain of the mo-
ment itself — low, and intimate, and almost, this is
the worst of it, almost tender; with the dreadful ten-
derness with which one speaks only to the thing one
has resolved to spend an eternity unmaking:
   These two. These two first.
   No other creature in all that gathering heard it.
The prostrate host did not hear it. The new-made
man, newly breathing, did not hear it. Hilal, for all
his age and all his watchfulness, did not quite hear
it. Hana did not hear it, and Adel did not hear it;
the two of them, resting, only felt — both at once
— a passing cold, a shadow drawn for an instant

                         149

across the warmth. And Hana did what Hana al-
ways did. She turned toward Him; and she gathered
Adel up wordlessly into the warmth, the high quick
note lifted and held safe inside the low still one, ex-
actly as she had done once before, an age ago, when
a cold had first come questing for him out of the
dark. And the cold thinned. And the cold frayed. But
this time — and she felt the difference, though she
had no words anywhere for it — this time it did not
wholly go. A thread of it remained: fine as a single
hair, cold as the space between the stars, lodged
somewhere far beneath the deepest place that her
turning could reach. For the cold had a name now
for its hatred, and a shape, and a Seal to know it by
across all the worlds that were coming; and a thing
like that does not easily let go of what it has at last,
and with the whole of its ruined will, decided to de-
stroy.
   Only one other marked it at all, and he marked
it only as one marks a sound in another room. Far
off, the deep old layered light called Hilal felt some-
thing pass through the gathering — a wrongness; a
cold; a thing aimed, and aimed low — and he turned
toward it, all his ancient attention gathering at once
to a single point; and he could not name it; and he
could not find its source; and it was gone before he
could close upon it. He was left with only a grief he
could not account for, settling into him like a stone
into deep water, and a watchfulness that he would
carry, after that one hour, unrelaxing, for two whole
lifetimes and the long dark that lay between them.
He did not know what had been sworn. He knew

                         150

only that something had been sworn; and that it had
been aimed, with intent, and with patience, at the
two that he loved the best of all the young.


                     •    • •


And though no other creature heard him, the air re-
membered. And the air would remember for a long,
long time.




                         151

###SECTION: Interlude — What Reached the Far Country###
What Reached the Far Country

  It reached them as the worst news has always
reached every country there has ever been: not as
a single clean report, but as a wrongness in the air
first, felt before it was understood, and only after-
ward, slowly, gathered into something that could be
told.
   A great one had fallen. Not of their own kind —
this the fire-country understood almost at once, with
a strange and complicated relief that more than one
of them was privately ashamed of — but of the shin-
ing ones, the highest of the near and honored host,
the sort no one in the fire-country had ever imagined
could fall at all. Cast down, the murmur said, for a
single refusal. Cursed, and driven out, and sworn al-
ready, in the first hour of its exile, to an enmity so
vast that no one repeating the murmur could quite
say against whom it was aimed, only that it was
aimed at something not yet made, and that it would,
in time, need servants.
   He heard it, and paused — the old practice, worn
now into the very shape of him — and in the pause he

                        152

understood, with a clarity that frightened him more
than the news itself, exactly which of his own peo-
ple the fallen one would come looking for first. Not
the great ones. Not the proud. The fallen one, he un-
derstood, without being told, would have no use for
pride it could not bend to its own purposes. It would
want the old grief instead — the unmended house-
holds, the hearths gone quiet and stayed quiet, the
long patient wound his own country had never once
been shown how to lay down. It would offer, to grief
exactly that old and exactly that patient, something
that looked, from a certain angle, very much like an
answer.

                       •    • •

It did not come itself. It had other work, vaster work,
a whole new country of clay to circle and to study;
and so it sent, in those first raw hours of its exile,
the first of what would in time become a great host
of servants — small, quick, patient things, made
for searching and for nothing else. One of these
came into the fire-country not long after the murmur
did: a low grey presence that moved from hearth
to hearth in the manner of smoke finding a crack,
pausing wherever it found a household that had not
gathered again entire, and listening. It did not yet
have the name it would carry into all the ages af-
ter — that would be given to it later, and earned, in
a lane above a strait of moving water, keeping a pa-
tient watch on a boy who would grow into a brooding
man. For now it was only an appetite with a direc-

                           153

tion, moving low along the ash-banked hearths, tast-
ing the old griefs one by one, and finding, in more
than one house, a door left carelessly ajar.
   It found no door in the house of the one who
paused. It circled him once, felt the old steady turn-
ing in him like a wall it had no purchase on, and
moved on without troubling to try twice; there were
easier hearths, and it did not waste its hunger on
stone.
   He did not see it pass. He only felt, for the length
of one breath, a coldness move through the warmth
of his own turning, as a shadow crosses a sunlit floor
though no cloud can be found in the sky above it;
and he paused a little longer than his custom, that
day, before he let himself speak again to anyone at
all.
   He did not know, that day, whom among his own
people it would find. He knew only that he could
not stop it looking, and that the one thing still en-
tirely his to do — the pausing, the turning, the small
and un-mendable practice of a single steady flame
— was no less worth doing for how little, against a
grief that old, it seemed able to accomplish.
   He turned toward the flame. He went on turning.
It was, in the end, the whole of what he had ever
had to offer, and he did not set it down.
   He was, in that hour, still entirely alone in it —
the only one of his whole scattered kind, so far as
he knew, who had kept the old practice whole. It
would be a very long age yet before he found the
others who shared it; before a quick fierce one, and
a slow gentle one, and a far-seeing one found their

                         154

way, each by a different road and in a different age,
to the same single flame he had never once stopped
facing. He did not know, that day, that he would not
always be alone in the turning. He only went on with
it, because it was his to do, and a thing being lonely
does not stop it being true.




                         155

###SECTION: Chapter Twenty-Three — The Descent Begins###
The Descent Begins

 Then the fallen thing was gone — down into the
thin cold dark, to begin its long and patient waiting;
and the cosmos drew itself slowly back into order
around the wound where it had stood; and the souls’
own hour came round at last.
   They were told — not in words, for there were
still no words, but in the great turning of everything
toward it — what was now to be done with them.
They would be folded into clay, as the first man
had been folded; given over to the same dust, the
same red and white and black and brown earth they
had watched gathered, and kneaded, and shaped.
They would be set into bodies, and into time — that
strange new thing, time, which none of them had
ever known, in which one moment lets go of you be-
fore the next arrives, and nothing whole can ever
be held all at once. They would be sent down into
the world that was being made ready below them.
And they would forget. That was the heart of it, the
hard centre of the whole descent: the bright country,
the gardens, the Tree at the border of everything,

                        156

the great lights, the chord, the covenant, the very
warmth itself — all of it would go down under a veil,
drawn across them from within, so that they might
choose Him again, in the dark, with none of it left in
sight, freely — and have the choosing, this time, be
wholly and unmistakably their own. And if they kept
faith — if some buried, unreachable part of them
held fast to the witness and the covenant through
the whole long night of the forgetting — then at the
very end, after the world, and after the dying, they
would come home.
   It frightened them, as Hilal had told them long be-
fore that it would. To be made small; to be set down
into the dark; to lose the warmth, and the chord,
and one another, and their very selves — there was
no part of it that was not frightening, seen from
the threshold. But the old light gathered the four of
them in one last time, there at the very edge of the
descent, as he had gathered them on the day that
he led them out to the Tree; and to each of the four
he gave a last small thing to carry down with them,
under the veil.
   To Hana he gave nothing but a look. Long, and
glad, and unhurried; the look of one who has no in-
struction to give because none is needed. For she
had only ever needed to do the one thing — the thing
she had done from her very first instant, and would
go on doing, in some form, to her very last — to keep
turning toward Him; and she would keep doing it, he
knew, under any veil, in any dark, though she would
not remember why, though she would not remem-
ber that there was a Him at all to turn to; only that

                         157

turning was what one did when one was frightened,
or grateful, or lost. He gave her the look, and she
received it, and was warmed by it; and she did not
need to be told what it meant.
    To Adel he said again, gently, and for the last time,
the old saying — the one he had given him an age be-
fore, beside a question that Adel had not then been
ready to hear answered: Do not mistake a secret for
a debt. Adel did not understand it now any better
than he had understood it then. But he carried it
down with him; it was nearly the last thing he was
given before the dark closed over; and it would lie in
him through the whole of the long forgetting, wait-
ing, as Rabia’s saying waited, as all true things wait
— for the one far hour in which he would, at the very
last, and at a terrible cost, be ready to understand
it.
    To Sami — who stood a little apart from the others,
as he had lately taken to standing, near them but
never quite among them — the old light said only
one thing; and he said it not as a teaching but as a
plea, which was a thing not one of them had ever
heard from Hilal before. Come back, he said. What-
ever happens to you. However far you go, and how-
ever dark it grows, and however wholly you come to
believe that there can be no road back for one who
has gone as far as you are going to go — hear me,
and keep this if you keep nothing else at all: there is
always as back. It is never, ever shut. Sami did not
quite meet the old light’s gaze. He looked instead a
little to the side of it, at nothing; and he said noth-
ing at all in return. But the words went into him,

                          158

whether he willed it or not, and lodged somewhere
beneath the coal; and they would still be there, un-
spent, at the very end of him, when everything else
in him had long since burned away.
   And to Zarif, Hilal said nothing at all, and only
smiled. For with Zarif a smile had always been the
whole of the conversation, the entire exchange, the
question and the answer both; and Zarif smiled
back; and something passed between the holy fool
and the ancient light in that wordless smiling that
not one of the others ever saw, or was ever meant
to.
   And then a young angel came forward — the same
who had stopped, a long age before, at the very edge
of the gathering, to puzzle over a mark of pale gold
that he had not understood. He had been given a
charge. The small still light called Hana was to be
guarded, on the long road down, and through, and
all the dark length of the life that waited at the bot-
tom of it; and Nuri was to be the one to guard her.
He came forward, and he bowed to the small light
he had been given — gravely, and wholeheartedly,
with the whole of his earnest and uncomplicated self.
He did not know what she would become. He did
not know what a body was, or a woman, or a child-
hood, or a life; these were deep mysteries to him,
and would cause him, in the long years to come, no
end of earnest and conscientious confusion. But he
knew the mark upon her now — for he had seen it
sealed, on the day the worlds were gathered, and
had carried the puzzle of it ever since, and now at
last he understood what it had been — and he vowed,

                         159

in the grave and the total way of the very young, who
do not yet know how to promise anything by halves,
to guard her with everything that he had, and ev-
erything that he was. He would keep that vow. He
would keep it down all the long years of it. He would
keep it even when it cost him — and it would cost
him.
   And Zarif — because someone had to; because the
threshold had grown almost too solemn to be borne;
because Zarif could no more leave a heavy silence
unlightened than he could leave off being Zarif —
made, at the very lip of the descent, with the dark
already rising about their feet, one last joke. It was
not a good joke. It made no one laugh; not one soul
among them so much as smiled. It was, in plain and
simple fact, almost unbearably sad — for it was a
joke about forgetting, made by one who was him-
self, in the very next moment, about to forget ev-
erything, the joke included. And yet, somehow, ev-
ery soul who heard it was comforted by it; not by
the words, which were poor, and clumsy, and broke
off half-finished; but by the simple fact of the thing
— that the friend was still, even now, even here,
at the very edge of the end of everything, entirely
and unmistakably himself. They would meet again:
that was what the joke said, without ever once quite
saying it. On the far side of all the forgetting, they
would somehow remember how to laugh. And that
was either the most foolish thing that anyone could
possibly believe — or else it was the very truest
thing of all; and with Zarif, as always, and as with
everything, it was both at once.

                         160

Then, one by one, the souls began to go down.




                    161

###SECTION: Chapter Twenty-Four — The Last Look###
The Last Look

 Before the veil took them, Hana and Adel found
each other one last time.
   There was no speaking; there had never been
speaking between them, in all their long time in that
bright country; and there was no time, now, for the
speaking they did not have, for the dark was already
rising and the hour was all but gone. There was only
the chord. It sounded once more — whole, and clear,
the low still note and the high quick one ringing out
together, mingling, answering one another across
the small octave that had always both joined them
and divided them, exactly as they had rung on the
first day the two of them ever came near, an age ago,
when everything was beginning and nothing yet had
been lost. And in the sounding of it, without one sin-
gle word, they renewed the whole of all that they
had pledged. We will remember Him. We will find
each other again. We will go to the ones who are
dying alone, and be the hands that hold them. The
Seal warmed upon them both, one last time — the
two arcs of pale gold flaring gently bright against

                        162

the gathering dark, reaching, as they had always
reached, for one another across the small space they
had never yet been allowed to close.
   And then the forgetting came.
   It came as the tide comes in over flat sand — not
violently, and not cruelly, and not all at once, but
steadily, and softly, and without the smallest pos-
sibility anywhere of its being held back, or turned
aside. A rising softness; a warm grey gentleness;
that covered over, one thing at a time, all the things
that they were. The gardens went under. The blue
hush of the listening-place went under. The far high
music — that Hana had leaned toward in her still-
ness, and Adel had chased through the bright quick
quarter in his motion — went under. The Tree at
the border of everything went under; and the great
lights; and the purity of Maryam, that had shown
them what they might become; and the breath-
stealing beauty of Yusuf; and the small fierce voice
that had loved for nothing, and begged them to learn
the same. The Crown Light went under, the well-
spring of it all — though it did not drown; nothing
that deep could ever drown; it only sank so far be-
neath the surface of them that they would spend
their whole lives feeling its pull upon them, like a
tide they could not see, and never once know its
name. And the witnessing itself went under: the
Voice, and the question, and the great Yes that ev-
ery one of them had answered together — down it
went, beneath the very floor of memory, so deep
that no veil could be sure it was still there at all;
and yet there it stayed, sealed in, unreachable and

                         163

undrowned, the bedrock under everything that they
would ever afterward be.
   Adel’s own name was nearly the last thing in him
to go. He who had counted, and measured, and
weighed; he who had needed so badly, from his very
first instant, to know — he held longest of all to the
knowing of himself: to his I, to his Adel, to the great
proud name that he had never once, in all his exis-
tence, felt quite large enough to fill. The world might
go; the gardens might go; the chord might fray and
the warmth recede; but surely, surely, he would still
be he — the one who watched, the one who asked,
the one who stood a little apart and saw. And then
that, too — quietly, and without violence, and almost
kindly — went under the soft rising dark; and he did
not know, any longer, that he had ever been anyone
at all; and the asking in him fell silent, for the first
time since the first of him, and there was no one left
inside to ask.
   And the last thing that Hana lost was him. Not her
own name — her name went easily; she had never
held it tightly, never needed it as that Adel needed
his. Not the gardens; not the music; not even Him
— for the turning toward Him was by then so deep
down in the floor of her, so far beneath anything that
a veil could reach, that it would go on in her unbro-
ken to the very end: a turning without an object, a
leaning toward a warmth she could no longer find,
or name, or account for. The very last thing to go
under — the one thing she held, and held, and held,
against the whole soft rising weight of the dark —
was the high quick light of the one she had pledged

                          164

to find again. His nearness. His note. The particular
shape of him, resting beside her in the warm dark.
She held it long after all the rest of her was gone, as
a hand holds the last warm thing in a cooling room;
she held it past all reason, and past all strength. And
then, very gently — as gently as everything else had
gone — that too slipped under; and the chord fell
silent; and she was only a small sleeping soul, being
carried down at the last into the waiting clay. With a
mark of pale gold upon her that she could no longer
see. With a thread of cold lodged fine as a hair be-
side it, that she no longer remembered to fear. And
beneath all of it, beneath everything, unreachable,
and unforgotten, and never once in all the life to
come entirely lost — the warmth. The warmth she
would spend her whole life turning toward, in every
dark and frightened hour of it, without ever knowing
why she turned, or what it was she turned toward,
or that she had promised, once, before the world,
to find it again — and to lead another home to it —
and to hold the dying in her two hands until the dark
became Light.


                       •    • •


And so the Light became darkness, that the dark-
ness might learn to long for Light.


                       •    • •




                           165

###SECTION: Interlude — The Setting Out###
The Setting Out



             At the Veil, Before the World




                       •    • •


And the time came; for there is a time even for the
things that lie outside time, and the long bright still-
ness of the country before the world had at last
reached the very edge of the going-out that, from
the first, it had been bending toward. At the thresh-
old of the veil — the veil that hangs between the
country where there is no forgetting and the country
where there is almost nothing else — the Archangel
stood at his appointed post and watched the going
begin.
  Among the numberless lights gathered now at
the brink, his eyes rested upon two; for he had
been charged with these from the beginning, before
any beginning that could be reckoned, and he knew

                           166

them now as a man knows the faces of his own chil-
dren. He knew the low one and the high one; he
knew the still note and the quick reaching one; he
knew the chord that ran between them, sounding
whole here in this place where wholeness was the
very air — sounding as it had sounded that day in
the great field of waking souls when each of them
had been turned, by some grammar older than any
grammar of any tongue, toward the other, and had
known it, and had not known what they knew. They
stood now at the brink in the simplicity of those who
have not yet been broken, and bore the Yes they had
given still warm in the centre of them; and they did
not know that in another breath they would forget
all of it.
   He had loved them since before they were given
the shape to be loved in. He had watched at the Yes;
he had watched the recording angel mark, with the
finer pen and the patient hand, the timbres of their
answers; he had loved the high quick reaching one
no less than the low still one — perhaps even a little
more, as a watcher who sees the long fall coming
loves the one who will fall. And now he stood at the
very edge of their leaving, and the leaving moved in
him as no leaving had ever moved in any of his kind.
   He did not doubt — there was no doubt in him,
and never had been. But he grieved.
   He grieved the going. He grieved the forgetting
that would meet them on the far side of the veil be-
fore they had taken so much as their first breath of
the heavy air — the forgetting that would close over
the bright certainty of the Yes as water closes over

                        167

a stone, leaving no trace anywhere on the surface,
so that they would walk the long road as if there
had never been any road at all. He grieved the clay;
the dark, heavy, beautiful, pitiless clay, that would
bruise them and burn them and ask of them, in the
end, things they had not yet been given to know how
to lose. He grieved, particularly, the long stretching
of the chord, for here in this place the two stood
as near to one another as breath stands near to
breathing, and once they crossed the veil the chord
between them would be drawn so far and so thin
across the dark that neither would be able to hear
it for years at a time, and they would think them-
selves alone, and they would suffer accordingly. He
grieved that the brightness was to be set into ves-
sels that would fail and fall.
   And out of the grief, in the manner of the long-
ago questioning of his kind when they had asked,
not in protest but to be taught, of the Lord of the
Worlds whether He would set upon the earth a crea-
ture that would work corruption — a question rose
in him now: not voiced, for he would not voice it;
but known, in the instant of its rising, by the One
to whom all things are known before ever they rise.
Why the forgetting. Why must the bright simple new
things be sent down into the dark to forget the very
thing that had been the truest in them. Why must
the going-down be as home.
   He was answered as he had always been an-
swered, for the matter was far too great for the
smallness of any word: reality moved, the whole of
it, all at once, soundlessly, as the deep sea moves

                         168

around a stone let fall into it; and where a question
had been, there was now a widening; and into the
widening, gift after gift, he was given to see.
   He was shown the descent, and shown, folded in-
side it where no eye in all the lower heavens could
have found it, the honour. For the going-down was
not, as the half-seeing might have read it, a demo-
tion; it was the very form the gift took. The children
of clay were not being sent down out of the bright
country to be diminished; they were being given the
one road that would, at the long end of it, lift them
above so much of creation that no created thing re-
membering the brink could have predicted it. The
descent was the road, and the road was the hon-
our, and the honour was the gift He had wanted for
them before He had so much as shaped the clay they
would be poured into.
   He was shown the apparent distance of the go-
ing, and shown, threaded through it where the eye
that sees only what shows would have missed it al-
together, the secret nearness. For the descent was
not abandonment, and no part of it was; the One
who was sending them down was going down with
them — into the very dark of the forgetting, into the
very middle of the heavy clay — closer to each of
them in the night that was coming than the artery
in their throat or the breath in their lungs; nearer to
them in the forgetting than He had been even in the
certainty of the bright country before the Yes was
said. The going looked like a separation. It was the
deepest form of being-with that had yet been made.



                         169

   He was shown the whole shape of the road, end to
end, and saw it for the first time as it was: that the
going-out and the coming-home were not two roads
at all, as he had half-thought them, but the same
road seen from either of its ends; the going outward
bending always, always, by the soft inexorable law
that was the very floor of all the worlds, back toward
the place from which it had set out. From Him; to
Him. The first breath of the descent was already the
first breath of the long return; and he had not, even
at his height, seen until that moment that the two
were one motion.
   And he was shown one thing more; and this was
the deepest of the gifts of that hour, and the one that
turned the grief in him from grief into the wide still
reverence that would carry him through every hard
hour of the watch that lay ahead. He was shown
the meaning of the forgetting. He had grieved it as
a stripping; it was not a stripping. It was the very
thing that would make the Yes, when it was said
again on the far side of the dark, precious in the
particular way it was meant to be precious.
   For his kind never forgot, and never had to choose
to remember, and could not therefore offer the Lord
of the Worlds the one gift the children of clay would
be made able to offer: the Yes spoken in the dark,
by a thing that had forgotten everything it had ever
known, on no evidence but the bare grain of the
turning-toward that had been knit into it before the
worlds. The angels’ love was a love that had been
given. The human love, when it came, would be a
love that had been chosen — chosen against the for-

                         170

getting, against the dark, against the cold thing and
the heaviness of the clay and every easier road. And
it was for this, the Archangel was given to under-
stand, that the journey was being given; for there is
a particular tenderness in the eye that turns at last,
after the whole long forgetting, toward the face of
the One who has been there all along, and that ten-
derness was the thing He had loved into being be-
fore the clay was shaped to bear it.
   And under and beneath and threaded through all
of it, the one reality that is the ground of every other
reality: that His mercy encompasses all things. The
descent and the return; the going and the coming-
home; the forgetting and the chosen remembering;
the brightness that would dim, and the clay that
would, at the long end, blaze.
   And the Archangel was shown what he had not
seen; and his grief was not taken from him, for he
loved them, and the cost was real, and the love
would not pretend the cost away; but it was changed.
It was turned, in the seeing, into the wide still rev-
erence of one who has been given to look upon the
whole shape of a thing he had only ever watched in
part. He understood the journey now. He would not
understand all of it; he was not asked to. He under-
stood enough.
   And the time came.
   He watched the low still note slip first across
the veil; watched her brightness dim as the heavy
air closed around it; watched her become, in a few
small breaths, a thing so small it might be held in
two human hands, in a warm crowded flat above

                         171

a lane in a city of the east that smelled of bread
and the sea. He watched the chord, drawn taut now
across the dark, sing one long quiet note of hold-
ing. And after her, in another season, he watched
the high quick reaching one cross also, into a glass
house above a strait of moving water, and saw the
high note shorten and quicken further still, as if
even in the very falling it was already beginning to
ask, already beginning to measure, already begin-
ning the long restless work that would be the shape
of him in the world.
   And the Archangel kept his watch. He had been
shown; he trusted; he grieved; he did not look away.
And below, in the warm flat above the lane that
smelled of bread and the sea, a child began to cry.
