                          ACT I
                      • •         •




                  The Descent

      We had taken a covenant from him before;
                   but he forgot.



                      • •         •


The Qur’an · Sūrah Ṭā Hā (20:115)




                          174

###SECTION: Chapter Twenty-Five — The Crossing###
The Crossing

 There is no remembering the crossing. Every soul
that has ever been born into the world has made it,
and not one of them has ever carried back so much
as a single word of it; for the crossing is the very
thing that takes the words away. It can be told only
from the outside, and only a little, by one who was
not himself crossing — and so it will be told here,
once, and gently, because you made it too, a long
time ago, and have spent the whole of your life since
then unable to recall the one journey that made you
what you are.
   She had been, at the end of all the things that
came before, only a small sleeping light, carried
down. Now she was arriving. And the arriving was
an unmaking. The weightlessness that had been the
only condition she had ever known gave way, by
slow degrees and then all at once, to weight; and
time — that strange new thing, in which one mo-
ment lets go of you before the next has come, so
that nothing whole can ever again be held entire —
time began, somewhere beneath her, to tick. The

                        175

great warmth that had been the floor and the ceil-
ing and the air of everything drew back, and back,
until it was no longer a place she was in but only a
rumour, a pull, a tide felt in the dark and nowhere
seen. And the bright country came loose from her,
piece by piece, as a tide goes out over flat sand: not
cruelly, never cruelly, but completely. The gardens
went. The far high music went. The chord went —
the low note and the high note that had once been
struck together and left to ring — and she did not
even feel it go, for the part of her that would have
grieved it was going under at the very same time.
  Everything went. The name she had not quite had
yet; the Voice, and the question it had asked, and the
great Yes she had answered with the whole of her-
self; the face she had held on to longest of all, an
age ago, against the rising dark — all of it loosened,
and slipped beneath the surface, and was gone. And
yet, beneath the floor of all of it, in the one room
the crossing could not enter, a single thing was not
taken. Not a memory; memory was the first thing
the crossing took. Only a direction. A leaning. A
turning-toward, with nothing left anywhere in her
to name the thing she turned toward, or to know
that she turned at all. The crossing stripped her of
everything she had ever known, and could not strip
her of the shape of herself; and so she came into the
world emptied of all that she had been, and still, un-
mistakably, and to the very end, herself: a small soul
that leaned, in the dark, toward a warmth it could
no longer find.



                         176

                       •    • •


It was nearly dawn, in a narrow room in the steep
old streets behind the great mosques, on the poor
side of a city built upon the seam between two
worlds. The window was going from black to grey.
The room smelled of clean water and of sweat and
of the particular closeness of a place where too
many people love one another in too few rooms.
A mother had laboured the whole night through,
and the labour was ending now; and there were
women\{}’s voices, low and certain, and an old
woman\{}’s hands that had done this many times
before and were not afraid; and a kettle somewhere;
and, under the window, the first grey stirrings of a
poor quarter waking to its work.
   And then the child was born; and she cried. It was
a small cry, and a furious one, and an astonished one
— the cry of a thing taken wholly by surprise to find
itself, of a sudden, with lungs to be surprised with,
and cold air to fill them, and a body, and a weight,
and a here. They wiped her, and wrapped her, and
laid her against her mother; and the mother wept, as
mothers weep, and the room let out a long breath it
had been holding since the dark.
   And then the oldest of them — a grandfather, gone
thin and bright with age — took the wrapped and
crying child into his two hands, as he had taken each
of them in their turn, and bent his cracked face close
to the small shell of her right ear, and spoke into it
the first words that the world would ever say to her.
God is greater, he said, very softly. God is greater. I

                           177

bear witness that there is no god but God. It was the
call to prayer; the words a man of his faith speaks
into the ear of every newborn, so that the first sound
it hears in this world is the same sound it will hope
to be saying as it leaves it.
   He did not know — how could he have known?
— what he was doing. He thought he was keeping
an old and beautiful custom, as his own father had
kept it over him. He did not know that the child in
his hands had, not one hour before, in the crossing,
lost the memory of a great Yes she had once given,
with her whole self, to that very witness, before the
world was made; nor that the first thing the world
now said into her ear was that same witness, called
softly back to her, in the dark, by a weeping old man
who believed he was only saying a prayer. The for-
getting and the first reminder, in the one same dawn.
So it is, for every one of them. So it was for you.
   And the strange thing — the thing the women in
the room would speak of for years, without ever
quite knowing why it had moved them so — was
that the child went quiet. Not asleep; quiet. The furi-
ous astonished crying simply stopped, between one
breath and the next, as the old words went into her
ear; and she turned her blind new face the smallest
fraction toward the sound of them, as a plant not yet
above the soil will turn, in the dark, toward a sun it
has never seen and cannot name. She did not under-
stand. She understood nothing; she was an hour old.
But the leaning in her, the turning-toward that the
crossing had not been able to take, knew the direc-
tion of those words the way water knows which way

                         178

is down. And the old man, who wept at every birth,
found that he was weeping a little harder at this one,
and could not for his life have said why.

                      •    • •

He came into the room then — the one who had been
waiting for her since before the world was lit. No
one saw him. There was nothing to see; he was not
a thing the eye was made for. There was only, of a
sudden, a steadiness in the room that had not been
there before; a particular settledness in the air be-
side the place where they had laid her; a peace, by
the cradle, that the tired mother felt without know-
ing she felt it, and was comforted by, and put down
to the coming of the dawn. He had crossed half of ev-
erything to be here. He bent close to the small new
light he had been given to keep, as he had bent once,
an age ago, at the edge of a great gathering, over a
mark he had not then understood — and he found it
again now, in the deep grain of her: the faint tracery
of pale gold, the two small arcs reaching toward one
another and not quite touching, that he had carried
the puzzle of for the whole long age between. And he
understood it again, freshly, and entirely. This was
the one. He took up his post beside her, and folded
himself into the long patience of the guarding; and
he did not mean to leave it, whatever it should cost
him, for as long as her life should last.
   And far off — at the thin cold edge of the warm
world, where such things keep their watch — it was
noted that a soul marked with the Seal had been

                          179

born in the old poor quarter of the city. The believ-
ing ones among the hidden people felt it pass, that
dawn, like a clear note struck somewhere below the
floor of the world, and were glad, and were uneasy;
for they had felt, in the very same hour, a second
mark answer it — a high quick one, born across the
water, in the bright tall houses that look down on the
strait from the wealthy hills. Two children, marked,
in one city, on one morning: the low note and the
high. And the cold thing that attended the second
birth, in the great bright house above the sea, did
not so much as glance toward the first. It had no
interest in the poor still child in the steep streets.
She would be easy, it had already decided; she was
the gentle kind, the contented kind, the kind that
leans and does not strive; there was no sport in her,
and no danger. Its whole vast and patient attention
was bent already, and would stay bent for a lifetime,
upon the clever one.
   They named her some days after, when the dan-
ger of the first days had passed and the naming
could be done with a glad heart. It was the grand-
mother who chose it, in the end, though everyone
was asked. They chose it for its meaning — ease, and
contentment, and a kind of quiet bliss — because
the birth had been so hard, and the child, once she
was safely in the world, had turned out so strangely
and so deeply easy to be near: a calm in the middle
of the crowded poor loud loving house, a stillness
that the others drifted toward without knowing they
were drifting, as the cold drifts toward a fire. Hana,
they called her. And not one of them knew that the

                        180

very thing they were blessing her with — her ease,
her contentment, the gentle un-striving peace of her
— was the same thing that something, somewhere,
had once spat at her like an insult, an age and a
world ago, and would one day try to use against the
one who loved her. They only knew that the name
was true the moment they said it; and that it fit her;
and that they loved her. And so, in love, and know-
ing nothing, they gave her back her own name.
   That first night she slept against her mother in the
poor warm room, with the city waking around them
to its prayers and its markets and its long, comfort-
able, reasonable forgetting; and the guardian kept
his post; and the Seal lay warm and unseen in the
deep of her. She had forgotten everything there was
to forget. She had forgotten Him, and the chord, and
the great Yes, and the gardens, and the Tree at the
border of everything, and the one she had promised,
before the world, to find again. All of it was gone,
down beneath the floor of her, under the whole vast
gentle weight of the crossing. And beneath even
that — in the one room the forgetting could not
reach — she remembered the only thing that was
needful, as you remember how to breathe without
ever being taught: which way to turn. Across the
water, that same grey dawn, a second cry had gone
up, in a great bright house above the sea — the
high note to her low. And neither of the two of them
would know, for seventeen years, that the other had
ever been born at all.




                         181

###SECTION: Interlude — A Face Not Yet Given###
A Face Not Yet Given

 Somewhere in the ordinary unfolding of that year,
in a hospital ward much like any other, a small new
soul opened its eyes for the first time on a world it
would spend the whole of its life failing to remember
it had already once agreed to enter.
   No trumpet sounded. No light broke through the
ceiling. A tired nurse noted the time, a mother wept
the ordinary exhausted joyful tears every mother
weeps, and the enormous covenant that had just
been folded, entire, into one small crying body went,
as it always goes, completely unremarked.
   Somewhere else in that same turning world, in a
different hospital, in a different tongue, it happened
again.
   Neither birth made the evening news. Neither
mother, drying her child’s first tears, could have
guessed that the ordinary miracle in her own arms
was already, silently, spoken for — that a promise
older than the mountains had just walked, unan-
nounced, back into the world in the smallest possi-
ble disguise, to spend the next several decades do-

                        182

ing what every such promise must do first: forget-
ting, completely, that it was ever made.




                       183

###SECTION: Chapter Twenty-Six — The Other Shore###
The Other Shore

 Cross the water, and the same dawn was breaking;
and it was not the same dawn at all.
   The same thin grey light that was creeping just
then over the steep poor streets of the old quarter
lay also upon the wide strait that split the city in
two, the long dark seam of moving water that the
whole place was built around and divided by; and it
crossed that water, the light, in the patient way of
light, and climbed the far shore, and there it found
a different world wearing the same morning. For on
the far hills the houses did not lean together over
the heads of too many people. They stood apart, and
back, behind walls and gardens and tall iron gates,
each one keeping its own grand silence; and they
grew, as the road climbed, taller and paler and more
glassed, until near the crest of the wealthy hills
there stood houses that were almost all of glass and
pale stone, with windows the height of three men
giving out across the strait, so that the people who
lived in them could stand in their own warm rooms
and look down upon the cold bright water and the

                       184

swarming city beyond it as one looks down at some-
thing one owns.
   In one such house, on that same dawn, a child was
being born.
   It was a house with a great many rooms and
a great many people to keep them, and very few
people to fill them. The air in it did not smell of
clean water and of closeness; it smelled of cut flow-
ers changed daily whether or not anyone had been
there to see them, and of polish, and of the cold
clean morning coming in off the strait through a win-
dow left open in another room. There was no crowd
of women with certain hands. There was a quiet,
costly room set aside for the purpose, and machines
that breathed and counted softly in the half-dark,
and a doctor who had been flown in and was very
good and very expensive, and nurses who moved
without sound; and there was a mother, young and
lovely and very tired, who had been told a great
many times that she need not be afraid and had not,
in the end, found this a comfort; and somewhere be-
yond the door, not in the room but near it, pacing,
there was a father.

                      •    • •

He too had made the crossing — the long unmaking
that takes the words away, that every soul makes
and none remembers. He too had come down out of
the warmth and the field of waking lights, and had
everything stripped from him, piece by piece, on as:
the music, the nearness, the great glad Yes, the low

                          185

still light that had once been turned toward his own.
All of it went under, in him as in her. But the tide that
runs out of a soul on its way into the world does not
leave the same thing behind in everyone; and what
it could not take from him — the one thing too deep
in the grain of him for the forgetting to reach — was
not a leaning. It was a reaching. Where she had been
left, beneath everything, with a turning-toward, he
was left with a question that had lost its words but
kept its shape: a restless, sourceless, sleepless need
to know — to take a thing up and turn it and find the
seam where it joined the next thing — that survived
the crossing whole, because it was not a thing he
had; it was the thing he was. He arrived emptied of
all he had ever known, and still, unmistakably, him-
self: a small soul that reached, in the dark, for an
answer it could no longer name and could not stop
wanting.
   And so when he was born — and he was born hard,
and fast, into the bright cold quiet of that costly
room — he did not cry as the other one had cried,
across the water. He cried as though he were ask-
ing something. There was in it, from the very first
breath, an insistence, a reaching, a refusal to be sim-
ply soothed; he turned his blind new head this way
and that as if the answer to the thing he was crying
about might be found if only he could get his face
pointed the right way; and the good expensive doc-
tor smiled, and the silent nurses smiled, and word
was carried out to the father that the boy was strong,
and vigorous, and alert — a clever one, they said,
you can see it already, look how he searches — and

                          186

the father heard clever, and was pleased, and came
in.
   The custom was kept. Of course it was kept; one
keeps the customs; an uncle had been sent for, an
old and dignified man who knew the words, and he
took the wrapped and searching child and bent to
his small right ear and said them, softly and cor-
rectly and well — God is greater; there is no god but
God — the same words, the very same, that had gone
that hour into another newborn ear in a poor flat
across the water. They were true in the rich room as
they were true in the poor one; words like those do
not change with the price of the carpet. They went
into him. And the strange thing — though no one in
that room would have thought it strange, for there
was no one in that room who knew the other child,
or what those words had done to her — was that he
did not still. He did not go quiet, and turn his face
the smallest fraction toward the sound, and rest. He
went on searching. The words went into him, and
were true, and he reached straight past them, al-
ready, for whatever was coming next; and the uncle,
who was a good man and meant the words sincerely
even if he had not bent his own face toward them
in years, handed the boy back, and the moment was
over, and it was buried at once under everything
that came after.
   For then the father spoke; and the father\{}’s
words were not few, and not soft, and not quickly
over. He took his son to the great glass window and
held him up against the cold bright morning and the
strait and the whole city laid out below, and he told

                        187

him — told him, an hour old, blind, searching, under-
standing nothing — what he would be. He would be
exceptional. He would want for nothing; there was
nothing the world held that would be kept from him.
He would be cleverer than all of them, and stronger,
and he would not be one of the small people who
are done things to; he would be one of the few who
do; he would stand one day where his father stood
and higher, and look down on more than his father
had ever looked down on. These were the first hu-
man meanings poured into the boy — not love, ex-
actly, though the father believed it was love, and
in his fashion it was; but ambition, and expectation,
and the long heavy freight of a great deal that was
wanted of him before he had done a single thing but
breathe. Across the water, that same hour, the first
meaning poured into another child had been only
this: that she was a calm in the middle of a loud lov-
ing house, and easy to be near, and theirs. Here the
first meaning was: you will be great. See that you
are.

                      •    • •

No one in the bright room felt the cold come in.
There was nothing to feel; it was not a thing the
body was made to notice. But it came — in across
the strait, up the climbing road, through the walls
and the iron gates that keep out everything except
the things that matter — and it gathered, with a ter-
rible and patient interest, at the edge of the costly
quiet room, and bent the whole of its attention upon

                          188

the searching child at the window. It had no inter-
est at all in the poor still girl across the water; it had
decided already that she would be easy, and gentle,
and no sport. But this one. This one reached, and
turned, and would not be soothed, and was being
told, an hour into the world, that he was made to
stand above other men. This one would be worth a
lifetime. And there settled in beside the child, that
morning, the close companion that settles in beside
every soul — a whisperer made to live at the very
ear of the mind, that feeds and grows on exactly the
thing this child had been left with, the reaching, the
asking, the sleepless turning-over of every seam —
and it took the measure of him, and found him good
ground, the best ground it had been given in a gen-
eration, and was glad.
   His guardian took up its post as well, quiet and
unregarded, as guardians do; and the faint pale trac-
ery lay warm and unseen in the deep of him, the two
small arcs of gold reaching toward one another and
not yet touching, exactly as it lay in the girl across
the water. He was as marked as she was. He was
as wanted as she was. He had come down trailing
the same warmth she had, from the same hand. It
was only that the warmth in his house had so very
much laid over it, already, on the very first morning
— so much glass, and ambition, and silence, and cold
bright water, and the long freight of all that was ex-
pected — that it would take him the better part of
two lifetimes to feel it again.
   They named him for greatness, as the father had
promised the window they would. They named him

                           189

Adel. It means the just — the upright, the fair, the
one who sets the crooked thing straight — and
the father chose it deliberately and proudly, for he
meant the boy to be a man of weight in the world,
a man who would right wrongs and be looked up to
and remembered; and he said the name out over the
city like a flag run up a pole. It was a great name. It
was, though the father did not know it and would
not have cared, one of the names that belong first
and most truly to Another, a fragment struck off the
divine and laid across the shoulders of a small new
soul like a king\{}’s own robe thrown over a child
who cannot yet stand. And it was too large for him —
not because he was less than other children, but be-
cause no child is as large as that — and some part
of the boy seemed to know it even then; for when
the name was spoken over him he did not quiet ei-
ther, but went on reaching, as though he had under-
stood, in the only way an hour-old soul can under-
stand anything, that a thing had been laid on him
that he would spend his whole life trying, and fear-
ing he would fail, to deserve.
   That first morning he slept at last, worn out with
searching, in a cot that had cost more than a family
across the water earned in a year, in a pale quiet
room with the cold bright strait beyond the glass;
and the city woke around him to its markets and
its prayers and its long comfortable forgetting, ex-
actly as it woke around her. Two children, born of
one dawn, on the two shores of one dark water: the
low note and the high, the rest and the reaching,
the one who had been met with love and the one

                         190

who had been met with greatness. Between them
ran the strait, and the whole turning city, and a bar-
rier neither could see and neither would cross for
a very long time. He had been given everything the
bright house could give. And he was already, faintly,
an hour into the world, reaching past all of it for the
one thing it did not contain — reaching, though he
would never have known to say so, across the water,
toward a stillness he would not meet for seventeen
years.

                   Two Houses

       Your wealth and your children are only a

The Qur’an · Sūrah at-Taghābun (64:15)

###SECTION: Chapter Twenty-Seven — The Still Child###
The Still Child

 The whole of the world, when she was small, was
four streets wide, and it was enough.
   It went uphill, the world, as everything in the old
quarter went uphill, in steep crooked steps of worn
grey stone that her short legs knew by heart before
she knew her own age; and it was loud, and close,
and full. There were her brothers, who were many,
and quick, and always shouting; and the smell of her
mother\{}’s cooking that lived in the walls and the
clothes and the hair of everyone in the flat, so that
she would carry it, the smell of that small kitchen,
in some deep fold of herself for the whole of her life
and never once smell it again. There were the cats
of the steps, and the old men at the tea house who
had been sitting in the very same chairs since be-
fore the world was made, as far as she could tell;
and the pigeons, and the washing strung between
the windows like the flags of countries no one had
ever heard of; and over all of it, five times in every
day, falling down across the crowded roofs from the
minaret just up the hill, the call — the long curling

                        192

climbing call to the prayer, that she had heard so
many thousands of times before she could walk that
it was not, to her, a sound at all, but simply one of
the things the day was made of, like the light, and
the smell of the bread, and her grandmother\{}’s
voice.
   She was, everyone agreed, a strange little thing.
Not strange in any way that troubled them; strange
in the way that a very calm child is always faintly
strange in a house full of loud ones. While her broth-
ers tore through the rooms and fought and wept and
laughed and broke things and were forgiven, she
would sit. She liked to sit. She would settle herself
in a doorway, or on the worn step by the kitchen,
or in the warm crook of whoever would have her,
and she would simply watch the loud bright world
go by with a kind of full and unhurried attention, as
though she were drinking it, as though there were
nowhere in all of it she would rather be and nothing
in all of it she lacked. Our little old soul, her mother
called her, half laughing, half something else. She
came out calm, said her grandmother, who had been
there. She has been calm since her first hour. You
remember. She would not cry.
   What none of them could have told you — because
none of them knew it, because the knowing of it had
been torn out of her, as it is torn out of everyone, on
the long road into the world — was that the stillness
was not nothing. It was a leaning, the deep grain
of her, made in her before she was made, running
quietly on beneath the loud little life of a poor child
in a poor quarter: a turning-toward, with nothing

                         193

left in her any longer to name the thing she turned
to. And so when the call came down across the roofs,
five times in the day, she did the thing she always
did, the thing no one had ever taught her and no
one had ever quite noticed: she went still, and stiller,
and turned the smallest fraction of herself toward it:
an instinct with no name and no teacher, unerring
as a compass needle finding north on its own. Her
grandmother noticed, once. Saw the small face go
quiet and tip, just slightly, toward the sound. And
said nothing; only put her old hand for a moment
on the child\{}’s head, and felt, without any words
for it, that she was in the presence of something she
had better not disturb.

                       •    • •

The day they found out what she could do, it was an
accident, and it was over a baby.
   A cousin\{}’s baby, brought to the flat in the mid-
dle of a long hot afternoon, and screaming — the par-
ticular boneless, sourceless, world-ending scream
of a very small child who cannot say what is wrong
and may not know himself. They had tried every-
thing. Her mother had tried, and her aunt, and
the cousin who was the mother and was by now
nearly weeping herself; he had been rocked, and
fed, and walked, and jiggled, and sung to, and still
he screamed, until the whole hot crowded flat was
wound tight as a fist around the noise of him. And
someone — her aunt, in the end, with a kind of laugh-
ing despair, having run clean out of grown people

                           194

to hand him to — put the screaming baby into the
lap of the strange calm six-year-old who was sitting,
as she always sat, quietly out of the way, watching.
Here, her aunt said. You try, then, since you are so
peaceful. And went to make tea, expecting nothing.
   And the baby stopped.
   Not at once; not as a tap is turned off. But the
great red roaring slackened, and caught, and slack-
ened again; and the small furious body, which had
been arched and rigid against the whole world,
went, by degrees, soft; and within the time it takes
to boil a pot of water the baby was lying against her,
hiccupping, spent, his wet eyes fixed on her face
with the dazed and total trust of a thing that has
come, after a long terror, somewhere safe. She had
done nothing. That was the part she could never af-
terward explain, the part that made the grown-ups
go quiet and look at one another over her head: she
had not jiggled him, or sung, or walked, or tried. She
had only held him, and been still, and let her own
deep stillness be a thing he could come and rest in
— had let the trouble in the small body know itself,
and tire itself, and settle, the way a frightened an-
imal settles when the thing holding it is not afraid.
She looked up at the ring of staring faces and did
not understand why they were staring. He was only
frightened, she said, as if it were the simplest thing
in the world. Now he is not.
   After that they brought her the criers. It became,
in the small economy of the flat, simply a thing that
was known and used: when a baby would not set-
tle, when a small one woke shrieking from a bad

                         195

dream, when even her brothers, much later, were
sick and miserable and impossible — sit with your
sister awhile, someone would say, and it worked, it
nearly always worked, and no one thought very hard
about why. Her grandmother thought about it. Her
grandmother, who taught her in those years all the
small things — how to fold her hands, and the short
prayers, and the beautiful names you could say over
and over on your fingers in the dark until the fear of
the dark went somewhere else — her grandmother
watched her sit with the frightened ones, and one
evening took the child\{}’s two hands in her own
old ones and looked at her for a long while, and
said: this is a gift, what you have. Do you understand
me? It is from Him. Most people, they make a fright-
ened person more frightened, because they cannot
bear the fear, and they fuss. You can bear it. You
can sit inside it and not run. That is a thing He gives
to very few, and He gives it to them for a reason,
and one day you will learn the reason. The child did
not understand most of this. But she remembered it;
she kept it as she kept everything, in the still place;
and a great many years later, in the dark, with a
stranger\{}’s hand in hers, she would understand
all of it at once.

                       •    • •

There was a boy, too, in those years — Zarif, from
the floor below, who was as loud as she was quiet
and as foolish as she was grave, and who attached
himself to her in early childhood for no reason any-

                           196

one could see and never afterward came unstuck.
He talked enough for the both of them. He was al-
ways in trouble, always falling off of things, always
saying the wrong thing to exactly the wrong person
and somehow being forgiven for it, because there
was a sweetness in him that disarmed even the peo-
ple he insulted; and now and then, in the middle
of all his clowning, he would say a thing so unex-
pectedly and so piercingly true that the grown-ups
would fall silent, and then laugh too loudly, and
change the subject. He made her laugh, which was
not easy. She made him quiet, which was harder.
They were, the pair of them, a kind of joke in the
building — the still girl and the silly boy — and they
did not care, and they were happy.
   And there was one more who kept her, in those
years, though she never saw it and never would. It
had been beside her since her first hour, since the
dawn it had crossed half of everything to reach her,
and it did not leave: a steadiness at her shoulder
that she felt, sometimes, as a kind of company in
an empty room, and put down to nothing, because
she was a child and children do not ask. It watched
her sit with the frightened ones. It watched her tip
her small face toward the call. It had guarded, in its
long existence, a great many souls, and it knew the
difference between the ones who would have to be
fought for every step of as and the ones who simply,
quietly, of their own deep grain, kept turning the
right way — and it knew which this one was, and it
loved her for it, in the clean uncomplicated way that



                        197

such beings love, and it kept its post, and was glad
of the keeping.
   Once — only once, in all those early years — some-
thing else came near. She was alone on the step
in the blue dark of an evening, waiting for the last
call, and a thought drifted into her that was not
hers: a small cold curling thought, that the warm
flat behind her was shabby, and poor, and that other
children somewhere had more, and that it was not
fair, and that she ought to mind. It was a reason-
able thought. It was the kind of thought that finds a
crack in almost anyone and widens it. But it found,
in her, no crack to widen — found only the deep still
leaning-toward, turned as it was always turned, with
no purchase anywhere on its smooth warm surface
— and it slid off her, as water slides off a stone that
the river has been polishing since before there were
rivers, and it went away, unfed, to look for better
ground; and it found that ground, that very night,
in a bright glass house across the water, where
a clever boy lay awake wanting things. She never
knew it had come. She only knew that the call began,
just then, up the hill, and that she turned toward it,
and was glad.
   That was her childhood: the four streets, and the
loud warm flat, and the smell of the bread, and
the criers brought to be quieted, and the grand-
mother\{}’s hands, and the foolish faithful boy, and
the call falling five times a day across the roofs, and
the unseen steadiness at her shoulder, and the deep
glad leaning that ran on beneath all of it like a low
note held under a song. She had almost nothing, by

                         198

the measure of the wide world. She did not know
it, and would not have believed it, and in the one
way that finally counts she was right not to: for the
very poverty of that house, which laid so little over
the warmth, was the clear glass through which the
light came straight to her, undimmed, every single
day — while across the water, in a house with every-
thing, a boy who had been given the whole world
was learning, a little more with each passing year,
how to want.




                        199

###SECTION: Dream Fragment — She had these dreams from very young, and never###
She had these dreams from very young, and never
told anyone but Allah about them, because there
were no words in the four streets she knew that
would have held them without breaking.

                  The Veiled Ones

After that, they began to feel the veiled ones.
   It came gradually, as one becomes aware, in a
darkened house, of a great room one cannot see —
not by the sight of its walls but by the changed qual-
ity of the silence, the sense of a space, and a pres-
ence, on the far side of a wall whose edge one can-
not find. The bright country, they understood now,
was not all one even brightness, all of a kind with
themselves. There were souls within it greater than
they were, far greater — veiled from them as the
great bearer had been all but veiled from Hana —
lights so high and so deep that to be shown them
unready, unprepared, all at once, would have been
not a gift at all but an injury, as the noon sun is an in-
jury to an eye that has known only candlelight. The
four of them could not see the veiled ones. But they
could feel, now, the very shape of the veiling: as one

                          200

feels a great heat through a closed wall, and knows,
without seeing a single flame, that somewhere just
beyond it an enormous fire is burning.
   And each of the four felt it differently. And in the
difference — in the precise way that each one met
the simple fact of a greatness veiled from him — the
whole of what each of them was, and the whole of
what each of them would in the end become, stood
already written, plain as a name, had any of them
yet known how to read it.

                       •   • •

Hana felt longing. Pure longing, and aching, and
without the faintest thread of resentment anywhere
in it — the longing of a child stood outside a lit
window on a cold road at night, who does not for
an instant envy the warm figures within, nor resent
the glass between, but only loves the warmth, and
wants to be nearer to it, and would wait a long, long
time in the cold for the door to open of its own ac-
cord rather than once complain that it was shut. The
veiling did not offend her. It could not; there was
nothing in her for it to offend. It made her yearn,
only; and the yearning was sweet, sweeter almost
than the having would have been; and she did with it
what she did with everything — she turned it toward
Him — until it became the truest and the simplest
prayer she had yet prayed in all her short life: not
let me in, not show me, not even nearer, but only,
and entirely, You. Whatever waits behind the veil, it
is Yours; and because it is Yours, I already love it;

                           201

and because I love it, and trust the hand that hung
the veil, I can wait.
   Adel felt hunger. Not longing — hunger; and the
two are cousins that the careless take for one an-
other, but they are not the same, and the difference
between them is the difference between a prayer
and a siege. Hunger is the sharp forward lean of a
mind that has caught the scent of a thing it does not
yet possess and cannot bear not to possess. The veil,
to Adel, was not a warmth to be loved from without;
it was a problem to be solved, a locked door with a
key somewhere if only he were clever enough to find
it, an affront, almost, to the deep article of his faith
that nothing — nothing anywhere — ought to stand
closed to a sufficiently determined understanding.
He did not want to wait at the window. He wanted
the house. And more than once, leaning toward the
high veiled quarter, he caught himself doing a thing
that ought to have frightened him and did not: lean-
ing, not in worship, but in calculation — measuring
the brightness with a cold appraising eye, asking
himself how near a soul might come to it if only it
were clever enough, only worthy enough, only owed
enough — and he did not see, for he had not yet
learned to look for such a thing in himself, how very
near that asking ran to another asking, far older and
far colder than his own, that had once measured
a brightness in just that way, and weighed it, and
found in it a reason that it was owed.
   And Sami felt neither longing nor hunger. Sami
felt the third thing, the thing that has no sweetness
in it anywhere at all, not even the bitter sweetness of

                         202

wanting. Sami felt the veiled ones as a rebuke — as
an insult aimed, somehow, precisely and personally
at him. For there were souls set above him. Above
him: above Sami, the brightest of all the young, the
flame that leapt the highest in the fire, the one the
eye went to without being asked. And the bare fact
of their height — the sheer intolerable existence of a
greatness that he had not been given and could not
reach — sat down in him like a coal that would not
cool, and would not be ignored, and would not, how-
ever he turned away from it, go out. He did not love
them. He did not even, in truth, wish to know them,
to come near them, to learn what they were. He
wished, simply and coldly, that they were not there.
Or, failing that — for even Sami could not quite wish
away the whole high country — he wished to be told
by what right they had been lifted up above him, and
by whom, and on what grounds; and he found, turn-
ing the grievance over and over in the small dark
place that was so quietly growing in him, that he
could imagine no right that would have satisfied him,
no reason, no merit, no decree, that would not still
have left him, intolerably, unbearably, below. It was
the smallest of seeds. No one watered it; no whisper
touched it; the cold at the edge of the country had
not yet so much as spoken to him. It did not need
watering. Some seeds carry their own rain.

                      •   •     •

Zarif felt all three at once — the longing and the
hunger and the rebuke, for Zarif felt everything that

                          203

there was to feel, and a good deal besides — and
was troubled by not one of them; because Zarif had
made his peace, long ago, in some hour none of the
others had witnessed, with not being shown. And
when Hana, who alone could speak with him in as
the wordless speak, leaned the bare shape of her
longing toward him — why are they veiled? why
may we not see, if the seeing would be so sweet a
thing to have? — he gave her, for the second time,
no joke at all; and the giving of no joke, from Zarif,
was always the surest sign that the truest thing was
coming. Some secrets, he shaped, gently, are not
walls. They are the caress of God. He keeps a thing
back from you the very way you would cup your two
hands about a small new flame against a wind —
not to keep the flame from you, never that, but to
keep it for you; to keep it alive, and burning, and
yours, until you have become a hearth wide enough
and strong enough to hold it without being burned
away to nothing by it. Do not grieve the veil, Hana.
The veil is not His refusal. The veil is His tenderness.
And on the day that it lifts — for it will lift — you will
look back and see that it had been His own hand all
along, held up between you and the brightness; and
you will see that the hand was warm.
  Hana carried that one too, down into the deep
place where she kept the things that mattered. She
was becoming, without ever once noticing that she
was becoming it, the keeper of the things Zarif said
when he was not being funny — which were always,
without fail, the truest things he said, and which



                          204

he always, without fail, said quietly, and only when
there was no one but Hana near enough to hear.
   So they stood, the four of them, at the very thresh-
old of the high country, and could not cross it: one
longing, and one hungering, and one resenting, and
one wholly at peace. The veil hung before them all
alike, warm and impassable and kind; and behind it
the great lights waited — Maryam, and Yusuf, and
the one who would love for nothing, and the rest, the
whole shining company of them — whom the four
would meet far sooner than they knew, and each of
them in the exact manner that each of them was,
in this very hour, all unknowing, preparing himself
to be met. For this is the way of it, and it is worth
the learning: we are never shown the great things
until we have already become the kind of soul that
will be shown them in one way rather than in an-
other. The veil does not only keep us out. It also,
mercifully, gives us the time — the one thing we are
always given, and almost always squander — to de-
cide who we will have made ourselves into on the
day that it at last comes down.
   She woke, as she always woke from these, not
frightened but full — too full for a small girl’s chest
to hold — and lay very still in the dark until the full-
ness settled, and did not know, and would not know
for years, that the veil she had dreamed of was the
same veil the whole of her waking life was quietly,
patiently, teaching her to lift.




                          205

###SECTION: Dream Fragment###
Another night, another year — she could never af-
terward have said which came first, only that they
came, always, in the hour just before the dawn call,
as though something on the other side of sleep was
timing them to the one hour the four streets them-
selves went quiet.

        The Garden Beyond Gardens

There came a time — if time is the word for it, and
it is not, for there was no time there, only the long
unbroken now — when the old light came for them;
and it did not come in the way that it usually came.
Hilal did not, this once, appear at the soft edge of
some moment and slip away again before they had
quite taken him in, like the moon turning its lit face
slowly out of sight. He came for them. He gathered
them in — Hana and Adel, Sami and Zarif, the four
of them together — drawing them to him with the
quiet, weighted deliberateness of one who has been
granted leave to show a thing he will not be permit-
ted to show a second time.
   He led them outward. They leaned, all four of
them, into the wake of his deep old layered light,

                        206

and were carried by the leaning — past the gardens
they had come to know, past the blue hush of the
listening-place and the grave rose-warmth of the pa-
tient ones, past even the high veiled quarter at the
rim of the bearable, where the young souls were al-
ways, always turned so gently back. He led them on,
further than any of them had ever gone, all the way
to the border of the bright country. To the very edge
of what they were.
   And there, across a remove that was not a dis-
tance — for distance had not been made, and this
was something else, something stranger and more
absolute: a difference, the sheer unbridgeable dif-
ference between what they were and what they
could never, ever be — they saw the Tree.
   It stood at the farthest edge of everything that
could be called a place, and everything that was
not it seemed to lean toward it, faintly, as the souls
themselves leaned toward the warmth — as though
it were a second center, or the same center seen
from nearer. Its leaves were vast beyond the mean-
ing of vast, wide as countries, and a light moved
within them that was borrowed from nowhere and
from nothing, that was simply and originally its
own. Its fruit hung heavy and golden, like vessels
of poured and standing light. And where its roots
should have gone down into some ground, some
floor, some dark sustaining earth, they went down
instead into the brightness itself — not the gentle
warmth of the country they knew, but something
nearer to the Source of that warmth, so pure and
so unmixed that merely to lean toward it was to feel

                         207

one’s own small self begin to thin, and stream away,
like a wisp of morning mist at the first touch of the
sun. And beyond the Tree there was no beyond that
their seeing could take hold of. There was the Tree;
and past the Tree the unsayable; and, rising in each
of them all at once and entire, the sudden and total
knowledge that here, exactly here, the made world
ended, and the nearness of its Maker began.
   They could not approach it. They leaned — they
could not help but lean — and the brightness turned
them back: gently, firmly, without the least unkind-
ness, as a warm hand turns back a small child from
the very fire it loves and reaches for. Even Sami,
who leaned the hardest of them all, who strained to-
ward it with everything that was in him, was turned
as gently back as the rest. There was no crossing.
There would be no crossing. There was only, for
them, the looking.
   This, said Hilal — and his old meaning came to
them clear, and edged, and almost grieving — is the
border of what we are. What lies beyond it is not for
souls to cross; not here, not now, not in the way that
you would wish; and perhaps not at all until every-
thing that you now are has been unmade, and made
again. Look at it. Look long. Remember, ever after,
that you looked. For I will tell you a thing, and you
will not understand it until it has already happened
to you: some of you will dream of this Tree when
you are flesh — when you have forgotten your own
names, and the warmth, and the chord, and one an-
other, and all the rest of it; you will dream of it for
the breath of a single night, far down in the dark of

                         208

some ordinary sleep; and you will wake with your
face wet and your chest aching, and you will have
no word, not one, for the thing that you have lost.
   Hana looked, and her longing went down so deep,
and grew so still, that it passed clean through long-
ing and came out the other side as something almost
like peace — the peace of one who has been shown,
from far off down the road, the door of the very
house she is walking toward, and now knows, past
all argument and all doubt, that the house is real,
and that the road goes there, and that she has only
to keep walking. Adel looked, and the old hunger
leapt up in him hot and immediate, and he found
himself turning over, helplessly, almost against his
own will, the only questions he had ever known how
to ask: how might a soul come nearer? what would
one have to become? what would one have to know,
to know enough, to be allowed at last across? Sami
looked, and felt the border not as a mercy and not
as a mystery but as a wall — a wall built, it seemed
to him, and built deliberately, to keep him out. And
Zarif looked; and Zarif, who had a word for every-
thing, who could not bear a silence and had never in
his whole existence let one stand — Zarif said noth-
ing at all. And the nothing that he said was the most
reverent thing that any of them had ever heard him
not say.
   Then Hilal turned them back toward the bright
young country, and they went; and the brightness
of the Tree fell away behind them. But each of them
carried it away inside — carried it down, and folded
it into the deep place beneath the place where the

                        209

forgetting would one day be able to reach: a single
seed of light, buried so far down, and planted so
deep, that all the long dark that was coming would
never, in all the worlds, quite manage to cover it
over. They did not know that yet. They knew only
that they had stood, once, at the very edge of ev-
erything; and had looked; and had been allowed
to look; and that nothing afterward, in any world,
would ever feel entirely whole or entirely finished
again — because they had seen, just once, with their
own waking sight, what wholeness was; and would
spend all the worlds to come, and all the long roads
of them, trying and failing and trying again to re-
member its name.
  She never remembered the name in the morning.
She remembered only the rain that had not touched
her, and the note under everything, and — for the
whole of that day, every time, without fail — an ache
with no handle on it that she had long since stopped
trying to explain to herself, and simply called, when
she called it anything at all, missing someone.




                        210

###SECTION: Dream Fragment — The last of these childhood dreams came to her only###
The last of these childhood dreams came to her only
once, on the night before her eighth birthday, and
she never dreamed anything quite like it again — as
though it had been permitted to her only the once,
and only because she would need, one day very far
off, to have somewhere in her to set the thing she
was about to be shown.

             The Light of Maryam

Hilal did not let them rest long after the Tree. There
was an order to the things that he meant to show
them, and only so long in which to show them; and
so, while the memory of the border was still raw and
bright and aching in them, he brought them back to-
ward the veiled quarter — not to its edge this time,
not to look across at the thing that could not be
crossed, but to stand, just once, for the space of a
single passing, in the very nearness of one of the
veiled ones herself.
  Bend your seeing, he told them. You cannot look
at her straight — no more than you could look
straight at the Tree. Lower it, as you lower your gaze
before a brightness too great to meet; and let her

                        211

pass; and let whatever reaches you reach you side-
long, from the corner of yourself, where alone you
will be able to bear it.
   They bent their seeing. And she passed.
   It was not brightness. That was the thing that
none of them had braced for, and so the thing that
undid them. They had steeled themselves against a
great light, a blaze fiercer than any they had yet
been shown; and what came was not blaze at all,
not light in any way they had a name for, but pu-
rity — a cleanness so complete and so total that it
had no edges anywhere; a stillness so deep that it re-
buked nothing, accused nothing, demanded nothing,
and yet made each of the four of them, helplessly
and instantly and without a word, achingly aware of
his own noise. She passed as a current of clear wa-
ter passes through troubled and clouded water; and
where she passed, the trouble saw itself, suddenly,
for what it was. Not one of them had known that he
was unclean — until the clean went by. Not one of
them had known that he was loud — until he stood,
stripped and silent, in the long wake of that silence.
   Hana wept. There is no other word for what her
light did in that moment, though tears had not yet
been invented, and lay a long age still in the fu-
ture; something deep in her broke softly and entirely
open, and poured, and went on pouring, and could
not be stopped, and did not for one instant wish to
be. She had met — without being told it, without
a word of explanation — the very pattern and the
promise of what she herself might one day become:
a soul so wholly given over, so perfectly emptied of

                         212

its own noise and its own clamor and its own self,
that God could pour Himself into it and fill it ut-
terly and leave nothing out. And the meeting did
not crush her, as it crushed Sami, into a sense of
her own smallness and unworth; it flooded her in-
stead with a longing so clean and so complete that
it could not be told apart from love. She wept, and
was changed by the weeping, and would carry the
print of that one passing pressed into her for the
whole of the long road that was coming; and would
find it again, faintly, unaccountably, every time in
all the worlds to come that she sat down quietly be-
side someone who was dying, and stopped her own
noise, and went still, and let the trouble in the room
know itself, and settle, and grow at last quiet too.
   Adel did not weep. Adel was undone in another
way altogether — in the way of a mind that has built
its whole house, every wall and beam of it, upon a
single foundation, and feels, of a sudden and with-
out warning, that foundation shift beneath it. For
Adel had believed — deeply, and without ever once
saying it aloud, even to himself — that the road
to the highest things ran through knowing; that if
only he could ask the right questions, and pierce the
right veils, and understand deeply enough and finely
enough and long enough, he would, in the end, rise.
And here, passing, was a height that no knowing in
all the worlds could ever have climbed to. She had
not asked her way to that purity. She had not rea-
soned it out, nor argued toward it, nor solved it like
a riddle. She had simply been so wholly and so ut-
terly given that there was no longer any room left

                         213

in her for anything that was not Him — and that,
Adel saw, with a cold lurch in the deep of him, stood
higher than the whole of his asking, higher than any-
thing his asking could ever build or reach or earn;
and his asking, for all its hunger and all its brilliance,
could not so much as come near it. It was the first
crack — the very first — in the bright cold thing that
the voice at the edge had lit in him. And he did not
know, standing there in the wake of her, whether to
be grateful for the crack, or to hate it.
   Sami felt only the humiliation. For here was a
greatness that had nothing whatever to do with
brightness — nothing to do with reaching, or leap-
ing, or burning highest, or being the one the eye
went to without being asked; a greatness, in fact, of
the precise and exact opposite of all of that: a great-
ness of smallness, of stillness, of self laid all the
way down and made perfect in the laying. And Sami
could find no way at all to want it. To have wanted
it would have meant wanting to be less; and every-
thing in him, every fiber of his bright and reaching
nature, strained always and only toward more. So
he stood in the long wake of the purest thing that he
had ever known or would ever know, and felt — of all
the unworthy things a soul could feel in such a pres-
ence — cheated. Cheated of a greatness he could
have understood. And the coal in him, the small dark
coal that would not cool, glowed a degree the hotter.
   And Zarif — Zarif, who had a word for every sin-
gle thing under and beyond the light, who could not
abide a silence and had never once in his existence
allowed one to stand unfilled — said nothing. When

                          214

at last Hana, still softly pouring, leaned toward him
the bare wordless shape of a single question — what
was that? what did we just stand in? — he gave her
no answer. He gave her no joke. He gave her no
truth wrapped in a hundred bright absurdities. I will
say nothing of that, he shaped, very simply, and very
quietly. It is the wisest thing I have ever done. And
he meant it; and it was.
   She woke with her face wet and did not know why
she had been crying, and told no one, and carried
the wanting she had felt in the dream — the wanting
to have earned such a light herself, plank by plank —
into a life that would spend itself, one dying stranger
at a time, trying, without ever quite knowing that
this was what she was doing, to build exactly that.
