###SECTION: Chapter Twenty-Eight — The Clever One###
The Clever One

 The whole of the world, when he was small, was
larger than he could ever get to the end of; and it
was never, for a single hour, enough.
   It had been built to be looked at, the house he
was small in — to be looked at, and looked out of,
and never quite lived in. It went on and on: pale
stone and cold glass, long rooms that opened into
longer rooms, a stair that turned and turned, and ev-
erywhere the great high windows giving out across
the strait, so that the moving water and the swarm-
ing far shore were always there at the edge of ev-
erything, framed and silent and owned. It was full.
It was full of beautiful things — of pictures, and
instruments no one played, and books in four lan-
guages, and objects whose only purpose was to be
costly and to be seen to be costly — and it was kept
by quiet people who were not family, who moved
through the rooms setting right what no one had
disturbed; and it was, for most of the hours of most
of the days, very nearly empty of anyone for him
to be with. His mother was a kind of glamour that

                        216

arrived and departed, smelling of somewhere else,
lovely and quick and gone. His father was a voice
on a telephone in a far room, a tall presence at the
head of a long table, a man who loved him as a man
loves a project he has high hopes for. There was al-
ways someone paid to be near him. There was al-
most never anyone who simply was.
   So he asked. From the time he could speak he
asked, and asked, and asked — why is the water
two colours where the seas meet, why do the gulls
follow that boat and not this one, why is that man
poor and we are not, why, why, why — not the idle
why of an ordinary child, that is satisfied with any
answer and forgets it, but a deep, driving, sleepless
why that took every answer it was given and turned
it over at once and found, on the underside of it, the
next question, and the next. He took his toys apart
to see the seam where one piece joined another. He
took apart, in time, ideas the same way. He could
not leave a surface alone; he had to know what was
under it; it was not something he did, it was some-
thing that was being done in him, all the time, by
the very grain of him, and it gave him no rest, and
he did not know how to want it to stop.
   And the world, his world, loved him for it. This
was the thing that shaped him more than the glass
or the silence or the absent lovely mother: that the
one part of him that reached — the asking, the clev-
erness, the quick hungry mind — was the one part
of him the grown people lit up at. Brilliant, they said,
over his head, to one another, in the satisfied voices
adults keep for a child who reflects well on them.

                         217

Listen to him. Did you hear what he asked? He had
learned before he was seven that a good enough
question would make the tall busy people stop, and
turn, and look at him with something that was al-
most, almost the thing he was hungry for; and so
he sharpened his questions as another child might
have sharpened a stick, for the power in them; and
the more he was praised the more he asked, and
the more he asked the more he was praised, and no
one in all that bright full house ever once noticed
that the asking had begun, somewhere underneath,
as a kind of reaching — or that it was slowly, year
by year, being taught to become a kind of winning
instead.

                      •   • •

He understood this best, long afterward, by remem-
bering a single afternoon.
   It was a holiday; the house was full for once, full
of relatives and the smell of good food and the par-
ticular hush of an old hoca who had been invited,
a gentle learned man with a white beard, to say the
words over the gathering. And the boy, who was per-
haps eight, and who had been listening to the talk of
God all afternoon the way he listened to everything,
with that turning-over hunger, waited for a silence,
and asked his question. It was a real question. It
came up out of the deep buried place in him almost
without his choosing it, and it had, though he could
not have said so, the whole of his hidden reaching
folded into it: if God has no need of anything, he

                          218

asked, and the old man\{}’s eyes came up, why did
He make us at all? What is it for? It was not a clever
question. It was the oldest and the most naked ques-
tion there is, and the boy had asked it nakedly, and
for one moment the old hoca looked at him with a
sudden deep attention, as though he had heard, un-
der the words, the thing that was really being asked,
and had meant to answer that instead.
   But the room did not let him. The room laughed
— fondly, delightedly, proudly — and his father\{}’s
voice rose over all of it, warm and ringing and
pleased: you see? You see what he is? Eight years
old and he argues theology with the hoca! He will
be a lawyer, this one — a professor — a minister! —
and there was applause, actual applause, and some-
one ruffled his hair, and a plate of sweets was put
in front of him as a reward; and the old man\{}’s
mouth closed on whatever he had been about to say,
and he smiled a smaller smile, and the moment was
gone. The boy ate the sweets. He had been told he
was wonderful. And somewhere far down beneath
the pleasure of it, in the place the question had come
from, a small thing that had reached out and not
been met drew quietly back, and closed a little, and
learned the lesson the whole bright afternoon had
been teaching him without one person in it mean-
ing to: that the reaching got you nothing, and the
cleverness got you everything; and that if there was
an ache under the asking, the thing to do with the
ache was to be brilliant about it, and be loved for
that instead.



                         219

  And here was the strangest part, the part that
should have warned somebody: none of it ever filled
him. He won, and won, and won — the praise, the
prizes, the wide eyes of the grown-ups, the small
kingdoms of the clever child — and each thing he
won was wonderful for about an hour, and then it
was nothing, it was already behind him, it was a
question with the answer found and therefore dead,
and the hunger stood up again inside him exactly
as tall as before and looked around for the next
thing. He did not understand it. He was told, often,
how fortunate he was, and he could see that it was
true, and that made the restlessness worse, because
a boy who has everything and is still not full has
nowhere to put the blame but on himself. He was
lonely in a way he had no word for, at the centre
of a great deal of attention. He had everything the
bright house could give. And it was not enough; it
was never, ever enough; and he was beginning, with-
out knowing it, to suspect that nothing ever would
be.

                      •   •     •

He was not alone in the wanting. There was a thing
that lived very close to him, closer than the paid
people, closer than his own breath — the near com-
panion that is given to every soul, that lives at the
very ear of the mind — and it was a thing that
grew strong on exactly what he had the most of: the
turning-over, the reaching, the sleepless hunger of
the clever mind. It did not speak in a stranger\{}’s

                          220

voice; that is not how it works; it spoke in his. It
was there in the hour after every triumph, when
the prize had gone cold, with the next thing already
named and gleaming: you were made for more than
this. You were made to know. They are slow and you
are quick; that is not your fault; that is simply true. It
is not wrong to want to be above them. It is only hon-
est. And the cold curling thought that had drifted off
a polished stone in a poor doorway across the wa-
ter, one evening years before, and gone looking for
better ground — it had found that ground here, in
this boy, and made itself at home, and it had been
patient ever since, and it was in no hurry at all, be-
cause it had a whole life to work in, and it knew, the
way a farmer knows good soil, exactly what it had
been given.
   His own guardian kept its post, as faithful as the
one across the water, and far more grieved. For it
could see all of it — could see the small reaching
thing draw back and close, could see the wrong food
set before the child and the right hunger fed on it
— and it could do almost nothing, because it is not
given to such beings to seize a soul and turn it; they
may stand near, and steady, and wait, and hope, and
they may never once be heeded, and they must keep
faith anyway. It loved the boy. It is worth saying
plainly, because so much of what was coming would
look from the outside like a thing unloved: he was
loved, fiercely, by the unseen thing at his shoulder,
the whole way down. He simply could not feel it, un-
der everything; and the door it kept open for him
stood open in a room he had stopped going into.

                           221

   Only at night, sometimes, when the great house
had gone quiet and the clever mind at last ran down,
something would surface in him that he could not ac-
count for. An ache. Not for anything; that was what
made it unbearable; if it had been for a thing he
could have gone and got the thing, and he could
get anything. It was an ache that had no object
— a sense, rising up out of the dark under every-
thing, that there was something, somewhere, that
he was meant for and had lost and could not name;
a warmth he had no memory of and missed as you
miss a face from a dream that is already gone by the
time you are awake enough to look at it. He would lie
in the wide quiet dark and reach for it, and it would
thin, and fade, and be gone, and leave him emp-
tier than before. And because he was clever, and
because cleverness was the only tool he had been
given, he would explain it to himself — too much
rich food, too little sleep, the strange moods of a
growing boy — and turn over, and reach instead for
tomorrow\{}’s questions, which at least could be an-
swered.
   That was his childhood: the glass, and the long
owned view, and the lovely absent mother, and the
proud planning father, and the praise that filled
nothing, and the questions sharpened into weapons,
and the patient voice in his own accent, and the
grieving guardian he could not feel, and the name-
less ache that surfaced in the dark and was rea-
soned away by morning. He had been given the
whole world, exactly as his father had promised the
window he would be. And across the water, four

                        222

streets wide and uphill all the way, a girl he had
never seen lay each night in a crowded poor warm
room and turned, in the last of the day, toward
a thing she could not name either — except that
where his nameless thing left him emptier, hers left
her full; and where he reached, and found noth-
ing, and called it nothing, she leaned, and was met,
and called it enough. The same warmth. The same
hand. One of them had almost nothing laid over it,
and one of them had the whole bright world. It was
not enough. For him, that early, it was already not
enough.




                        223

###SECTION: Chapter Twenty-Nine — The Meeting of the Waters###
The Meeting of the Waters

 There is one place in that city where everyone,
sooner or later, stands in the same air.
   It is down at the bottom of everything, where the
hills give up at last and the land runs out into the
meeting of the waters — where the Golden Horn
comes down to join the great strait, and the ferries
churn back and forth between the two shores all day
long, and the old bridge stands crowded rail to rail
with men fishing over the side into water the colour
of milky tea. It is the one place the whole divided
city is poured through and stirred: the rich and the
poor, the pious and the careless, the ones from the
bright hills and the ones from the steep streets, all
of them shoved up against one another in the smell
of fried fish and diesel and the sea, under a sky torn
to pieces by the gulls. Up in the city the two of them
could live a whole childhood four miles and a whole
world apart and never come within sight of one an-
other. But the waterfront gathers everyone in the
end. And on one bright hard blue afternoon, when
each of them was eleven years old, it gathered them
both.

                        224

                       •   • •

She had come down with her grandmother, who
was slower now than she had been, and leaned
on her, and stopped often, so that the errand that
should have taken an hour took the whole after-
noon — which suited the girl, who never minded
slowness, and who liked the waterfront as she liked
everything, fully, without needing it to be anything
other than what it was. She carried the basket. She
watched the boats. She watched a boy sell sesame
rings off a swaying tray and never drop one; she
watched an old man feed a gull from his fingers; she
stood in the great moving crowd as still and as con-
tent as she stood anywhere, a small still point with
the river of the city pouring round her, holding her
grandmother\{}’s thin arm and turning her face up
now and then to the salt wind off the water. She
was happy. She did not know why she should be es-
pecially happy that day, of all days; she only was;
there was a brightness on the afternoon that she
could not have accounted for and did not try to.

                       •   • •

He had been brought down by a driver and a tutor,
on the way to somewhere else — there was always
a somewhere else — and made to wait while the car
was seen to, and he had slipped, in the way of clever
bored boys, a little distance off the leash, just to the
rail, just to watch. He hated being managed and he
loved the water, and down here the water was not

                           225

the silent owned thing it was from the high windows;
down here it was loud, and dirty, and alive, and full
of working boats and shouting men, and he leaned
on the rail and drank it in with the turning-over
hunger, asking himself a hundred questions about
tonnage and currents and the trade of the bridge,
and feeling, underneath the questions, a thing he
felt sometimes among ordinary people in ordinary
places and could never explain: a wish, sharp and
surprising, to be one of them — to be a boy who be-
longed down here in the noise, with somewhere to
be and someone waiting, instead of a watched and
separate thing on his way to a somewhere else he
did not care about. He was restless that afternoon
in a way that was almost an ache. He put it down to
the heat.

                      •   • •

And the crowd, which is a kind of weather, and has
currents of its own, drew them slowly together.
   Neither of them felt it begin. How could they? It
was only a crowd, only the ordinary shoving river
of a city\{}’s afternoon — and yet, as the gap be-
tween them closed, from a hundred yards to fifty to
twenty, each of them grew, without knowing why,
more and more peculiarly alert. The girl found her
stillness gone strangely taut, expectant, as the air
goes taut before a name you have forgotten is about
to come back to you; she kept half-turning, looking
for something she could not have described, certain
for no reason that there was something there to be

                          226

looked for. And the boy felt the restless ache in him
swing round and sharpen to a point and begin, ab-
surdly, to feel like waiting — like the half-second at
the very top of a stair you have climbed in the dark,
when your foot is already lifting for a step that you
are suddenly, wildly sure is there. Each of them, in
the great careless crowd, began to hunt the faces.
Neither knew for whom.
  And for one moment — one — the city brought
them within the reach of an arm.
  It happened at the mouth of the bridge, in a hard
press of bodies, where a ferry had just emptied its
hundreds into the path of the hundreds waiting to
board, and the whole crowd locked and surged. A
gull went off like a thrown stone overhead; a horn
blared from the water; the press shoved, and shoved
back; and in the middle of it the boy was knocked
half off his feet, and put out a hand to catch him-
self — and a hand caught his elbow and steadied
him. A girl\{}’s hand. Small, and brown, and certain.
He felt it close on his arm and hold him up and let
him go, all in the space of a breath, and he turned
— and she had already half-turned toward him, the
steadying done before she had thought to do it, as
she had reached for a hundred falling things — and
for the length of a single heartbeat they were face
to face in the roaring crowd, the still girl from the
steep streets and the clever boy from the glass hill,
eleven years old, an arm\{}’s length apart, looking
straight at one another.
  And something almost happened.



                         227

   Neither of them ever had a word for it, because
neither of them ever remembered it, because there
was nothing in either of them left able to know what
it was. But for that one heartbeat a low note and
a high note hung in the same small pocket of air
for the first time since the world began, an octave
apart, and very nearly — very nearly — sounded.
The girl felt a warmth go through her that she had
no cause for, looking at a strange rich boy she had
steadied and would never see again, a warmth like
the warmth of the call coming down across the roofs,
here, absurdly, in a stranger\{}’s face. The boy felt
the nameless ache that surfaced only at night rise up
in him in broad afternoon and stand wide open and
almost — almost — find its object, so that he opened
his mouth, with no idea on earth what he meant to
say, to say something to her. And the chord leaned
toward its sounding. And the whole turning machin-
ery of two lives leaned with it.
   It did not sound. The crowd, which had pressed
them together, tore them apart in the very same
breath; a wall of strangers drove between them; the
boy was carried back toward the rail and the tu-
tor\{}’s shout, the girl forward with the surge to-
ward her grandmother\{}’s reaching hand; and the
heartbeat ended, and the moment closed over, and
the barrier that ran between the two of them — the
same barrier that runs between the two seas where
the waters meet and will not mix, that they had been
born one on each side of — held, as it had been made
to hold, as it would go on holding for years. Each of
them turned, once, twice, scanning the crowd, sud-

                        228

denly and inexplicably bereft, hunting a face they
could not even picture, finding only the thousand
indifferent faces of the afternoon. The boy let the
tutor take his shoulder and steer him to the car.
The girl let her grandmother lean on her arm again.
And by the time they had each gone twenty steps,
the strange taut brightness had begun to fade, as a
dream fades; and by the time they slept that night
it was gone from both of them entirely, filed under
nothing, a small good melancholy with no story at-
tached.

                      •   • •

They had been, for one heartbeat at the meeting of
the waters, nearer to one another than they would
come again for six long years — nearer than the
search that was coming would manage for a very
long time — and they had not known it, and now they
never would. The cold thing knew it. It had been
there, at the boy\{}’s shoulder, and for that one
heartbeat it had felt the chord lean toward its sound-
ing and had gone rigid with something close to fear,
for it understood, as the children could not, what
would wake in the world if those two notes were
ever truly struck together; and when the crowd tore
them apart it had eased, and gloated, and told itself
the barrier would always hold. Far off, the gentle
ones who keep watch over such things had felt the
near-sounding too, and had ached, and hoped, and
gone on waiting. And the two children were carried
up into the city by its two separate streams, the low

                          229

note uphill into the steep poor streets, the high note
out toward the bright glass hill; and the waters met
below the bridge, and did not mix; and the long for-
getting closed back over the afternoon as the tide
closes over a stone.



                  BOOK TWO · ACT III
                       • • •




                 The Whisperer


          From the evil of the whisperer who
                    withdraws —


         who whispers in the breasts of men.




                       • • •


The Qur’an · Sūrah an-Nās (114:4–5)




                         230

###SECTION: Chapter Thirty — The Far Country###
The Far Country

 They told him it was the greatest gift they could
give him, and they were not lying, and it was also
the loneliest thing that had ever been done to him;
and the strange double truth of that — that the same
act could be a gift and an exile in the one breath
— was the first genuinely grown-up thing he ever
understood, and he understood it at thirteen, in an
airport, watching his father shake his hand.
  For they were sending him away. To the best
school in the world, his father said, and said it as
he said all the things he was proud of, as though
the saying of it conferred something on the room;
a school in England older than the Ottoman house
they were standing to leave, a place that had made
prime ministers and poets and the men who ran
the world, and that would now, at no small cost
and after no small effort, make him. It was an hon-
our. It was the door to everything. And it was also
a cold stone country two thousand miles from the
only light he had ever lived in, where he would live
among strangers, and be a stranger, and sleep in

                        231

a row of beds full of boys he had never met, and
not see his mother\{}’s quick lovely distracted face,
or the strait, or the gulls, or the steep bright city,
for the better part of a year. His father shook his
hand — shook it, did not hold him — and told him
to make them proud, and to remember whose son
he was, and that was the whole of the goodbye; and
his mother kissed him in a cloud of scent and was al-
ready, he could see, half gone to wherever she was
going next; and then the glass doors closed between
him and the two of them, and he was, for the first
time in his life, entirely on his own in the world.
   He did not cry. He had learned already, in the
glass house, that there was no profit in it; there was
no one there to be moved by it, and so he had quietly
stopped, years before, and he did not begin again
now. He turned, and squared his narrow shoulders
inside the new stiff coat that had been bought a size
too large so that he might grow into it, and he car-
ried his own grief as he would carry every grief for
the next twenty years: folded small, and put away
somewhere high up where it would not show, and
reasoned about. It will be interesting, he told him-
self, on the plane, watching his city tilt and shrink
and vanish under the wing. I will learn a great deal.
And both of those things were true, and neither of
them was what he felt, and the gap between the
thing he told himself and the thing he felt was an-
other of the small rooms he was learning, that year,
to live above instead of in.


                      •   • •

                          232

England was grey, and it rained, and the rain was
not like the warm theatrical rain of home but a thin
grey persistence that did not so much fall as sim-
ply be everywhere, all the time, in the stone and the
air and the bones; and the school, when the car at
last brought him to it down a mile of dripping limes,
was beautiful and ancient and cold in a way he had
not known a building could be cold — cold not in its
temperature, which was managed, but in its soul, in
the centuries of it, in the rows of dead headmasters
glaring down from gilt frames and the Latin cut into
the lintels and the great hush of a place so sure of
itself that it did not need to raise its voice. He stood
in the entrance hall with his too-large coat and his
single case and his careful unbreakable face, and
he felt the whole weight of the place look at him,
this small brown foreign boy with the difficult name,
and find him, for the moment, wanting; and some-
thing in him — the reaching thing, the hungry thing,
the thing that could not bear to be found wanting
— stood up inside him and answered the great cold
hush, silently, with the first vow he ever made en-
tirely on his own: very well. We will see.
   The first months were the worst of his life so far,
and he told no one, then or ever. Boys are a nation
with their own cruel and ancient law, and the law
has no mercy at all for the outsider, and he was
the most outside thing the school had seen in years:
foreign, and rich in the wrong way, and clever in
a manner that the English boys had been carefully
taught to find distasteful, and possessed of a name
they would not trouble to pronounce and a faith they

                          233

knew nothing of except that it marked him further
out. They were not, most of them, monstrous; that
was almost the worst of it; they were simply care-
less, in the bottomless way of the young and the se-
cure, and their carelessness fell on him like the grey
rain, everywhere, all the time, in a thousand small
dismissals that no single one of which he could ever
have pointed to and called a wound. He lay in the
dark in the long cold dormitory among the breath-
ing of strangers and he was lonelier than he had
known a person could be and live, and he reached,
in that dark, for the warmth that had used to surface
in him at home — and found it further off than ever,
buried now under exile as well as everything else —
and he set his teeth, and did not weep, and began,
with the whole force of his frightened brilliant mind,
to plan how he would win.

                       •   • •

And he won. That was the thing about him, the thing
that was true his whole life and that makes him so
hard to tell rightly: that you wanted to follow him. It
did not happen all at once. But he was watchful, and
he was quick, and he learned the place as he learned
everything, from the underside, finding the seams —
learned its codes, its cruelties, its secret terrors and
its things-not-said — and he found that the very gifts
that had marked him out as prey could be turned,
with care, into the things that made a boy a king.
He was funny; he learned to be funny in their lan-
guage, dry and quick and merciless, and a boy who

                           234

can make the hard boys laugh is halfway to safe. He
was brave — genuinely, recklessly brave, in the way
of someone who has decided that the worst has al-
ready happened and there is therefore nothing left
to be afraid of — and boys worship courage above
every other thing. And he had that quality that can-
not be taught and cannot be bought and that the
cold school for all its centuries could not manufac-
ture in its sons: he had fire. He walked into a room
and the room turned. Within a year the boys who
had dismissed him were telling stories about him;
within two, the dormitory that had frozen him out
would not settle at night until he had spoken; and he
discovered, with a feeling he mistook for happiness,
that he had become the thing he had vowed in the
cold hall to become — not safe, but more than safe:
wanted. Followed. Loved, even, in the rough coded
way of boys. And he was good at it, and it filled, for
an hour at a time, the place in him that nothing had
ever filled.
  He used it well, at first: he was not cruel with his
new power, as the cruel boys had been cruel; he re-
membered too exactly what the underside felt like.
There was a small boy in the year below, a stammer-
ing undersized creature whom the pack had marked
as packs do, and one grey afternoon Adel came
round a corner to find four of them at it, and he did
not walk past, though walking past would have cost
him nothing and stopping might have cost him ev-
erything he had built. He stopped. He put himself
between the small boy and the four, and he was so
cold, and so funny, and so absolutely without fear,

                         235

that he took the whole thing apart with words alone
in under a minute and sent the four of them away
looking like fools instead of lions; and the small boy
followed him about with wet adoring eyes for years
afterward, and Adel let him, and was gentle with
him, and never once made him feel the weight of
the debt. He could not have told you why he did it.
It came up out of the same deep buried place the
oldest question had once come from — a thing in
him that could not stand to see the strong grind
down the weak, that wanted, with a passion that
frightened him, to step in and set the crooked thing
straight. It was the best of him. It was the very thing
his name had meant. And it would, in the end, be the
part of him the enemy found it easiest to use — for
there is no vanity in all the world so subtle, or so
hard to repent of, as the vanity of the man who is
genuinely, and admirably, and gloriously good.

                       •   • •

For it cost him something, all of it, that he did not
see he was paying. The winning of the room was a
performance, and a performance must never stop;
and so he was never, now, not performing — never
simply himself, because he no longer entirely knew
what that would even be, the boy under the fire hav-
ing been folded away so small and so high up that
whole weeks went by without Adel visiting him. He
was surrounded, and admired, and alone in a way
that the loneliness of the first cold months had at
least been honest about; this new loneliness wore

                           236

a crowd like a coat. And he had learned the lesson
of the school all the way down into the marrow of
him, the lesson that would shape every choice of his
life: that to be needed you must be remarkable; that
weakness, shown, is death; that love is a thing you
earn, freshly, every single day, by being more than
the others, and that the day you are merely equal
to them is the day it stops. He did not know these
were beliefs. He thought they were simply as the
world was; he had the evidence of his own triumph
to prove it; and a clever boy with proof is the hardest
thing in creation to save from a wrong conclusion,
because he can always, always argue you down.
   The prayers were the first thing to go, and they
went so quietly that he could never afterward name
the day. He had kept them, at the start — a for-
eign boy alone in a cold country keeps the shape
of home about him as a man keeps a coat — had
washed and turned toward Mecca in the cramped
privacy of a bathroom stall while the English boys
clattered past outside, and felt, in those few stolen
minutes, a thread run taut between himself and the
steep bright city and his grandmother\{}’s God and
something older even than those. But the thread
was thin, and the place was cold to it, and there
was a voice — close, and reasonable, and entirely
in his own accent, so that he never once thought to
wonder whose it was — that found the doing of it
harder for him every week. You are exhausted, said
the voice, on the mornings he was exhausted. Do
it later; He will understand; He of all beings under-
stands a tired boy. You do not even know why you

                         237

do it, said the voice, on the mornings he was honest;
your grandmother does it, that is all; is a clever per-
son to arrange his life around a custom he cannot
defend? And the cleverest thing the voice ever said,
the thing that did the most work, it saved for the
nights the old nameless ache rose up in him sharp
as ever, and he half-turned, half-remembered, half-
reached toward the prayer as toward a thing that
might answer it: you, of all people, do not need a
crutch. And he was proud, and the word crutch did
its work, and the prayers grew further apart, and
were shorter, and were begun and abandoned, and
at last simply were not; and the thread between him
and the bright city went slack, and he did not feel
it go, because he was busy, and admired, and win-
ning, and because the only hours he might have felt
it were the hours he had taught himself to live above
instead of in.
   None of this was visible: from the outside he
was the most enviable boy in the school — brilliant,
beloved, magnetic, kind to the weak and deadly to
the cruel, marked from across a crowded hall as one
of the few the world makes way for. The masters
wrote his father letters that made his father glow.
And only at night, sometimes, when the dormitory
had finally let him stop, did the other thing surface:
the melancholy that had no cause and would not be
reasoned away, the sense of a vast and beautiful
country he had been exiled from that was not Istan-
bul, the ache for a warmth he had no memory of ever
having had and missed, now, with a grief that as-
tonished him, lying dry-eyed in the dark with every-

                         238

thing a boy could want and the one thing he could
not name. He was, his whole brilliant rising life, a
little in mourning. He never knew for what. And the
patient thing that lived at his ear, and had a great
deal of room to work in now, and was in no hurry
at all, watched the bright doomed magnificent boy
mourn in the dark for the very thing it was keeping
him from, and was well content, and let him sleep.

                       •   • •

He had not, in truth, climbed out of those first cold
months alone; and it matters to the whole of this
story to say who reached down a hand, because of
what that hand would one day be asked to do. For
when Adel first came to the school he had been thir-
teen, and small for it — the brown clever scholar-
ship boy two thousand miles from home, who did not
know one of the place’s thousand unwritten rules
and had no father near to teach him a single one —
and for a season he was exactly the kind of lost, raw,
frightened new boy that such a place exists to find
out and grind down.
   And there had been an older boy who would not
let it grind him. A year above Adel, already golden,
already fluent in the cruel grammar of the school,
belonging everywhere without effort in the way Adel
would have to fight for years to belong anywhere
— a boy who, for reasons Adel never fully under-
stood and never stopped being grateful for, crossed
a crowded hall to the unclaimed new arrival no-
body else had troubled with, and claimed him. His

                           239

name was Sami. He took the lost boy under his
wing: taught him which masters to fear and which
to charm, which of the older boys to avoid and which
to stand near, talked him through the first terrible
weeks when a single wrong word could have marked
him for years; and by the sheer warmth of him, and
the shelter of his easy belonging, made the unsur-
vivable suddenly survivable. Adel never forgot it. A
boy does not forget the one who was kind to him in
the place where no one was kind; and the debt of
those sheltered months sank so deep into him that
he would still be trying to repay it, at a cost that
would nearly destroy him and would, in the end, be
the saving of him, a lifetime and a whole world later,
in the worst hour he would ever know.




                         240

###SECTION: Chapter Thirty-One — The Last Nights###
The Last Nights

 By the time she was fifteen, the people of the four
streets had a quiet habit, when there was trouble in
a house, of sending for the girl.
   They could not always have told you why. She did
nothing that anyone could name; she had no cures,
and no wisdom beyond her years that she ever spoke
aloud, and she was, to look at, only a still grave girl
from a poor flat, neither pretty nor plain, who car-
ried the bread home and minded the little ones and
slipped into the mosque for the women\{}’s prayer
with her mother and her aunts. But when a baby
would not be comforted, or an old man lay fright-
ened in the last grip of a fever, or a young wife wept
and would not say why — sit with her awhile, some-
one would murmur, send the girl in — and she would
go, and settle herself, and be still; and the trouble
in the room would, after a while, grow quiet, as a
startled bird grows quiet in two cupped and patient
hands. She never thought of it as a thing she did.
She thought of it, in so far as she thought of it at
all, as simply going and being there. And the deep

                         241

grain of her, the turning-toward that ran on beneath
her whole quiet life, kept its old direction through
all of it, unremembered and unbroken; so that to
sit beside a frightened person and be unafraid was,
for her, only the same leaning she had always done,
pointed now at a person instead of at the sky.
   The one who had first seen it in her, all those years
ago, was her grandmother; and it was her grand-
mother, that year, who began to die.
   She was very old. No one knew quite how old; she
had outlived the records and most of her own teeth
and all of the people who could have said for cer-
tain, and she had been, for the whole of Hana\{}’s
life, the still warm centre of the loud warm flat — the
lap that was always free, the hands that had taught
the girl to fold her own, the cracked low voice that
had said the beautiful names of God over her in the
dark until the dark stopped being frightening. It was
she who had taken the child\{}’s two hands once, af-
ter the business with the screaming baby, and told
her that what she had was a gift, and that it came
from Him, and that one day she would learn the rea-
son it had been given. Hana had kept the words, as
she kept everything, in the still place. She had not
understood them. She understood them that winter,
all at once, and for the rest of her life, in the small
back room where the old woman lay dying through
the longest nights of the year.
   It came on slowly, and then not slowly. There
was no money for it to be anywhere but home,
and no wish in any of them that it should be; you
did not send your grandmother away to die among

                          242

strangers; she died where she had lived, in the back
room, with the noise of the family going on around
her and the smell of the cooking reaching her under
the door, and the whole crowded building taking its
turn on the stairs with soup, and prayers, and the
small fierce useless errands that love runs when it
cannot do the one thing it wants. And the family was
afraid. That was the thing Hana saw most clearly, in
those weeks: how afraid they all were, how the fear
came off them in the sickroom like heat off a stove
— her mother brisk and bright and terrified, forever
plumping the pillow that did not need plumping; her
aunts weeping in the kitchen and then fixing their
faces to go in; her brothers hovering in the doorway,
unable to enter, unable to leave. They loved the old
woman with their whole hearts, every one of them.
And their love, made frantic by their fear, filled the
little room until there was scarcely any air in it left
for the dying woman to do her dying in.
   And Hana remembered, then, the other thing her
grandmother had said, the part she had not under-
stood as a child: most people make a frightened per-
son more frightened, because they cannot bear the
fear, and they fuss. You can bear it. You can sit in-
side it and not run. She had not known, at six, what
it meant. She knew now. She watched her family
love their way into making the old woman\{}’s last
weeks harder, not from any failing but from the
sheer unbearable weight of caring and being able
to do nothing with it; and she understood that the
gift she had been given was not a gift for the easy



                         243

hours. It was for this. It was for exactly, and only,
this.

                       •   • •

So she took the nights.
   No one decided it; it simply became so, the way
water finds the low place. The others could not bear
the long dark hours in the sickroom, the hours when
the fear had nothing to push against and grew huge;
and Hana could; and so, by a kind of unspoken con-
sent, the nights became hers. She would settle her-
self on the low stool by the bed when the rest had
gone at last to their exhausted sleep, and she would
take the old woman\{}’s hand — light now, and dry,
and loose-skinned as a glove that no longer fit — and
she would simply be there, through the slow tick-
ing hours, while the building slept and the city slept
and the long winter night wore itself out toward the
first call. She did not chatter. She did not plump the
pillow. She did not pretend, as the others did with
their bright frightened voices, that the old woman
was not dying, or that dying was not happening, or
that everything was fine; for she had understood,
without anyone teaching her, that the lie is a kind
of abandonment, that to insist a frightened person
is fine is to leave them all alone with the truth. She
simply stayed, inside the truth, unafraid, and let her
grandmother not be alone in it.
   And her grandmother, who had been frightened
— for even the faithful are frightened, at the edge;
faith is not the absence of the fear but the going

                           244

toward Him through the middle of it — grew, in
the girl\{}’s company, by slow degrees, less afraid.
It was the thing she had done all her life with the
criers and the fevered and the weeping, only now it
was the person she loved most in the world, and the
stakes were the last ones there are; and it worked
as it had always worked, not by anything she said or
did but by the plain steady fact of her being there
and not running. The old woman would surface out
of the long grey reaches of her sleep and find the
girl\{}’s still face in the lamplight and the girl\{}’s
hand around hers, and something in her would let go
of a little more of its terror, and she would sink back
easier. You are still here, she said once, wonder-
ingly, in the small hours. You are always still here.
And Hana said, I am not going anywhere, grand-
mother; and the old woman closed her eyes, and be-
lieved her, and rested.
   There was a night, near the end, when she was
lucid for a long quiet hour — the strange clear lull
that often comes before the last — and she lay look-
ing at the girl in the lamplight with her old eyes
gone soft and far away, and she said: do you re-
member what I told you? That you would learn the
reason? Hana said that she remembered. The old
woman smiled, a small slow smile with not many
teeth in it and the whole of a life behind it. This is
the reason, she said. This. You will do this for peo-
ple who have no one. You will sit with them in the
dark when everyone else has run, and you will not
be afraid, and it will be the kindest thing anyone
has ever done for them, and most of them will never

                         245

know how rare it was. He made you for it. I saw it
when you were six. And she lifted the girl\{}’s hand,
with the last of her strength, and pressed her dry old
lips to the back of it, the elder kissing the child\{}’s
hand, which is a thing that is not done, which broke
something open in Hana\{}’s chest that would never
quite close again; and she said, thank you for stay-
ing with me. And that was the last wholly clear thing
she ever said.

                       •   • •

She died three nights later, in the blue dark before
the dawn, with the girl beside her.
   It was not terrible. Hana had braced, in some an-
imal part of her, for it to be terrible, and it was not;
it was the quietest thing she had ever seen. The old
woman had been far away for a day, breathing in the
slow ragged way that the family had been warned
of, and Hana had sat the whole of that last night ex-
actly as she had sat all the others, holding the loose
light hand, being there; and somewhere in the deep-
est trough of the night the breathing changed, and
gentled, and grew further and further apart, like
a clock running down, like footsteps walking away
over snow. And Hana did the only thing there was to
do, the thing she would do a thousand times in the
life that was coming and did not yet know was com-
ing: she leaned close, and she was unafraid, and she
said the beautiful names softly into the dim room
as the old woman had once said them to her in the
dark — giving them back, at the very end, to the

                           246

one who had given them — so that the last sound
her grandmother carried out of the world was the
same sound she had carried Hana into it with. And
the old woman, right at the end, with her eyes shut
and her face gone smooth and young, turned. Hana
felt it more than saw it: a small final settling, a lean-
ing, the whole of the spent old self tipping by some
last instinct toward a warmth that the girl could
not feel and the dying woman, it seemed, suddenly
and entirely could. And then the next breath simply
did not come. And the room was very still. And the
long forgetting that had begun for the old woman
eighty years before, in a back room not unlike this
one, with the call whispered into her newborn ear,
ended; and the remembering, Hana thought without
knowing why she thought it, the remembering must
surely now begin.
   She sat a while in the stillness, holding the hand
until it cooled, in no hurry, unafraid. She was emp-
tied; she had never in her life been so emptied; the
person who had been the warm centre of the whole
warm world was gone out of it, and the grief of
that would come, and did come, in its time, in great
waves, for a long while after. And yet — and this
was the thing she could never explain to anyone, the
thing that sat strangely beside the grief and did not
cancel it but did not yield to it either — she was,
in the very same hour, more full than she had ever
been. She had been let do the thing she was for. She
had stood at the very threshold, where almost every-
one runs, and she had not run, and her staying had
been a real thing, a thing that had mattered, per-

                          247

haps the only thing she had ever done that entirely
mattered. She did not have the words filled and emp-
tied for it; she was fifteen, and undone with sorrow,
and not given to fine phrases. But she felt the two
things sitting together in her, the great emptiness
and the great fullness, and she understood dimly
that they were somehow the same thing seen from
two sides; and that this, too — this strange complete-
ness on the far side of loss — was part of the reason
she had been made.

                       •   • •

There had been, all that winter, a thing at her own
ear — the near companion that is given to every
soul, that had been given to her too, and that had
found, in her, almost nothing it knew how to feed on,
and had grown thin and quiet and half-asleep across
the years of her steady turning. It had watched the
nights in the sickroom. It had felt her not run. And
something in it, on the night the old woman died,
that had been cold for longer than the city had stood,
was for the first time in its long starved existence
not quite cold — was moved, against everything it
was, by what it had been made to witness through
her; so that there began in it, that night, so slowly
that it would not bear fruit for years, the faint first
turning of a thing that even the gentle watchers had
not dared to hope for. And her guardian, who had
kept its post beside her since her first hour, and
who had no such turning to make because it had
never once been anything but turned, stood in the

                           248

still blue room over the two of them, the living girl
and the gone grandmother, and was filled to over-
flowing with something that, in a creature of that
kind, can only be called praise.
   Far off, on the other side of the turning world, a
boy her own age lay that same night in a long cold
dormitory, beloved and brilliant and surrounded
and entirely alone, being filled, day on day, with ev-
erything the world could pour into a person, and
growing, day on day, emptier; while here, in a poor
back room over four steep streets, a girl knelt by a
deathbed with nothing left and was somehow brim-
ming. Neither knew the other was alive. But the two
notes had been struck once, years before, at the
meeting of the waters, and not quite sounded; and
they were sounding now, each alone, in their two far
rooms, the high one hollowing and the low one fill-
ing — the same chord, the same hand, the same long
patient mercy working itself out by opposite roads
— and drawing, though neither could have dreamed
it, by every passing year a little nearer to the night,
still far off, when they would sound together at last,
and the whole of it would come, all at once, awake.

###SECTION: Chapter Thirty-Two — The Two Kings###
The Two Kings

 And in time — for Adel rose fast, once he had been
shown the way up — the sheltered new boy grew
into something nearer an equal, and then, in the
eyes of the school, a second king beside the first;
so that for the back half of their school years they
were the two bright princes of that place, and they
were happy, and it is important to say so before say-
ing anything else: that it was real, and that it was
good, and that for a while it was the best thing in
either of their lives.
   It had not begun as a rivalry; it had begun as a res-
cue, and only ripened, as Adel grew into his powers,
into the friendly ceaseless contest of two who were
each, by then, the only one who could truly push the
other. After those first sheltered weeks the watch-
ing between them had cracked open into laughter —
over what, neither of them could ever afterward re-
member, only that one of them had said the exactly
right merciless thing and the other had been unable
not to laugh — and that was the beginning of the
thing. After that they were inseparable. Where you

                         250

found the one you found the other; the school sim-
ply learned to speak of them in a single breath; and
the long cold corridors that had nearly frozen Adel
out in his first raw season now arranged themselves,
without quite knowing they were doing it, around
the daily question of where the two of them were
and what the two of them were going to do.
   Sami was the golden one. That was the word ev-
eryone reached for, and it was the right one: there
was a warmth and an ease and a brightness to him
that drew people as a lit window draws them on
a dark road, an effortless belonging that Adel, for
all his fire, would never quite have — for Adel had
won his place, by force, against the grain, and you
could still see the grain; whereas Sami simply be-
longed, everywhere, instantly, as though the world
had been arranged for his comfort before he arrived
in it. He came of one of those glittering cosmopoli-
tan families with a famous name and a thinning for-
tune, a family that lived on the memory of grandeur
and the charm of its children; and Sami had been
raised, without anyone ever saying it aloud, to un-
derstand that charm was the family trade now, that
being adored was not a vanity but a livelihood, that
the day the room stopped turning to him was the day
the floor went out from under the whole gilded pre-
carious enterprise. He was, in his way, as frightened
as Adel. He simply wore his fear as gold, where Adel
wore his as fire; and that difference was the whole
of their friendship, and the whole of its ruin.
   For each of them had exactly the thing the other
lacked, and could not stop being aware of it. Adel

                        251

had depth — the buried reaching, the real questions,
the fire that came up out of some genuine deep place
and made his cleverness matter in a way mere bril-
liance never does; and Sami felt it, and envied it, in
the small hours, with a shame that astonished him,
because he loved Adel, he genuinely loved him, and
a man should not envy what he loves. And Adel had,
in Sami, the first sight in his whole guarded life of
someone he did not have to perform for — some-
one who could do everything Adel could do, who
saw all his tricks because he had the same ones,
before whom the exhausting endless performance
could, for an hour at a time, simply stop. He told
Sami things he had never told a living soul. Not the
deepest things; he had no language for the deepest
things, and would not for years; but more than he
had ever told anyone, more than he had known he
had to tell. He let the wall down, an inch, for the
first time since the glass doors had closed between
him and his father. It was the bravest thing he had
done since coming to the cold country, and the most
dangerous, and he never knew it was either.

                      •   • •

It was not always noble between them. There was an
evening, mid-way through that second year, when
Sami came back from a weekend at some cousin’s
estate glowing with a story about a girl, an actual
girl, who had apparently found him extraordinary,
and could not stop repeating one particular thing
she had said about his laugh; and Adel, who had

                          252

never in his life been told anything of the kind by
anyone who was not being paid in some fashion to
say it, felt something small and ugly rear up in him
that had nothing to do with fire or depth or any of
the things he told himself made him the better man.
He said, lightly, in the exact tone he used for deliver-
ing a killing line in debate, that it must be a very low
bar wherever that estate was. It landed harder than
he meant it to. He saw it land — saw the glow go out
of Sami’s face for one unguarded second — and he
did not take it back. He let it sit there, because some
small mean part of him that he would have denied
under torture had wanted, just once, to be the one
who put the weather in the room instead of merely
enduring it. Sami laughed it off a moment later, the
way Sami laughed everything off, and the evening
went on as though nothing had happened. But Adel
lay awake afterward turning it over, not with any
of his usual clean high-minded self-correction, only
with the flat, unheroic recognition that he had been
petty on purpose, for no reason, to the one person
in the cold country who had never once been cruel
to him first; and he did not apologize the next morn-
ing, or the one after that, and after a while it was too
late to without making it strange, and so he simply
never did.

                       •   • •

The thing they both wanted came down, in the end,
to a single afternoon in their last year, and a single
name read out from a single envelope.

                           253

   The school had a custom, very old and very prized,
of sending one boy each year — only one — to
a national contest of speech and argument that
the whole grand machinery of the place existed, in
some sense, to win; and to be the one chosen was
not merely an honour but a door, the kind of door
that opens onto the rest of a life: the universities
watched it, the old men in the gilt frames seemed
to lean down out of their canvases for it, careers
had been founded on it. And in that year there was
no question of who the two contenders were. It was
the two kings. They had built the thing together, in
truth — had spent two years sharpening each other
in the debating hall until each was twice the speaker
he would have been alone, trading arguments and
angles and the cold art of taking an opponent\{}’s
case apart at the joints — and the cruel arithmetic of
the custom was that, having made one another, they
now had to be set against one another, because the
door was wide enough for only one.
   Adel did a thing, in the run-up to it, that tells you
who he still was under everything. He found, the
week before the final trial, the argument — the one
angle, the unanswerable line, the thing that would
certainly win — and he was so pleased with it, and
so used to sharing everything with Sami, and so gen-
uinely, foolishly sure that the friendship was the
larger thing, that he gave it away. He showed it
to Sami the night before, the whole beautiful struc-
ture of it, as freely as one shows a friend a thing
one has made. He told himself it did not matter who
used it; that they were better than the contest; that

                          254

whichever of them won, the two of them won. It was
the most generous thing he ever did. It was the thing
his name had meant. And it was the opening through
which everything came.
   For Sami used it. The next afternoon, before the
masters and the leaning portraits, with Adel sitting
three feet away waiting his turn, Sami stood and
laid out Adel\{}’s argument — the structure, the
angle, the unanswerable line — as his own, calmly
and beautifully and to devastating effect, so that
when Adel rose afterward and began, of necessity,
the same case, it was a hollow second hearing of
a thing the room had already been won by, and he
knew within a sentence that it was over, and that he
had handed Sami the knife himself. The envelope,
when it came, had Sami\{}’s name in it. Of course
it did. And Sami crossed the floor through the ap-
plause and embraced him, and said into his ear, low
and warm and almost weeping, I\{}’m sorry, I\{}’m
so sorry, it just came out of me, I didn\{}’t plan it —
forgive me — and the terrible thing, the thing Adel
turned over for years afterward in the dark, was
that he believed him. It had not been a plot. It was
worse than a plot. It was simply the gold doing what
gold does when the floor opens under it: reaching,
without quite deciding to, for the nearest thing that
would hold its weight — and the nearest thing had
been Adel\{}’s outstretched, trusting hand.


                       •   • •




                           255

Adel forgave him. That, too, tells you who he was.
He did not rage, or expose him, or break the thing in
public, though he could have done all three and the
room would have been his if he had; he looked at his
friend\{}’s stricken weeping face and saw, with the
awful clear-sightedness that was the best and the
worst of him, that Sami had not done it from mal-
ice but from terror, that the gold was hollow and
afraid, that to destroy him now would be to grind
a weak thing down — and the one act his deepest
self could not perform was that one. So he smiled,
and embraced him back, and said it was nothing,
that the best speaker had won, that he was glad
for him; and he meant the mercy if not the words;
and the friendship went on. But it went on changed,
with a thing under it now that neither of them ever
named, a hairline crack run clean through the floor
of it; and they both knew the crack was there, and
walked carefully around it for the rest of their time,
and were never, after that afternoon, quite the two
kings again.
   Adel took from the best and most generous act of
his life exactly the wrong lesson, and he took it all
the way down. He did not conclude that he should
choose his friends more wisely, or that Sami was
weak, or even that he himself had been unlucky. He
concluded that he had been a fool. That the wall he
had let down an inch was the inch through which
the knife had come; that the trusting, the sharing,
the letting-in, the soft generous underside of him —
that was the flaw, that was the wound waiting to be
made, that was the thing the world punished. The

                         256

lesson of the cold country, the lesson he had been
learning since the glass doors closed, came down on
him now with the full weight of proof behind it: that
to show the underside is to be cut there; that love is
a competition like everything else; that the only safe
man is the one who needs no one and stands alone
at the top and lets nobody, ever again, close enough
to reach the soft place. He did not become cruel.
He never became cruel; that was not in him. He be-
came, instead, something quieter and sadder and
far harder to save: a generous man with a locked
room at the centre of him, who would give you any-
thing in the world except the key.
   The patient voice at his ear had not needed to
say one word through the whole of it. It had only
watched, and waited, and let the world do its own
work, which is the cleverest thing it knows how
to do; and then, in the quiet afterward, when Adel
lay turning the betrayal over in the dark and draw-
ing his terrible wrong conclusion, it had murmured,
very softly, in his own voice, the only thing it needed
to: you see. You always knew, really. You are alone;
you have always been alone; it is only that now
you are wise enough to stop pretending otherwise.
And Adel, who was clever, and who had the proof,
agreed; and pulled the last warm thing in him a lit-
tle further back behind the wall; and the thread that
ran between him and a bright city and his grand-
mother\{}’s God and a warmth he could not remem-
ber went slacker still, and he did not feel it, because
the only hours he might have felt it were the hours
he no longer let himself have.

                         257

                      •   • •


He was seventeen that summer, and beautiful, and
brilliant, and bound for the best of everything, and
more alone than he had ever been; and he stood
one evening at the end of the term at a high win-
dow of the old school looking out over the grey En-
glish country going gold in the late light, with the
whole shining ruin of the friendship behind him and
the whole shining empty future in front, and felt the
old nameless melancholy rise up in him like water
in a well — the grief with no object, the ache for
the country he had never seen — and reached for
it, once, as you reach in a dream; and could not
find it; and turned from the window, and went down
to be charming at dinner. Far away, on the other
side of the turning world, a girl of the same age
who had lately knelt by a deathbed and learned that
the whole of her life was to be the staying, the not-
running, the keeping of faith with people right to
the last dark — a girl who had been taught by every-
thing she was that you do not let go of the hand,
ever, whatever it costs you — was walking home
that same evening through the steep bright streets
with the call coming down across the roofs, and turn-
ing her face up to it, and being, in her poverty, and
her grief, and her plainness, more whole than the
most envied boy in England. The two notes went on
sounding, each alone, by their opposite roads. And
the night they would sound together was closer now
by a year; and neither of them knew there was a
song at all.
