###SECTION: Dream Fragment###
He did not call them dreams, even to himself. He
called them, when the matter came up at all, which
was almost never, the old films — as though naming
them something manufactured and harmless could
keep them from meaning what some closed-off part
of him already suspected they meant.

           The Whisper at the Edge

Adel went, in the end, to the edge by himself.
   He could not afterward have said why he went, or
even, quite, that he had decided to go. The hunger
was in him from the veiled ones, sharp and unsatis-
fied. The grain was in his shoe from the old light’s
riddle, and from the fool’s truth wrapped in far too
much paper, and from a dozen smaller withholdings
he had stopped troubling to count. And the warmth
of Sami, which had once quieted all of it, had begun
of late to feel less like an answer to his questions
than like a bright pleasant room he could step into
to avoid them. So, restless, leaning, dissatisfied, he
drifted — further out than he had ever gone before.
Past the bright gardens. Past the warm thronged
company of the other souls. Out toward the cold

                        259

thin edge of the country, where the warmth ran low
and grew sparse and uncertain, and where few souls
ever cared to go, because there was nothing out
there to be near. He told himself, the whole long
way, that he was only thinking. He was always only
thinking. It is a remarkable thing, how far a soul can
walk while assuring itself that it is only thinking, and
has not, in any sense that could ever be held against
it, truly gone anywhere at all.
   And at the edge, something was waiting for him.
It had been waiting, in truth, since the very first in-
stant of his existence — patient past all telling, pa-
tient as stone is patient — but never until now had
it been given the smallest cause to believe that the
long waiting was nearly done.


                       •   • •


It did not look at him directly. That was the first
thing, and the cleverest. It looked, somehow, just
past him — at some thing always slightly more in-
teresting a little beyond his shoulder — so that Adel
felt, in the very same moment, both the cool absence
of its full attention and a sudden, surprising, wholly
unfamiliar ache to earn it. And when it spoke — for it
could speak; it was older and other than the souls, a
thing of fire and not of waiting clay, and the shaping
of meaning into the clean hard edges of speech cost
it nothing at all — its voice was quiet, and reason-
able, and entirely, perfectly without menace; which
is the only kind of voice that has ever, in all the his-


                           260

tory of all the worlds, talked a single soul out of par-
adise.
   ”You ask better questions than the others,” it said.
   Adel had never, in all his short life, been called
anything that was his and his alone. He had been
a note within a chord; a friend among friends; one
young bright soul among uncountable young bright
souls. And now a voice from outside the whole of it —
a voice that seemed to come from somewhere higher
and older and wiser than the warm crowded country
he knew — had reached out and named aloud the
one thing he had always, secretly, in the deepest and
least admitted chamber of himself, believed to be
true about him. And it had named it not as a charge,
but as praise.
   ”They feel,” the voice went on, unhurried, in no
haste at all. ”They accept. They lean. It is beautiful,
in its way; the flock has its own grace, I would never
deny it. But you — you ask. You measure. You want
to know why one thing and not another; why a veil
here and a brightness there; why you should be told
in time, as though you were a child, when you have
every right to be told now. The others do not mind
the dark. I have listened a long, long while, in this
country, and I have not once heard a single one of
them mind it as that you do. It is no flaw in you, this
thing you carry. Do not let them tell you that it is.
It is the rarest thing in all this bright and incurious
country — and I should be sorry, more sorry than
you know, to see you talked out of it.”
   And here it told the truth — which is the most
dangerous thing that it ever does, and the thing the

                         261

careless never expect of it, having been taught to
look only for its lies. For Adel did mind the dark.
He did want to know. He had carried the sharp and
lonely difference of it in himself since his very first
counting, in his first waking instant, and had never
once been able to set it down. And to have that thing
seen — seen, and named, and admired — by some-
thing that appeared to understand him more truly
in a single passing moment than all his friends had
understood him in the whole long warmth of their
play, loosened something deep inside him; some-
thing that he would afterward spend the better part
of four worlds trying, and failing, to tie shut again.
   ”Your companion,” the voice said — and at the
word Adel’s whole attention sharpened to a point,
for there was only one soul in all the country it could
mean. ”The low sweet note; the one you became a
chord with, in the bright quarter, when you were
new. I have felt her. She is lovely; I will not insult you
by pretending otherwise. But ask yourself — hon-
estly, now, in the way that only you, of all of them,
are able to be honest — has she once, even once,
asked why? She leans. She is content. She takes
whatever is given her, and turns toward the Giver,
and is” — and here it chose its single word with
great, and tender, and unhurried care — ”happy.
Why, it is even her name, is it not? Bliss. Content-
ment. Ease.”
   It let the name lie there between them a moment
— the gift turned weapon, the blessing turned to a
small cold blade — set down so gently, so admir-
ingly, that it sounded for all the world like praise;

                           262

and only much later, far too much later, turning it
through and through in the dark, as he turned ev-
erything over, would Adel taste at last the contempt
that had been folded down inside it like poison in-
side a sweet. Her bright, true, given name — made,
by nothing more than the tone of a voice, to mean
something small. Made to mean: she does not think.
She does not strive. She is the easy road, the gen-
tle slope, the path for those who cannot manage the
climb. You, now — you, who ask, who measure, who
mind the dark — you are something else. Something
rarer. Something more.
   ”That is no fault in her, you understand,” the voice
added, kindly, as if anxious that he should not think
it unkind. ”She was made for the easy way, and the
easy way is a mercy — a real one — for those souls
who are able to walk it. But you were not made for
the easy way. You were made for something harder,
and higher, and lonelier. Some souls, my friend, are
born to obey. And some — a very few — are born
to know. I would have said as much to you a great
while ago, but I did not wish to offend you with what
would have sounded like mere flattery, before you
were ready to hear it for the plain and simple truth
that it is.”
   Adel’s questioning — the endless restless engine
of it, the thing that never stopped — had gone en-
tirely silent. He was listening now with that partic-
ular open and defenseless helplessness with which
we listen, all of us, only ever to the one who appears
to have understood us before we had managed to un-
derstand ourselves. And in him, quiet and new and

                         263

terribly sweet, a small private warmth had kindled
and begun to glow: the warmth of being seen; of be-
ing singled out from the uncountable; of being told,
by a voice that seemed to know, that the sharp and
lonely and difficult thing he had carried alone since
the beginning was not a wound to be healed or a flaw
to be forgiven, but a crown — a thing to be borne up
high, and proudly, and apart.
   The voice did not press. It knew far better than
to press; it had had whole ages in which to learn
the patience of not pressing. It said only, lightly,
as though half to itself, already beginning to turn
away: ”Well. I have kept you far too long. It was
good — it was rare — to find, in this place, a soul
who could truly think.” And it withdrew. Not sharply,
not all at once, but as the tide withdraws from a
shore: slowly, smoothly, without any visible moment
of leaving, drawing back and back and leaving ev-
erything it had carried in arranged just so upon the
sand, exactly where it would be found again. No
name had been given. No farewell was made. Adel
did not know what manner of thing he had spoken
with, out there at the cold edge of everything. He
knew only that he had been recognized; that the
recognition had been warmer and finer than any he
had been given in all his life; and that the one who
had given it had not lingered quite long enough to
be asked a single question in return.
   In the deep place of him the seed lay where it
had fallen, in the dark, in the warm soil of his own
most secret pride. He had no inkling that it had been
planted. He did not feel it as a wound; he felt it, if

                         264

he felt it at all, as a gift. He thought only that he had
had a remarkable conversation — the most remark-
able of his existence — and that someone, at the last,
had seen him exactly as he had always most wished
to be seen. He did not go and tell Hana. He did not
go and tell Hilal. He told no one at all. He kept it —
the first thing he had ever in his life kept — and in
the keeping of it, down in the dark and the warmth
and the silence, the seed put out its first thin, pale,
questing root.

                        •   • •

Far off, in the warm heart of the country, Hana felt
a cold pass over her light.
   Where it had come from, she could not have said.
It was simply there, and then it reached for her —
a thin questing thread of something that was not
the country’s warmth at all; something that had
touched, somewhere out at the edges, a thing she
loved, and come away with a chill on it, and brought
the chill to her. It was beyond her understanding.
She had no name for it, and no notion of what could
make so cold a thing in a place that was made of
warmth. She did what she always did with whatever
she could not understand: she turned — toward the
radiance that no soul could look upon, and toward
which every last thing leaned — and in its warmth
she lifted up, wordlessly, the only thing she knew to
lift: him. The high quick note of her chord. Wherever
he had wandered; whatever the cold had wanted
with him out there in the dark. She gathered him

                            265

into the warmth and held him there, a moment, in
the wordless and unthinking way that the worlds
to come would one day learn to call a prayer, and
would never quite manage to do better than she did
it now, untaught and unaware.
   The cold thinned. The cold frayed. The cold was
gone.
   And she did not know that she had done anything
at all. She turned back to the long lit now, and the
chord in her went quiet and warm again, and she
never even thought to mention the cold to anyone —
for she had no words to mention it in, and the only
soul she might have told was the very one that it
had come from: still out at the cold edge of things,
still warm with the new warmth of having been seen,
still turning it in the dark, like a stone in the hand, a
sentence about who is born to obey and who is born
to know.

                        •   • •

So the first battle of the long war was fought; and
neither of the two it was fought over knew that any
battle had been fought at all. The one came away
wounded, and believed that he had been crowned.
The other turned a deadly chill aside with a single
wordless motion of her love, and believed that she
had done nothing whatever. And between the two
of them the seed lay rooting quietly in the dark; and
the cold drew back to its edge well satisfied — more
than satisfied — for it had learned, on that day, the
two things it had most needed to know: that the

                            266

bright clever one could be made to keep a secret;
and that the still sweet one would, without ever once
waking to what she did, turn aside for him a cold
that he himself had walked all the long way out to
the edge to embrace.
   It would be a long war. It would be longer than
either of them could have begun to imagine, and
it would cross worlds. But it had begun now, in
earnest, at the cold edge of the first of all the worlds.
And of the two sides in it, only one, as yet, so much
as knew that there was a war at all.
   God is the Light of the heavens and the earth.


                        •   • •


The Qur’an · Sūrah an-Nūr (24:35)



                         ACT III
                        • •       •




                        • •       •


               The Greater Lights

He woke, always, with his own voice half-formed
in his throat, some word he could never afterward
catch the whole of, and lay in the dark of the cold

                            267

school dormitory turning over, without knowing he
was doing it, the exact same question the whisper
had once asked him: whether he was, in fact, not
like the others.




                      268

###SECTION: Dream Fragment — This one came to him only twice in his whole boy-###
This one came to him only twice in his whole boy-
hood, and both times in the same week he had, in
the daylight world, done something small and mean
and gotten away with it cleanly.

               The Light of Yusuf

If Maryam had been purity, then the next of the
veiled ones that Hilal showed them was beauty —
and beauty, they were about to learn, can wound a
soul far more easily, and far more deeply, than pu-
rity ever could; for purity humbles, and the humbled
soul is, for that moment, safe; but beauty tempts,
and the tempted soul stands in the gravest danger
of all, and does not feel itself to be in any danger
whatever.
   He was called Yusuf; and when his light passed
— they bent their seeing for him too, though he was
bearable in a way that Maryam, in her terrible clean-
ness, had not quite been — the whole bright coun-
try seemed to draw in one long breath, and hold it.
Everything was, for the space of his passing, a lit-
tle more beautiful than it had been the moment be-
fore: the colors of the gardens deepened and grew

                        269

rich; the far high music sweetened; the very warmth
at the heart of all things seemed to lean a degree
nearer, to brighten, to want — as though even the
country itself, even the warmth itself, were not en-
tirely above wishing to be looked at as that one
looked at him. He was the kind of beauty that does
not turn your eye back upon itself, that does not say
look at me. He was the rarer and the higher kind —
the kind that makes you think, helplessly, clean past
it: there must be a Maker; and the Maker must be
very great, and generous beyond all reckoning, to
have spent so much of Himself, so freely, upon the
mere adorning of the world.
   That was how Hana received him. The beauty
went into her, and through her, and out the far side
of her, and pointed — like an arrow loosed and flying
— clean past itself: if this is only the gift, she thought,
with no words to think it in, then what, what must
the Giver be? And she followed the arrow, as she
followed everything, all the way home. She did not
want to keep the beauty. It never once occurred to
her to want to keep it. She wanted only to find Some-
one to thank for it.
   Sami received it altogether differently. Sami
looked upon the most beautiful thing that he had
ever beheld, or ever would, and what rose up in him
— hot, and instant, and helpless — was not thank the
Giver. It was a single naked cry, flung up out of the
deepest place in him before he could think to gov-
ern it: How can anything be so beautiful? I want to
be like that. I want to be that.



                           270

   It seemed a small thing. It seemed, almost, a kind
of praise — and had anyone accused Sami, in that
moment, of anything worse than an excess of admi-
ration, he would have been honestly bewildered. But
Hilal, watching, felt his old deep light go cold and
still with grief; for he had seen this exact wish be-
fore, in soul after soul after soul, across the long
uncounted ages of his watching, and he knew it for
the disease that it was, and he knew the whole cruel
course that it ran. The wish to be beautiful like Yusuf
is innocent. There is no harm in it, at the first. But
it does not stay the wish to be beautiful. It curdles
— so slowly, so gradually, by so fine and so invisi-
ble a turning that the soul which hosts it never once
catches the moment of the change — into the wish
to be admired like Yusuf: to be the one the whole
country holds its breath for; the one the eye goes to
without being asked; the one whose mere passing
makes all other things, for a moment, a little less.
And that second wish, unlike the first, has no bot-
tom to it anywhere. It cannot be fed enough. It can-
not truly be fed at all; every mouthful of admiration
only widens the hunger; and in the end it eats the
very soul that hosts it, hollows it out from within —
and, this is the cruelest turn of all, calls the eating
love.
   Hilal said none of this. There are warnings that,
spoken too soon and to the wrong soul, become only
a map to the very ruin they had meant to prevent;
and besides, Sami, in that hour, would not have
heard a word of it, and would have heard in the
very trying only envy, or rebuke. So the old light

                         271

only grieved, quietly, where none of them could see;
and let the wish lie where it had fallen, in the warm
dark of Sami’s pride; and resolved, instead, to be
near — later, much later, when it would come to mat-
ter most — if, when that hour came, there were any
nearness left at all that Sami would still allow him.
   Adel felt the pull of the beauty too. Of course he
felt it; he was made to be pulled, made to lean, made
to want to come nearer to every high thing he was
shown. And he felt himself begin to lean toward it
now in the old hungry way, the calculating way, as
that asked how near might I come, and what would
I have to be — and then, for the first time in all
his existence, the lean caught. It caught upon some-
thing. It caught upon Maryam — upon the lesson of
the purest thing, still raw in him, which had taught
him, against everything he had believed, that the
very highest height is not seized but received; not
climbed to but given; not earned by any asking at
all but poured into the one who has made room. He
did not understand it fully. He could not have ex-
plained it had he tried. But he held the beauty, this
once, at a small and a deliberate distance; and was
glad, obscurely, that he held it so; and did not know
— could not have known — that the holding was the
first small thing that he had ever done, in all his life,
to save himself.
   And at the cold edge of the country, where the
warmth ran thin and the patient watcher kept its
long vigil, the watching thing felt the bright soul
called Sami cry out, in the silence of his own light,
I want to be that — and it knew the cry, at once

                          272

and intimately, for exactly what it was; because the
cry was kin, close kin, to its own oldest cry, the cry
it had been nursing in itself in secret since before
ever the gardens were lit. And it marked the widen-
ing of the crack in Sami with the quiet, unhurried
satisfaction of a thing that has waited a very long
while, and has begun, at last, to see its patience re-
paid. It did nothing. It never needed to do very much.
It only watched the door in that bright soul swing,
of its own slow accord, a little further open; and it
folded itself down again into the cold; and it bided;
and it was content.
   He never told Sami about these, though Sami was
the one person alive he told almost everything to.
Some part of him understood, without being able to
say why, that this particular dream was not for shar-
ing — that it belonged to a ledger he had already,
even that young, begun keeping in a drawer no one
else was permitted to open.




                         273

###SECTION: Dream Fragment###
Once — only once in all his years at the cold school
— he had this one, and it left him unable to speak
at breakfast, so that Sami asked him twice if he was
well.

                The Crown Light

And then Hilal brought them to the last and the high-
est of all the things that they would be shown; and
it was not a thing that they could see at all.
   They felt it long before they neared it. A presence
so vast, so deep, so wholly beyond the measure of
anything they had yet been shown, that the veiled
ones themselves — Maryam in her terrible purity,
Yusuf in his breath-stealing beauty, the small fierce
one who loved for nothing — seemed, beside it, no
more than small lamps carried out into the open at
the first grey of dawn — lamps that had been pre-
cious, and sufficient, and lovely all the long night,
and were now, of a sudden, almost nothing, swal-
lowed up in the coming of a far greater light that
they had had no part in making. For they had been
brought nearer here to the Source of all things than
to anything they had yet been shown — nearer than

                        274

the gathered lights, nearer than the Tree at the bor-
der of everything, nearer than they had ever known
a made thing could be brought and still remain a
made thing. What it was, they were not told; the
naming of it was not given to them, and some things
are veiled in the very midst of their nearness, pre-
cisely so that a small soul will not snatch at them
and name them wrongly. They knew only that here
was the brightness beside which every other bright-
ness in all the worlds stood revealed at last as a far,
and a borrowed, and a lesser thing; the nearness to-
ward which the long leaning of everything had, from
the very first, been leaning. They could not see it.
To see it was not possible for things made as they
were made. They could only stand within the fact of
it — as one stands, at dawn, with one’s eyes shut
fast against the brightness, and knows, through the
very lids, through the warmth on the face and the
red glow behind the eyes, that the sun has surely
come.
   Everything went still. The far high music stilled.
The gardens stilled. The leaning of all things stilled.
And at the cold edge of the country, in the thin
places, the watching thing — the proud old patient
thing that had always, until now, a clever answer
ready for every height, a measure to take of every
greatness, a ledger to enter every glory in — the
watching thing fell silent too. For one whole moment
it had nothing. No answer, no measure, no account.
And the silence of that thing, in that moment, was
its own kind of testimony, wrung out of it against



                         275

its will — though it would never, to the very end of
everything, have consented to call it that.
   And the four of them fell. They did not decide to
fall; there was no deciding in it, no choosing, no in-
stant in which falling was weighed against standing;
the fall was simply and only what a soul does in the
presence of the wellspring of all light: as water falls
when the ground gives way beneath it, without con-
sultation and without consent, because it could not
do otherwise and still be water. Hana fell. Adel fell.
Zarif fell. And Sami fell too, though it would be a
long while yet before anyone thought to hold that
fact close and take it out to look at again, in the dark
years still to come when he had grown bitter and
hard, and one might have wondered whether there
was ever, in him, anything clean at all. The coal in
him was not yet grown so hot that it could hold him
upright in that Presence. He went down with the
others, all the way down; and for the whole length
of that one prostration he was, exactly as they were,
wholly and only a soul before its Source, with noth-
ing held back and nothing kept aside and nothing
reaching after more; and the endless wanting in him
went, for once, entirely quiet; and he was — briefly,
and truly, and it must be said — well.
   Remember this, said Hilal, when at the last they
rose. Whatever comes to you hereafter. However far
you go, and however long, and however dark. From
this you will borrow for the rest of eternity — every
one of you, the greatest and the least, the faithful
and the lost alike — for there is no light anywhere,
in any world, in any soul, in any hour however black,

                         276

that is not borrowed, in the end, from this one light.
Remember that you stood in it once. It will matter
— more than you can now conceive, and at the very
end, when nothing else seems left to matter at all —
that you stood in it once.
   And they remembered. Even afterward; even
when they had forgotten everything else there was
to forget — their names, the warmth, the chord, one
another, the very fact that they had ever been any-
thing other than what the cold world would tell them
they were — some buried and unreachable part of
each of them went on, quietly, in the dark beneath
all the dark, remembering: that once, before the
worlds, before time, before the forgetting, they had
stood in the very nearness of the Source of all light,
and had fallen down before it, and had been, for the
length of a single fall, entirely and perfectly well. It
is the deepest thing in the whole of this story. Ev-
erything else that happens, in all the worlds that are
coming — every grief and every glory, every fall and
every rising — is built, in the end, upon it.
   He was not well, not in any way he had words for
at eleven years old. He was simply, for the length of
one whole grey morning, too full of something enor-
mous and wordless to manage his own tongue — and
then it passed, as such things pass in boys who have
been taught, however young, that fullness of that
kind is not a thing a clever boy admits to having felt.




                          277

###SECTION: Dream Fragment###
The last of his boyhood dreams — and, though he
could not have known it, the most important one
he would carry unknowingly into every argument he
ever won and every kindness he ever calculated the
cost of before giving — came to him the same winter
Sami first began, without either of them noticing, to
lean on him more than he leaned back.

      The One Who Loved for Nothing

The veiled ones did not speak. That was simply
the way of them, and the four had learned it: they
passed, and what they were reached you sidelong,
from the corner of your seeing, and they did not
stop, and they did not explain themselves — because
what they were was itself the whole of the explana-
tion, and to have stopped to put it into words would
only have made it smaller. So the four of them, after
the Crown Light, had not expected to be spoken to
at all. They had expected to be sent home full, and
silent, and changed.
   But one of the lights stopped for them. A small one
— small as the veiled ones went, which is to say still
immeasurably far above the four — fierce, and clear,

                         278

and burning at a heat that seemed somehow far too
great for so slight a flame to hold, as a single coal,
rightly kindled, can burn hotter than a whole bon-
fire of green wood. She did not pass them by. She
turned. And she did the thing that none of the great
ones had done, that none of them had so much as
imagined could be done: she spoke to them straight.
Her meaning came to them not sidelong, not glanc-
ing, but aimed — aimed like Hilal’s, and hotter than
his; hot enough, almost, to scald.
   Listen, she said, for I will say it once, and you
will need it. I do not love Him for the Garden. If He
offered me the Garden in exchange for my love, I
would count it an insult — as though my love could
be bought with rooms and rivers. And I do not love
Him for fear of the Fire. If I worshipped Him only
to save my own skin from the burning, then it is my
own skin I love, and not Him at all. I love Him —
hear me — because He is worthy of being loved. For
nothing. For no wage. Because He is He.
   She burned a moment, letting it land in them, let-
ting it sink down past the surface; and then, more
gently, the rest of it: If that is hard for you now —
here, in the warmth, where He is so near that you
may lean the whole of your weight upon Him — then
know that it will be harder, far harder, when you are
flesh; in the cold world, where He will be veiled, and
the wages will be dangled before you on every side,
and every voice you meet will tell you to love Him
for what you can get from Him. So begin now. Begin
learning it now, while it is only hard, and not yet al-



                         279

most impossible. Love Him for nothing. It is the only
love that will survive the forgetting.
   And then she turned, and was gone, back into the
shining company of the veiled; and they never saw
her again in all that world, nor learned her name,
nor needed to.
   Hana took the words whole, as she took every-
thing that was true — without examining them, with-
out testing them, without setting them aside to be
weighed; she simply opened, and let them in, and
they went all the way down into her and became, at
once and permanently, a part of the very floor that
she stood upon. She did not have to learn them. That
was the strange thing, the thing she half understood
even then: she had been doing them, dimly, blindly,
without a name for what she did, since the very first
turning of her very first instant. The small fierce
light had not taught her anything new. It had only, at
the last, given a name to the thing that Hana already,
helplessly, and from the beginning, was. She would
carry the saying unbroken through every world that
was coming, under all the forgetting, down all the
cold roads of it. It would be on her lips, more or less,
in one form or another, at the very last hour of the
very end of her.
   Adel took the words too, but differently: not down
into the floor of himself, but up into the high cool
room where he kept things to be examined later
— filed, and shelved, and labelled, and not yet felt.
He turned the saying over once, admired the clean
edges of it, found it interesting, and set it carefully
away. He did not know — he could not possibly have

                          280

known — that he was, in that one idle careful mo-
ment, shelving the single sentence that would, at
the very blackest hour of the very last of all the
worlds, when he was certain past all argument and
all hope that he was a thing beyond loving and be-
yond being loved, reach down off its high shelf of its
own accord, and find him in the dark, and save his
life. We are none of us ever told which of the things
we merely file away, half-listening, will turn out in
the end to be the whole of our rescue. He filed it.
And it waited. It was patient, on its shelf, in its dark,
patient as all true things are patient. And the cold
things, out at the edge, were patient in just exactly
the same way; for both the saving of a soul and the
ruin of one are built, in the main, of seeds planted
long, long before, in some hour since forgotten, and
then simply, quietly, waited upon.
   Sami heard the words, and they slid off him like
water off a smooth stone, finding nothing anywhere
to sink into. For Sami did not love for nothing, and
could not even imagine it; Sami loved for what the
loving won him — the brightness, the height, the
eye turned admiringly his way — and a love that
asked no wage and sought no return was, to him,
not a hard teaching but simply an unintelligible one:
a coin struck in a currency he did not hold, and did
not wish to. So he let it pass, untroubled, as one lets
pass a remark in a language one has never learned.
And Zarif — Zarif heard, and beamed, and shaped
at the departed light a single delighted thing: she is
right, he said, and she is the funniest of all of you —
because she means it. Which made no sense what-

                          281

ever; and which was, of course, exactly and entirely
true.
   He woke certain, for exactly as long as it takes a
certainty to meet the cold light of a school dormi-
tory and begin to doubt itself, that he had just been
asked the one question his whole clever life was
built, brick by careful brick, to avoid ever having to
answer honestly. By breakfast he had half-forgotten
it. Some nights, decades later, in a flat with two cups
of cold tea, he would wish very much that he had
not.




                         282

###SECTION: Chapter Thirty-Three — The Homecoming###
The Homecoming

 He came home to Istanbul in the first heat of sum-
mer, eighteen years old, finished with the cold coun-
try and bound in the autumn for a great university
in Berlin; and the city met him as it meets everyone,
all at once and with both hands, and he stood on the
pavement outside the airport in the thick gold light
and waited to feel something, and was interested, in
his detached and watchful way, to find that he did
not.
   It should have undone him. That was the thought
he turned over, standing there: that by every law
he knew it should have undone him. The light alone
should have done it — that particular Istanbul light,
thick as honey and falling on everything at a slant,
so unlike the thin grey English wash that he had
half-forgotten a sky could pour itself out like this;
and the heat, and the smell of the place coming up
all around him, sea and diesel and grilled meat and
dust and the faint green rot of the strait, the exact
smell that had been the air of the first years of his
life. He breathed it in and waited. I am home, he told

                        283

himself, deliberately, as you press a bruise to find
out whether it still hurts. Nothing answered. The
words sat in him like coins dropped down a well
that turned out to have no water at the bottom of
it, no far-off splash, only the small dry sound of the
drop and then silence. Home, he thought again, and
felt the clever part of him already moving to explain
it — jet-lag, fatigue, the natural flatness of a long-
anticipated thing — and let it explain, because the
explanations were a comfort, and because the alter-
native, which was that he had become a man who
could come home and feel nothing, was not a thing
he wished to look at directly in the gold light of the
afternoon.
   The glass house received him like a hotel that
loved him. His father was magnificent with pride
— Berlin, the school prizes, the whole gleaming tra-
jectory unrolling exactly as the man had promised
the window it would, eighteen years ago, holding
up a searching newborn against the dawn — and
he gripped Adel by both shoulders and looked at
him with a fierce wet proud old eye and said, you
have become everything I wanted, and Adel smiled
the smile and said the right warm thing back, and
thought, behind the smile, with a flatness that did
not even have the decency to be bitter: yes. I have.
That is precisely the trouble. I have become every-
thing you wanted, and I am a beautifully appointed
house with no one living in it, and you cannot tell
the difference, and neither, most days, can I. His
mother drifted through in a cloud of scent and told
him he had grown handsome and that there was a

                         284

dinner Thursday she needed him to be charming at.
He said of course. He was always of course. He had
a locked room at the centre of him now, kept since
a certain afternoon in England, and he had grown
so used to carrying the key that he scarcely noticed
any longer that there was a door, or that he never
opened it, or that the thing he had shut in there for
safekeeping had been, once, the whole of him.

                      •   • •

He walked the city, in those weeks, as a man walks
through a gallery of paintings he has been told are
priceless and finds, to his quiet horror, that he can
admire and cannot love.
  And it was beautiful; God, it was beautiful; he was
not so far gone that he could not see it. He walked
down through the old quarters where the wooden
houses leaned their foreheads together over lanes
too narrow for the sun, and out along the water
where the great mosques sat their domes upon the
skyline like a fleet at anchor, and across the heaving
bridge with the fishermen shoulder to shoulder at
the rail and the ferries shouldering the bright chop
below; and he caught himself appraising it all, rank-
ing it, composing it like a photographer framing a
shot — that is a fine prospect; that dome is older
than the school; the light on the water there is re-
markable — and underneath the appraising ran the
old melancholy, deeper now, because here was the
loveliest city on the earth, his own, the one that had
made him, and it reached him as a beautiful sen-

                          285

tence reaches you in a language you have stopped
being able to feel: he knew, abstractly, that it meant
everything; he simply could not get it to mean any-
thing to him. What is wrong with me, he thought, on
the bridge, in the wind, with the gulls screaming and
the whole golden impossible city pouring past, that
I can stand in the middle of all this and be bored?
And he had no answer, because the only being who
could have answered him was the one he had spent
ten years learning not to hear.
   The prayer died on a rooftop, at sunset, without
drama, as most important things die.
   He had gone up to a high terrace above the fam-
ily\{}’s part of the city to be alone, which was the
one appetite that had only grown in him — to get
up above people, where the performing could stop
— and he was leaning on the parapet with a cooling
glass in his hand when the sun went down behind
the Old City and the call rose. Not one call: all of
them, all at once, from every minaret in every quar-
ter, as it does over Istanbul at the maghrib, a thou-
sand voices climbing and overlapping and folding
into one vast aching architecture of sound that filled
the whole bowl of the sky and the whole bowl of the
city beneath it; and below him, in the streets and
the courtyards and the small bright rooms, he knew,
the whole living patient mass of the place was turn-
ing, as it had turned five times a day for a thousand
years, toward a single point on the far side of the
world. He watched it. He felt the beauty of it land
on him and slide off, the way the rain had slid off
another, polished stone, in another doorway, years

                         286

and a world away, though he would never know that.
And he thought, with great calm, the calm of a man
signing a paper he has already decided to sign: I do
not believe this. I have not believed it for years; I
have only been too sentimental, or too cowardly, to
say so plainly, even to myself. I have been perform-
ing a faith for the same reason I perform everything
else — because it was expected, because my grand-
mother\{}’s ghost was watching, because it was a
thread back to a self I have outgrown. And a man
who is going to Berlin to think for a living cannot go
on muttering words at a wall because they comfort
an ache he is too clever to indulge. It is finished. Let
it be finished honestly. And he raised his glass, a lit-
tle, to the dying light, in a small dry private toast to
his own honesty; and that was the end of it; and he
genuinely believed, draining the glass, that he had
just done a brave and an adult and a clarifying thing.
   He had not. He had only, at last, set down the one
thing that had still, faintly, by a thread, been car-
rying him; and somewhere too deep in him for the
clever mind ever to go, the faint warm tracery that
had lain in the grain of him since before the world —
the two small arcs of gold, reaching, not touching —
went, for the first time in his life, entirely cold. He
felt nothing. That was the mercy and the horror of it
both: that so great a loss could be suffered and regis-
ter as nothing at all, as a faint relief, as the pleasant
lightness of a man who has put down a bag he had
stopped noticing he carried. Far off, the gentle ones
who keep watch over such things bowed their heads.
The patient voice at his ear said nothing, and was

                          287

glad, and rested; it had been ten years in the doing
and it could afford, now, to be silent. And the door
of the locked room at the centre of him, which had
only ever been shut, was now — he did not hear the
sound it made — quietly, and from the inside, and
by his own clever reasonable hand, bolted.

                      •   • •

It was three days later, in a sudden violent summer
downpour, that he met the old man.
   The rain had come as it comes in that city in high
summer, out of a clear sky and all at once, a warm
grey deluge that turned the steep lanes to rivers in
a minute and sent everyone bolting for the nearest
awning; and Adel, caught far up in the tangle of old
streets behind the covered bazaar with no awning
near, ducked through the first open door he found
and fetched up, dripping and laughing despite him-
self, in a shop he had not known was there. It was a
bookseller\{}’s — a tiny, deep, dim cave of a place,
one narrow room running back further than seemed
possible into a brown gloom, every wall and every
surface and most of the floor stacked and crammed
and overflowing with books, old books, books in Ot-
toman script and Arabic and French and Persian,
the air so thick with the smell of them — dust, and
foxed paper, and old leather, and something sweet-
ish underneath, like dried flowers pressed and for-
gotten between pages — that breathing it was like
breathing the inside of a century. The roar of the
rain dropped to a hush the moment the door swung

                          288

to. And at the back, in a pool of low yellow lamplight,
behind a desk drowned in volumes, an old man sat
reading, and did not look up.
   Adel waited, in the way one does, expecting to be
noticed, to be served, to be asked. He was not. The
old man read on, unhurried, turning a page with one
finger; and Adel, who was used to rooms turning
toward him and found that he did not in the least
know what to do with one that did not, drifted along
the shelves to cover the strange small awkwardness
of it, taking down a book here and there, putting it
back. He is simply deaf, he told himself, or absorbed,
or one of those eccentrics the old city is full of; and
he had nearly talked himself back into ease when
the old man said, without looking up from his page,
in a low dry amused voice, in perfect unhurried Turk-
ish: ”You have the look of a young man who has just
thrown something away, and is very pleased with
himself, and has not yet noticed that his hands are
emptier than he meant them to be.”
   Adel went still. He turned. The old man was look-
ing at him now over the top of the book, and there
was nothing of the fortune-teller in it, no theatre, no
glitter; only a pair of very old, very clear, very calm
eyes that seemed to be regarding Adel from a great
and friendly distance, as one might watch, with af-
fection, a thing one has known for a very long time.
Who are you, Adel thought, with a prickle along his
arms that the warm rain did not account for; and
then, immediately, on top of it, the clever cold re-
flex: a parlour trick. He sells books in the old city;
he reads people for sport; everyone my age has the

                         289

look of a young man pleased with himself; it means
nothing. He arranged his face into the easy charm-
ing mask and said, lightly, ”And you have the look of
a man who has been waiting all afternoon for some-
one to say that to.” The old man laughed — a real
laugh, delighted, that crinkled the whole brown map
of his face — and said, ”There. You see. You are
quick. They were right that you would be quick.”
They. The word went into Adel like a splinter going
in clean, too fast to feel until later. Who is they, he
thought. But he did not ask; asking would have been
to admit the splinter had gone in; and so he only
smiled, and set down the book he was holding, and
said the rain was easing, though it was not.
   The old man let him go. He did not press, or ex-
plain, or do any of the things that would have let
Adel safely file him as a madman; he simply returned
his eyes to his page, as though the whole strange
exchange had been the most ordinary thing in the
world, and said, as Adel reached the door and the
roar of the rain came up again to meet him: ”Young
man.” Adel stopped, his hand on the latch. ”You will
spend a great many years now,” the old man said,
mildly, not looking up, ”paying off a debt you do
not owe, and looking everywhere but the one place,
because the one place frightens you. So I will give
you a thing to carry, since you like to carry things,
and you may take it out and look at it when you are
ready, which will not be soon. Here it is. Do not mis-
take a secret for a debt.” A pause. The lamp gut-
tered. ”That is all. Go on. You will be late, and you
are never late.” And Adel stood one moment longer,

                         290

the rain loud beyond the door, the splinter working,
his clever mind already swarming over the sentence
to take it apart and finding, to its quiet outrage, no
seam in it anywhere to get a grip on; and then he
went out into the warm grey flood, and walked, and
did not look back.
   He told himself, by the time he reached the bot-
tom of the hill, that it was nothing: a mad old
bookseller, a practised line, the kind of fog-cutting
nonsense such men sell along with their mildewed
books to impressionable tourists; and he was nei-
ther impressionable nor a tourist, and he had a din-
ner to be charming at. Do not mistake a secret for a
debt. He turned it over, walking, as he turned over
everything. It was not even good nonsense, he de-
cided; it did not parse; a secret and a debt were
not members of the same category and could not be
mistaken for one another; the sentence was a sort
of profound-sounding rubbish engineered to lodge
in the mind precisely because it could not be re-
solved, and the proof that it was rubbish was that
it had lodged. He was rather pleased with this anal-
ysis. And he carried the sentence, lodged and unre-
solved and refusing every solvent his clever mind
could pour over it, down out of the old city and back
to the glass house and across the whole of the sum-
mer; and when, weeks later, he went looking — idly,
he told himself, merely curious — for the little shop
again, to put a face and a place to the thing and so be
rid of it, he could not find the street; and then could
not find the quarter; and at last gave it up, with a
coldness at the back of his neck that he did not ex-

                         291

amine, and never spoke of it to anyone, and filed it,
as he filed everything that could not be solved, in
the locked room he no longer opened.


                      •   • •


At the summer\{}’s end he flew to Berlin, into the
sharp clear northern light and the wide grey pur-
poseful city and the life that was waiting to make
him formidable; and he was glad to go, glad of the
new hard surfaces to throw himself against, glad to
leave behind the golden city that would not mean
anything to him and the splinter of a sentence that
would not come out. And down in the steep streets
he was leaving — four miles and a whole world from
the glass house, in a quarter he had passed through
once in the rain and would never connect to any-
thing — a girl of his own age was at that very season
turning a thing over in herself, too: a strange new
restlessness under her old contentment, a sense,
since the winter her grandmother died, of someone
missing whom she had never met, an ache that was
almost the twin of his own except that hers, being
unbolted, had somewhere to go. They had been in
the one city all summer long, the high note and
the low, and had not come within a mile of one an-
other. He went north into his future. She stayed, and
turned her face to the autumn call, and waited, with-
out knowing she was waiting, for a night that was
now only a year away.

                 BOOK TWO · ACT IV
                      • • •




                    The Veil

     Truly, in the remembrance of God do hearts
                       find rest.



                      •   • •


The Qur’an · Sūrah ar-Raʿd (13:28)




                          293

###SECTION: Chapter Thirty-Four — The Two on the Shoulders###
The Two on the Shoulders

 Berlin took him in the way cold water takes a swim-
mer who has decided, on principle, not to gasp.
   It was nothing like the cities he had come from.
Where Istanbul poured itself out in gold and noise
and leaning crooked beauty, and the English school
had been all moss and centuries and damp self-
assurance, Berlin was wide, and grey, and straight,
and ferociously awake — a city that had been bro-
ken and rebuilt and broken again and had stopped,
somewhere in all of it, expecting to be comforted;
a city of long bare boulevards and raw concrete
and enormous cold light, where the past was not
hidden as it was hidden everywhere else but stood
out plainly on the streets in the form of scars, and
where everyone seemed to be arguing, beautifully
and without rest, about how a person ought to live.
He loved it at once, with the only kind of love he had
left, which was the love of a sharp mind for a sharp
place. Here, he thought, walking the wide grey pur-
poseful streets that first cold autumn with his collar
up and his whole future in front of him, here at last

                        294

is somewhere that does not ask me to feel anything.
Here they only ask me to think. And thinking, I can
do.
   And he did it magnificently. He had come to study
the law, and the philosophy under the law, and the
long human argument about justice that runs un-
der both; and he fell on it as a starved man falls
on bread. The seminar rooms became his kingdom
— better than the debating hall had ever been, be-
cause here the stakes were real and the opponents
were grown — and within a single term the profes-
sors had marked him, and within two the other stu-
dents had arranged themselves around him in the
old familiar way, half in admiration and half in some-
thing warier, and he felt the hollow place fill, for an
hour at a time, after a brilliant seminar, as it had
used to fill after a won debate; and then empty again
by morning, exactly as before, so that he had always
to go and be brilliant again. I am happy, he told him-
self, often, in those first Berlin months; and he was
so good at telling himself things that he very nearly
believed it; and only late at night, sometimes, walk-
ing home alone along the cold canal with the black
water sliding by, did the old nameless ache come up
out of him like breath fogging on the air, and stand
there, asking its wordless question, until he turned
his collar higher and walked faster and reasoned it,
once again, away.


                       •   • •




                           295

He did not know — he had decided years before
that there was nothing there to know — that he was
never, for one instant of any of it, alone.
   There were two of them, and they had been with
him always, one at his right hand and one at his left,
so near that no thought of his was too quiet for them
and no deed too small; and they were writing. That
was their whole work and their whole joy: to write
him down, all of him, every word and every act and
every turning of the heart, in a book that did not
miss things. The one on the right kept the record of
the good. The one on the left kept the record of the
wrong. And though you might think, hearing it, that
such a pair would be cold things, clerks, auditors,
watching a man only to catch him out, the truth of
them was the very opposite, and is one of the better-
kept secrets of the world: they loved him. They had
been appointed to him and to no other; his story was
the only story they would ever write; and they had
come, over eighteen years of writing it, to love their
difficult brilliant charge with an ache that the cold
scribbling clerks of human imagining could never
have begun to feel.
   And the book they kept was a stranger and a
kinder book than a watching man would ever have
guessed, because the hand that had set them writ-
ing had laid down, before the world began, the rules
of how the writing went; and the rules were these.
When their charge so much as leaned toward a good
thing — only leaned, only wished it, only half-rose to-
ward it and sat back down — the one on the right
was permitted to write it as a thing fully done, a

                         296

whole good deed entered for an intention barely
formed. And when he did the good thing in truth,
the one on the right wrote it ten times over, and on
certain days, for certain deeds done in a certain hid-
den sincerity, very much more than ten, more than
the angel could see the end of, the single act flower-
ing on the page into a hundred, a thousand, a num-
ber known only to the One who had set the rule. But
when their charge leaned toward a wrong thing —
wished it, turned toward it — and then, for the love
of God or the fear of Him or the plain mercy of a
better impulse, did not do it, the one on the left was
made to lay down his pen, and the one on the right
wrote, in its place, another good deed, for the wrong
refused. And only when the wrong was truly done
did the one on the left write at all; and then he wrote
it once, once only, a single mark for a single sin, no
matter how the right-hand page might blaze with
tens and hundreds beside it. The whole arithmetic
of the book was bent, from its very first rule, toward
mercy. They had not made the rule. They only kept
it, and marvelled at it, and loved the One who had
written it more than they could say.
   The one on the left had, besides, a habit that he
had never been forbidden and that no one had ever
taught him, which was that he was slow. When their
charge did some wrong thing — spoke the cutting
word, chose the cold proud thing, bolted some inner
door against the light — the left-hand angel did not
write it at once. He waited. He held his pen above
the page and he waited, with a patience he had
learned from watching the patience of his Lord, in

                         297

the simple unkillable hope that the man would turn,
in an hour or a day, and be sorry, and ask to be for-
given; for if he did — if he turned even a little, even
clumsily, even once — then the thing need never be
written at all, and the left-hand angel could close
his pen with a gladness that was the best part of
his whole existence, and the page would stay clean.
He had held his pen so, over this particular man, a
great many times in eighteen years. More and more,
of late, he had had to write the thing down in the end.
But he never wrote it quickly. He never gave up the
waiting until the waiting was truly over. And every
single time, before he wrote, he hoped.

                       •   • •

The thing that woke the best in him, that grey Berlin
winter, was a wrong done to someone small.
   He had taken up, half for his studies and half from
a restlessness he did not examine, a little unpaid
work at a legal clinic in the poorer east of the city
— a bare room over a launderette where the desper-
ate came with their papers in plastic bags — and it
was there, on an ordinary afternoon, that he met the
man: a quiet, frightened, dignified man from some-
where that had burned, who had crossed half the
earth with his family and his terror and his small bag
of documents, and who was about to be sent back to
the fire by the calm machinery of a state that had de-
cided, on a technicality, on a misfiled form, that his
terror did not count. Adel listened to the whole of it
with the cold clear attention he gave to everything.

                           298

And then something happened in him that had not
happened in years; something came up out of the
locked room without asking his permission; the old
buried thing, the thing that as a boy had not been
able to walk past four bullies and a small stammer-
ing victim, the thing his name had meant before his
name became a weight — it stood up in him, white-
hot, and looked at the quiet doomed dignified man
across the cheap table, and said, silently, with the
whole force of him: no. Not this one. Not while I am
here. This is wrong, and I can read, and I am clev-
erer than the whole misfiling machine of them put
together, and I am going to take this apart at the
joints and give this man back his life. It was the first
wholly clean thing he had wanted in years.
   At his right hand, the angel wrote, and wept for
joy as he wrote, and the deed went down on the page
blazing, and began at once to multiply. For the want-
ing alone, the bare leaning toward the good, a whole
good deed; and for the work that followed — and he
did the work, nights of it, brilliant and unpaid and fe-
rocious, and he won, he tore the misfiled cruelty to
pieces and the quiet man and his family stayed, and
were safe, and wept their thanks in a language Adel
did not speak — for that, ten, and more than ten. And
here was the thing the two of them watched with
such complicated love: that the deed was real, and
good, and gloriously his, and that woven all through
it, like a dark thread through bright cloth, ran the
other thing, the old thing, the I am cleverer than the
whole machine of them, the hunger to be the one
who saves, the great man\{}’s vanity wearing the

                          299

robe of justice so well that the man himself could
not have told the two apart. The angels could tell
them apart. They saw the gold and they saw the
dark thread both. And the mystery they had learned
not to question was that the gold was still written,
all of it, in full — that a mixed and partly-vain good
deed done by a half-lost man was not struck out for
the vanity in it, but entered, and counted, and mul-
tiplied, and the dark thread left for another page
and another reckoning and the wide mercy of the
One who alone could weigh a heart; so that even
now, even bolted, even with the door shut from the
inside, their charge was busily, unknowingly, lay-
ing up light on the right-hand page faster than he
was laying up dark on the left. He thought he had
stopped believing in any of it. He was, the whole
time, writing himself a book full of mercy, and could
not see the page.

                        •   • •

But he could not feel it; and that was the veil, and
the veil was the whole grief of those years.
  For he could now do a magnificent thing — could
save a man, could burn a night to ash fighting for a
stranger — and feel, at the end of it, only the brief fa-
miliar fading warmth of a thing well done, and then
the hollow, and then the reach for the next thing;
and he could sit in a bright seminar and argue the
existence of God for two hours, brilliantly, on either
side, as a chess problem, as an exercise, and walk
out having felt precisely nothing, because the God

                            300

he argued was a proposition and the God he had
bolted out was a Presence, and he had long ago lost
the thread between the word and the thing. Once,
late, a gentle earnest fellow student asked him, after
such a seminar, but what do you believe, yourself,
underneath it all? — and Adel had given some glit-
tering deflecting answer and gone home and stood
a long time at his window over the black canal, and
the question had sat in him next to the old splinter,
do not mistake a secret for a debt, the two of them
lodged side by side in the locked room, and he had
thought, with a desolation that frightened him: I do
not know. I genuinely do not know what is under-
neath it all, because I have not been down there in
years, and I have lost the key, and the worst of it is
that I bolted the door myself and cannot now remem-
ber why it seemed so clever. And the nameless ache
rose, and stood, and asked; and he did the only thing
he knew to do with it; he reasoned it away, and went
to bed, and rose the next day and was formidable.
   At his left hand, that night, the angel held his pen
over the bolting of the door, and waited, and hoped,
and at last, when no turning came, wrote it down —
once, only once, and slowly, and with grief. And the
two of them, the scribe of the right and the scribe of
the left, did the thing they did on the hardest nights,
the thing that is the unseen mercy folded inside ev-
ery human darkness and is perhaps the best teach-
ing the angels have to give the world if the world
could only hear them: they did not give him up. It
is not permitted to them to give a soul up; despair
of the mercy is the one thing forbidden to the crea-

                         301

ture as it is forbidden to the man, for the door that a
man bolts from the inside he can unbolt from the in-
side, in one breath, in one whispered turning, on the
very last night of his life, and the whole of Heaven
is arranged to fly to him the instant he does — as,
it is said, a man who has lost everything he owns
in a trackless desert and lain down to die looks up
to find his lost beast standing over him with all his
goods on its back, and is undone with a joy too large
for his body; just so, and more, is the joy in the heav-
ens over one bolted door swinging open. The angels
knew this as they knew their own names. So they
kept writing, and kept hoping, and kept the book of
mercy open at the bright page; and they watched
their brilliant lost beloved charge sleep, and they
did not despair of him, because they were not per-
mitted to, and because they did not want to, and
because they had read further in his book than he
had, and had reason, which they could not tell him,
to hope.
   And there was a reason; and it was coming; and
even the patient cold thing at his ear had begun, that
winter, to feel the first far edge of it and to be afraid.
For the faint gold tracery in the deep of him, that
had gone entirely cold on a rooftop in Istanbul, had
begun — so faintly that only the unseen could have
caught it — to be not quite so cold; to stir, now and
then, in his sleep; as though, across the whole turn-
ing width of the world, in a steep poor quarter of
a golden city, a low and steady note that had never
once stopped sounding were at last, by some turning
of the great slow mercy that bends every arithmetic

                          302

toward itself, beginning to be heard. He knew noth-
ing of it. He slept, and rose, and argued God as a
chess problem, and saved the helpless for the love
of his own cleverness, and lay awake aching for a
country he did not believe in; and over him the two
faithful scribes wrote down the whole magnificent
ruin and glory of him, the gold in full and the dark
thread once, and waited, with all of Heaven, for the
night the door would open.




                        303

###SECTION: Chapter Thirty-Five — The Open Door###
The Open Door

 She woke, as she had woken every day for years, a
little before the dawn, into the cold and the dark and
the silence of a sleeping house, and lay a moment
listening to the breathing of her sisters in the bed
beside her; and then she rose, because it was time,
and went to meet her Lord before she met the day.
   There was no drama in it, and that was the whole
beauty of it. She moved through the cold flat by feel,
not waking anyone, and ran the tap until the water
came, and washed — the hands, the mouth, the face,
the arms, the wet hand over the hair, the feet — the
same washing, in the same order, that she had done
thousands of times and that a thousand million oth-
ers were doing or had just done or were about to
do, all the way around the turning dark world, so
that the small private act in the cold bathroom was
also, secretly, one stitch in something vast; and the
cold water on her face in the dark was, every single
morning, a small clean shock of being alive and be-
ing awake and being, for these few minutes before
the whole clamouring day, entirely His. She spread

                        304

the worn mat toward the southeast. She stood in the
dark. And she prayed the dawn prayer as she did
everything, without flourish and without hurry, only
present, only there; and somewhere in the middle of
it the first grey light would begin to come up at the
window, and the first call would lift from the minaret
up the hill and be answered by the next and the next
across the waking city, and she would be already
standing in the thing the call was calling everyone
else toward, and her heart, in those minutes, was as
quiet and as full as a bowl held level and brimming
and not spilling a drop.
   This was the architecture of her days, and had
been for as long as she could remember: the five
quiet turnings, spaced through the hours like the
supports under a bridge, holding the whole weight
of the ordinary day up off the water. Between
them she was as busy and as harried as anyone —
there was the studying now, which was hard, and
the housework, which was endless, and the little
ones, and the shopping done with too little money
stretched too far — but the turnings held it all up,
and kept it from becoming merely a list of burdens,
because five times in the day, whatever was hap-
pening, she stopped, and washed, and stood, and
remembered; and the remembering was a kind of
rest that had nothing to do with sleep. She told the
names over on her fingers in the dark when she
could not sleep, the beautiful names, the Merciful,
the Near, the Giver, the One who turns toward those
who turn to Him, until the fear of whatever it was
went quiet and she did. She gave away what she

                         305

could not afford to give, because that was simply
what you did, the small coin pressed into a smaller
hand, and found, as the poor who give always find,
that it never left her poorer. And when the month
of fasting came round, the whole quarter went lu-
minous with it — the long bright hungry days, the
streets at dusk gone quiet and tender as the whole
exhausted city waited together for the single sip of
water, the cannon, the dates, the rush of mercy that
came down on the breaking of the fast like rain on a
cracked field, the warm crowded nights — and Hana,
fasting, thin, tired, scrubbing pots at midnight so
her mother could rest, was as happy in that month
as a person can be in this world, and could not have
told you why, and did not need to.

                      •   • •

The door to the wide world opened for her in the
spring she was eighteen, and it opened, as such
doors do, partly by her own hard work and partly
by the eye of someone who happened to see.
   She had worked, quietly and ferociously, at the
great hard examinations that are the one ladder a
poor child in that country can climb; and she had
done well, better than well, because she had a clear
still mind that did not panic and a patience that
did not break; but it was not the marks, in the end,
that did it. It was a doctor — a tired kind over-
worked woman who ran a charity clinic in the quar-
ter, and to whom Hana had been sent, that winter,
to sit with the frightened ones in the waiting room

                          306

because somebody had noticed that the frightened
ones grew calm when the still girl sat with them —
it was she who took Hana aside one evening and
looked at her hard and said: child, what you have
cannot be taught. I have spent thirty years trying
to teach it to clever young people and it cannot be
done. You walk into a room where someone is afraid
to die and the room changes. Do you know how rare
that is? You are going to study medicine, and I am
going to help you, and you are not going to spend
it chasing money, you are going to spend it where
the others will not go — at the bedsides everyone
else has run from. And Hana, who had never once
thought of herself as having anything anyone would
want, went home through the cold bright streets
turning the words over with a wonder that was al-
most fright, and stood a long time at the window
over the steep roofs, and thought, simply: so this is
the reason. Grandmother said I would learn the rea-
son. This is it. He has been getting me ready the
whole time.
   And so she began, that autumn, to go down each
day to the great teaching hospital across the city —
a poor girl in a worn coat among the confident chil-
dren of doctors — and to learn the long hard science
of the body; and she was not the quickest of them,
and did not mind, because she had understood some-
thing the quick ones had not, which was that she
had not been sent there to be the cleverest in the
room. She had been sent for the rooms the clev-
erness could not reach. And it was there, in those
first months, in a quiet ward at the end of a corridor

                         307

where they put the ones who were not going to get
better, that she first did, for a stranger, the thing she
had done for her grandmother: sat through the long
afternoon with an old man who had no one, and held
his hand, and was not afraid, and said the beauti-
ful names softly when his breathing frightened him,
and stayed; and when he slipped away at last it was
easy, and unafraid, and he was not alone; and she
understood, walking home, that dying was not the
end she had been half-taught to dread but a turn-
ing, a going-back, a setting-out — the loosing of a
small boat from a dark jetty toward a sea it had come
from and half remembered — and that to stand on
the jetty and not run, and to wave the boat off with
love, was perhaps the truest work a person could
be given. The doctor had been right. She had been
made for the bedsides everyone else ran from. And
she went to them gladly, and they were never, after
her, run from again.

                        •   • •

And yet, that year, in the midst of all of it — the brim-
ming dawns, the luminous fasting, the opening door,
the work she had been made for — a new thing had
come into her, and she did not understand it, and it
was the first thing in her whole life she had not been
able simply to carry.
  It was an ache. It had no cause that she could find,
and no object that she could name, and it had begun,
as nearly as she could tell, somewhere around the
winter her grandmother died, and it had only deep-

                            308

ened since. It was not grief; she knew grief now, and
this was not it, or not only it. It was more like a home-
sickness — but a homesickness for nowhere, for no
house she had ever lived in, for no place on any map;
a sense, rising up in her at the strangest moments,
in the middle of a prayer or a crowded street or the
grey hour of a hospital dawn, that there was some-
one she was missing. Someone she had lost. Some-
one she had known, once, somewhere, so well and
so dearly that the loss of them was a hollow shaped
exactly like a person — and yet she had never, in
the whole of her short life, met anyone whose loss
could account for it. How can you miss someone you
have never met, she asked herself, lying awake in
the dark beside her sleeping sisters. How can there
be a person-shaped emptiness in you for a person
who has never been there? It made no sense. And
still the ache came, and stood, and would not be
reasoned away — the same nameless ache, though
she could not have known it, that rose in a brilliant
lost young man at his window over a black canal
two thousand miles to the north, on the very same
nights, asking the very same wordless question into
the very same dark.
   But here was the difference, the whole difference,
the difference that was the hidden engine of both
their lives: that his ache had been bolted away be-
hind a door he had shut from the inside, and so it
could only batter at the walls of him and find no
way out and curdle, at last, into a despair he was
too clever to name; while hers had nowhere it was
forbidden to go. She did the thing with the ache

                          309

that she did with everything. She took it to Him.
She did not understand it, and she did not need
to; she simply carried it, like a hurt child, into the
one place she had been carrying things her whole
life, and laid it down in the dark in the middle of
the night prayer and said, in the wordless way that
was the deepest speech she had, I do not know what
this is. I miss someone and I do not know who. You
know who. You know everything. Keep them, wher-
ever they are, and keep me, and let it be as You see
best; and the strangest thing happened, the thing
that would have astonished the clever young man
at the canal past all believing: the ache did not go
away, but it stopped being unbearable. It turned, in
the bringing of it to Him, from a wound into a kind
of prayer; from an emptiness into a leaning; from a
thing that hollowed her into a thing that, impossibly,
drew her closer in. She found — she could not have
put it into these words, but she lived it every night —
that the restless thing in her went quiet the instant
she remembered Him, as a fretting baby goes quiet
against the one shoulder that knows how to hold it;
and that this was not a trick she did but simply how
a heart was built, as a key is built to a lock; and
that whatever the someone-shaped emptiness was,
it was somehow, in the end, only one more road that
led where every road in her had always led, home,
toward the warmth, toward Him.
   There was an old song her grandmother used to
sing, low, over the washing-up, a song older than
anyone could say, that she had never thought about
and now could not get out of her head: a song about

                         310

a reed cut from the reed-bed, crying in every note
it played for the bed it had been torn from; a song
about the soul as a stranger in a far country, weep-
ing in the night for a home it can no longer quite
remember but cannot stop longing for, certain only
that it does not belong here and came from some-
where else and is, the whole of its life, walking the
long way back. She understood it now in a way she
had not as a child. She felt herself, that aching lu-
minous year, to be exactly that: a stranger, far from
a home she could not name, walking back; and the
ache was the song the reed could not help singing;
and the singing, far from being a thing to be cured,
was the truest sound she made.

                       •   •     •

The unseen ones about her took heart from all of
it. Her guardian, who had kept its glad post since
her first hour, watched her carry the nameless ache
to the night prayer and lay it down, and was filled
again with that thing in such beings that can only
be called praise. The thin starved companion at her
other ear — the one that, on the night her grand-
mother died, had felt for the first time in its long
cold existence something almost like warmth — had
gone on, all this while, very slowly, against every-
thing it was, turning; and the believing ones among
the hidden people, who had marked her since her
first dawn and felt the faint gold tracery in the deep
of her, felt it now begin, unmistakably, to warm — to
glow, on the cold and patient nights, a little brighter,

                           311

a little steadier, as though it were leaning, across
the whole turning width of the world, toward a far-
off answering glow that had lately, after long years,
begun to warm again as well. They did not know
what was coming. But they had served long enough
to know the feeling of a thing about to happen, and
they drew near, and they waited.
   And so the two notes went on sounding in their
two far cities, through that last whole year before
the night that was coming: the high one in the cold
clear north, brilliant and bolted and aching in a
dark it would not name; the low one in the steep
gold streets, poor and luminous and aching too, but
aching toward, aching home, the ache itself turned
into a leaning and the leaning into a rest. The same
ache. The same hand. The same long mercy, work-
ing by its opposite roads. And every night that Hana
laid her nameless longing down in the dark and felt
it turn to peace, she drew, without the faintest idea
that she was doing it, one night nearer to the boy
at the far end of the missing, and to the single hour,
now almost upon them both, when the bolted door
and the open one would be opened at the very same
time, and the reed and the reed-bed would, for the
length of one impossible dream, be one.




                         312

###SECTION: Interlude — Nearer###
Nearer

 There is no instrument made that can measure the
distance between two people who have not yet met
and are, nonetheless, drawing nearer to one another
— but had there been one, it would have shown, in
the ordinary unremarkable years of two ordinary un-
remarkable lives, a long slow narrowing, as two cur-
rents in a wide sea can be, for a great while, indis-
tinguishable from separate seas, before the charts
begin, almost imperceptibly, to show them converg-
ing.
   He did not feel it as nearness. He felt it, when he
felt it at all, as an absence with a shape — a chair at
a table he had never set for two, a certain kind of si-
lence in a certain kind of room, a face he sometimes
thought he glimpsed in a crowd and, turning, never
found. She did not feel it as nearness either. She
felt it as a patience she could not account for — an
unhurried certainty, entirely without evidence, that
whatever she was waiting for had not yet arrived
because it was not yet time, not because it did not
exist.

                         313

  Neither of them would have called it faith, in
those years. They would have called it, if pressed,
nothing at all — an idle feeling, not worth examining
too closely, the kind of thing sensible people learn
not to build a life around.
  They went on with their sensible lives. And the
distance, unmeasured and unmeasurable, went on
narrowing anyway, as it had been narrowing since
before either of them was born, toward a bridge, in
the rain, that neither of them yet knew was already
being prepared to hold them both at once.




                        314

###SECTION: Chapter Thirty-Six — A Stirring in the Dark###
A Stirring in the Dark

 There is a world laid over the world, and most of
those who live in the first one go their whole lives
without once suspecting the second; and that is a
mercy, on the whole, and was never more of one
than on the night the second world began, very qui-
etly, to stir.
   It is a world of fire, as ours is a world of clay —
made before us, of a flame that throws no light and
casts no shadow, peopled by a race as old as the
hills and as various as our own: some of them de-
vout, and some of them careless, and some of them
turned, long ago and with their whole wills, to a pa-
tient hatred of the soft clay creatures who came af-
ter them and were, to their endless fury, preferred.
They move through our streets unseen. They see us
where we cannot see them. And they are bound, ev-
ery one of them, by laws they did not choose and
cannot break: that they may whisper, but never com-
pel; that they may suggest the step, but never take
the foot and move it; and that the future, which is
the one thing they would give all their fire to know,

                        315

is closed to them as utterly as it is closed to us, so
that they too must watch the world unfold one dark
hour at a time, and guess, and fear, and wait.
   A few of them, in that city, were friends. Not to
one another only — though they were that, the small
scattered band of them, in the way of any people
who hold an unpopular faith in a hostile age — but
friends, after their strange fashion, to the two clay
children they had watched over since the morning
the two were born. There was Layth, who was quick
and fierce and had spent an age learning to love a
