thing he had been made to envy. There was Wahb,
gentle and slow, who gave things away. There was
Sadira, who saw further than the rest. And there
was the one they all deferred to, the most ancient of
them, so old that he remembered the broken world
before the first man, who had seen empires of fire
rise and fall to dust and had turned, somewhere in
the long ruin of all that, toward the Light, and never
turned back: Marid al-Qadim, the Old One, in whom
the whole long memory of their kind was kept. For
years now the four of them had felt, at the edges
of the unseen, the two faint marks the two children
carried — the pale gold tracery, the two small arcs
reaching and not touching — and had watched, and
been glad of the watching, and asked of it nothing.
   And now the marks were warming. Both of them.
The high one in the cold north, that had gone dead
as a cinder on a rooftop and had, against all the Old
One\{}’s expectation, begun lately to glow again,
faint and fitful, in the young man\{}’s sleep; and
the low one in the steep streets, that had never

                         316

once gone cold and was now brightening, steadily,
like a coal breathed upon; and between the two of
them, stretched across the whole turning width of
the world, a thing the four of them could feel as you
feel a held breath in a dark room — a tension, a lean-
ing, a chord drawn taut and not yet struck. They did
not know what it meant, and that not-knowing was
the very thing that made that night so terrible to
them. They could feel that something vast was gath-
ering, and they could not see one inch into what it
was, or when, or whether it would come to good or to
ruin; they could only feel it gathering, as the beasts
feel the storm while the sky is still blue, and grow
restless, and lift their heads.
   ”It is close,” said Sadira, who saw furthest, and
could not say what it was. ”Whatever it is. It is very
close now.” And the Old One, who said little, said
only: ”Yes. And we are not the only ones who feel
it.”

                      •   • •

For across the unseen, in the cold places where the
turned ones keep their watch, the same chord had
been felt; and it had not been felt with gladness.
   The thing that had attended the clever child since
his first hour — the patient one, the cold one, that
had crossed the strait that long-ago dawn and bent
its whole attention upon the boy and ignored, with
contempt, the still girl across the water — had spent
eighteen years on its slow, beautiful, nearly finished
work; had brought the brilliant soul, step by reason-

                          317

able step, to bolt its own door from the inside; had
stood at the young man\{}’s shoulder on the night
the prayers died and counted the campaign all but
won. And now, in the very hour of its triumph, it had
begun to feel the bolted door grow warm; to feel the
dead cinder stir; and it was afraid, which it had not
been in a very long time, because it understood, as
the soft watchful believing ones perhaps did not yet
fully understand, exactly what it was feeling, and
exactly how much it stood to lose.
   For it knew what the chord was. It had been there,
after all, at the very beginning, in the bright coun-
try before the clay, on the day the two notes were
first struck together and left to ring; it had heard
the sound they made; and it had hated the sound
with a purity that eighteen years of patient work had
not cooled by a single degree. It knew that if the
two notes were ever truly sounded together again
— if the high and the low were ever, even for an
instant, even in a dream, allowed to meet and ring
as they had been made to ring — then something
would wake in both of them that no whisper could
afterward put back to sleep; a memory below all
memory; a certainty that would burn through every
reasonable doubt it had spent two decades so care-
fully constructing, as the sun burns through fog. The
whole edifice of the young man\{}’s forgetting, ev-
ery patient brick of it, would not survive one clear
note of that chord. And so the cold thing, in the hour
of its triumph, found itself staring at the ruin of ev-
erything it had built, and asked itself the question
it should have asked eighteen years before; and the

                         318

asking of it was the most dangerous thing that hap-
pened in the unseen that whole long year.
   The question was: where is the danger coming
from? And the cold thing, which was old and clever
and had been hating cleverly since before the city
was founded, turned the question over, and arrived,
with a slow cold horror, at an answer it had been too
proud to see. The danger was not the boy. The boy
it had in hand; the boy was bolted, and broken to
its harness, and even the warming of him was only
an effect, a symptom, a thing caused by something
else. The cause was elsewhere. The cause was the
other note — the low one — the one it had glanced
at once, eighteen years ago, in a poor flat across the
water, and dismissed in a single breath as easy, and
gentle, and no sport, and never looked at again. It
had made, it understood now, the one mistake its
kind can make that cannot be undone by patience:
it had decided, on the first day, that an enemy was
beneath its notice, and it had been wrong, and it
had given that enemy eighteen unwatched years to
become whatever she had become. And it turned,
for the first time since the morning she was born,
the whole of its cold attention across the water, and
looked at her, and was appalled by what it saw.
   For she could not be reached. That was the horror
of her. The boy had been a hundred open doors —
vanity, ambition, loneliness, pride, the hunger to be
exceptional, a dozen sweet handholds by which a
patient climber could go up and in. She had none.
It searched her, that night, as a thief searches a
wall in the dark for the one loose stone, running its

                         319

cold attention over the whole surface of her — and
found no greed in her to swell, no vanity to flatter,
no bitterness to feed, no despair to widen, not one
of the thousand familiar cracks. She wanted nothing
it could offer. She feared nothing it could threaten.
She had built her whole still life, without ever once
knowing there was a builder watching, out of ex-
actly the materials that gave it nothing to grip: con-
tentment, and patience, and a turning-toward that
never wavered, and an ache that, by some infuri-
ating reflex, she carried straight to the one place
it could not follow her into and laid down and was
eased. How, it asked itself, in something close to
despair of its own, do you stop a soul you cannot
tempt? How do you turn one that will not be turned?
   And it found an answer. It was not a clever an-
swer; the cold thing was capable of great clever-
ness, but this was older and simpler and crueller
than cleverness, and it had been used against the
faithful since the very first of them. If a soul cannot
be moved from within, the cold thing reasoned, then
it must be struck from without. You cannot make her
want what is wrong; very well; you can take from her
what is right. You cannot widen a crack that is not
there; very well; you can swing a hammer. Send her
grief. Send her loss. Send her, who has borne her
poverty so sweetly, a poverty sharper than she has
yet been asked to bear; take from her the things she
loves; pile the hard dark hours on her one upon an-
other until the sweet still patient creature looks up
at last from the wreckage and asks the question that
has broken better souls than hers — why me; why,

                         320

when I have been faithful; why does He do this to
me — and in the asking turns, even a hairsbreadth,
even for an instant, from open to bitter, from lean-
ing toward to drawing back. That was all it needed.
One degree of turning. One night of why. And it be-
gan, that very night, within the narrow bounds it
was permitted and not one inch beyond, to arrange
the falling of the hammer.

                       •   • •

The believing ones felt it turn. They felt the vast cold
attention swing across the water and settle, for the
first time, upon the girl in the steep streets, and they
understood what it meant, and Layth was on his feet
in an instant, all fierceness, ready to do they knew
not what; and the Old One stilled him with a look.
”We cannot fight it for her,” he said, and his ancient
voice was heavy. ”That is not given to us. We may
not take the hammer out of its hand; we did not put
it there, and the hand that did has reasons we are
too small to see. We may stand near. We may steady
what is permitted to be steadied. We may carry a
word, if a word is asked for. And we may do the
one thing that is never forbidden to any creature in
any world.” ”Which is?” said Wahb, softly, though he
knew. ”We may pray for her,” said the Old One. ”As
we pray for ourselves. And we may hope, against ev-
erything that is coming, that what was built so well
will stand.” And the four of them, the small unlikely
faithful band of the people of fire, turned together
toward the One they had each, in the long ruin of

                           321

their long lives, turned toward, and asked Him to
hold the girl who did not know she was about to be
tried; and then they could do no more; and they drew
near to the steep streets, and they waited, and they
were afraid.
   And down in the warm crowded flat, on that same
night, the girl herself slept the deep clean sleep of
someone who has done a long day\{}’s good work
and washed and prayed and laid the whole of it
down; and far to the north, in a cold bright apart-
ment over a black canal, the young man sat up late
over his brilliant pages and felt, for no reason he
could name, a sudden chill cross the back of his
neck, and pulled his coat closer, and worked on;
and neither of them knew — how could they have
known? — that the unseen world had bent its whole
attention upon them that night; that a chord eigh-
teen years silent had been heard at last by every-
thing that had ears to hear it in the dark; that the
faithful of two races had begun to pray and the cruel
of one to plot; and that a hammer they could not see
had begun, somewhere above the steep gold streets,
to fall.




                        322

###SECTION: Interlude — The Hidden Ease###
The Hidden Ease



           Between the Veil and the Trial




                      •    • •


There is a place that is not a place, at the bound-
ary of the first garden, beneath the great unkindled
canopy where the stars had not yet been set to their
turning and the light had as yet no source that any
made thing would ever be able to name; and there,
at the station appointed to him before standing was
a thing that could be done, the Archangel kept his
watch. Jibrāʾīl — who had borne the weight of the
worlds’ first errands, who would one day bear the
Word itself down through the seven heavens into
the trembling heart of an unlettered man in a cave;
who loved the children of clay with a love that was
the very nearest a being of pure and undivided light
could come to grief — bent the whole of his vast

                          323

and patient attention down the long reach of the not-
yet, past the numberless threads of all the lives that
were and had been and were yet to be, and fixed it
upon a single one; and he watched.
   He was watching a girl. She was small, and poor,
and not yet grown; she lived in a steep eastern quar-
ter four streets wide, in a warm crowded flat above a
lane that smelled of bread and the sea; and she had
lately laid her grandmother in the earth with her
own hands and her own steadiness, and had found,
in the doing of it, the work that she had been made
for before the worlds were made. He had watched
her since her first dawn — since the morning the
cold thing crossed the water and looked at her once
and turned away, finding nothing in her to take hold
of; for she was, of all the threads he kept, the most
whole, the most quiet, the one whose turning toward
her Lord had never, from her first breath, so much
as wavered. She was, he would have said, had any
asked him, the gentlest of all the lights then living
in the world.
   And he saw what was coming for her.
   He saw it poised above her unknowing head the
way one sees, from a great height, the shadow of
a thing before the thing itself: the hardship, mea-
sured and ready and very near — the strong father
who would fall and not rise whole; the bright door,
only just opened, that would close again with the
soft inexorable gentleness of all the doors that close
in such houses; the long grey grinding year, and
the loneliness in it, and the night — he saw that
night too, far off and certain — when the still faith-

                         324

ful girl who had never once failed to turn the right
way would kneel at the bottom of everything and
very nearly, for the first and only time, not be able
to turn at all.
   And the Archangel was moved; and the motion
in him was not doubt — there was no doubt any-
where in the whole vast reach of him, nor had there
ever been, nor could there be; doubt was simply not
among the things that he had been given the power
to do. The motion in him was the old protective grief,
the ache of the watcher who is permitted to see, and
to love, and never to reach; and out of that grief, as
once before at the forming of the clay a question
had risen among the ranks of the watching host, a
question rose in him now — not a challenge, for the
creature of light does not challenge; not an objec-
tion, for he had seen enough of the wisdom of his
Lord to object to nothing; but a question asked as a
child asks, to be taught: why this one. Why the gen-
tlest. Why the soul that never turned away should
be the very soul to be loaded until it bent; why the
hammer should be raised over the one head in all
that quarter that had never once been lifted in com-
plaint. He had watched the children of clay shatter,
in their long sad history, under so very much less.
   He did not speak it; there was no speaking, and
no need; the question was known, in the instant of
its rising, by the One to whom all things are known
before ever they are. And he was answered — not
as he had half-braced to be answered, with a reason
set out plain and flat that he might hold it up and
take its measure; not in words at all, for the matter

                         325

was far too great for the smallness of any word. He
was answered as the deep sea answers a stone let
fall into it: the whole of reality moved, soundlessly,
all at once, and where there had been a question
there was now a widening; and into the widening,
gift upon gift, he was shown.
   He was shown the hardship — and shown, folded
inside it, knit through its very fibre where no eye
in all the heavens but the highest could have found
it, the ease. Not the ease that waits on the far side
of a hardship, the dawn that comes after the night
and is a separate and a later thing; but an ease wo-
ven into the hardship itself, inseparable from it, the
sweet kernel set inside the bitter shell so that the
one could not be taken without the other — so that
the very breaking he had grieved to see was not
the enemy of her mercy but the vessel that carried
it, and the night at the bottom of everything held,
folded in its own black heart, the dawn that would
break her open into more than she had ever been.
   He was shown the weight — and shown that it had
been measured. Every ounce of it; weighed, before
it was laid on her, against the exact and particular
strength that had been knit into her and no other;
set to the very edge of what she could bear and not
one grain beyond, because it is a law woven into
the floor of all the worlds that no soul is ever bur-
dened past its carrying — and he understood, being
shown it, that the measuring was not arithmetic but
tenderness; that the hand which raised the hammer
had first, and with infinite care, taken the measure



                         326

of the one it would fall upon, and loved her in the
measuring.
   He was shown the door — the bright door clos-
ing, the future taken from her in the very hour it
had been given — and shown that the taking did not
mean the thing its closing seemed to mean. For the
road that was being shut in the east was being shut
to bend her toward another; and the other ran, by
ways she could not see and would have refused out-
right had she been shown them, toward a far grey
city across a sea, and a house for the dying, and the
precise and particular work that her whole self had
been shaped, before her birth, to do. She could not
be led to the thing she was for except down the one
road she would never, of her own will, have chosen;
and so the thing she would weep to lose was, in a
truth too high for her to reach from where she knelt,
the very best of all the gifts she would ever be given
— and the bitterest, and the two were the same gift,
and always had been.
   And folded through the whole of it — beneath the
hardship and the measured weight and the door that
closed in mercy — there was one reality that is the
ground and floor of every other, and it came into
the Archangel not as a sentence to be parsed but as
a single overwhelming soundless certainty, vast as
the moving of the whole sea: that His mercy encom-
passes all things. All of them. The hardship and the
ease alike; the falling father and the closing door;
the grey years and the black night and the small
kneeling girl at the bottom of them — all of it held,
every grain of it, inside a mercy that had no edge

                         327

anywhere that he could find, however far he was
given to look.
  So Jibrāʾīl was given to know what he had not
known; and his grief was not taken from him — for
he loved her, and the cost was real, and the love did
not pretend the cost away — but it was turned, in
the knowing, from the grief of one who fears for a
thing into the grief of one who trusts the hand that
holds it: a grief with the whole weight of the worlds’
mercy underneath it now, and so no longer heavy in
the way it had been heavy a moment before.
  And he turned his vast attention back to the steep
streets, to the small sleeping girl who did not know
that the whole of Heaven loved her, nor that the
hardest year of her life would begin with the rising
of the very next sun, nor that the ease had already
been folded, and was already waiting, in the very
heart of the stone that was about to fall upon her.
He watched her stir in her sleep, in the grey hour
before the dawn, and turn, without waking, as she
always turned, toward the far house of her Lord. He
kept his watch over her. He had been shown, and he
trusted, and he grieved, and all three at once; and
he did not look away.
  And the stone began to fall.



                  BOOK TWO · ACT V
                       • • •



                   The Dream

                        328

      And He placed between you affection and
                      mercy.



                     •   • •


The Qur’an · Sūrah ar-Rūm (30:21)




                         329

###SECTION: Chapter Thirty-Seven — The Hammer###
The Hammer

 The hammer fell on an ordinary Tuesday, in the
middle of the afternoon, while she was three streets
away buying lentils.
   Her father was a man who worked with his hands
— had worked with them every day of his life, lifting
and carrying and mending, a strong quiet man who
had never once been ill that anyone could remember
— and he was up a ladder in another man\{}’s work-
shop when the thing happened in his head, the sud-
den silent catastrophe that no one saw coming and
no one could have stopped, and he came down the
ladder not as a man climbs down but as a sack falls,
and by the time they got to him the whole right side
of him had gone, his face and his arm and his leg and
his speech, all of it, in the space of a breath. Hana
came home with the lentils to a flat full of weeping
and a father on the floor who looked at her out of
one working eye and could not say her name; and
that was the end of the world she had known, and
the beginning of the year that nearly broke her.
   He lived. That was the mercy, and it was a real
one, and she held to it; he lived. But the man who

                        330

came home from the hospital weeks later was not
the man who had gone up the ladder. He could not
work; he would never work again; he could barely
cross the room on the arm of one of his sons, and
the effort of it shamed him so deeply that he wept,
this strong proud quiet man, wept like a child at the
table, and there was nothing any of them could do
but look away and pretend, for his sake, that they
had not seen. And the family that had been poor but
standing was, within a season, poor and falling; for
he had been the wage of the house, and the wage of
the house was gone, and there were the little ones
still, and the rent, and the medicines that cost what
none of them had, and the slow grinding arithmetic
of a poverty that had always been just bearable and
was, now, every month, a little less so.
   And the door that had opened for her closed.
   No one slammed it. That would almost have been
easier. It closed as such doors close in such houses
— gently, reasonably, with love, and without anyone
ever quite saying the words. There were simply too
many mouths and too little money and one grown
daughter with two strong hands and a clear head,
sitting in a lecture hall across the city learning the
names of bones, while her mother aged ten years in
one and her brothers left school to run errands for
coins and her father wept at the table.

                       •   • •

There was an evening, before any of the deciding
was final, when her youngest brother left his wet

                           331

boots square in the middle of the hallway floor for
the third time that week, and Hana, coming in ex-
hausted from the ward with an exam in eleven hours
she had not yet opened a single page toward, did
not step around them. She kicked them, hard, into
the wall, and said something to him that she would
be ashamed of for years and never quite apologize
for properly, something about how none of them had
the faintest idea what her days actually cost her, and
how she was so tired of being the strong one, so un-
believably tired of it, that she could have lain down
on the wet floor right there among his stupid boots
and simply not gotten up. He was eleven. He cried.
She did not comfort him. She went to her room and
sat on the bed in the dark, not praying, not reach-
ing for anything, simply and uselessly furious — at
the boots, at the exam, at her brother’s face, at the
whole unfair shape of a life where the capable were
expected to go on being capable forever, for noth-
ing, for no one’s thanks — and she stayed furious
for a long time before the fury finally, grudgingly,
gave way to guilt, and she went and knocked on his
door, and neither of them mentioned it again. It was
not a test of anything. It was only a girl who had not
slept enough, being unkind to a child who had left
his boots in the hall.
   No one told her to stop the school. They did not
have to. She saw it herself, lying awake at night
doing the same grinding arithmetic the whole fam-
ily did in its sleep now: that the money the schol-
arship did not cover had to come from somewhere;
that the hours she spent at the hospital were hours

                         332

she was not earning; that there was work she could
get, real work, the work poor capable girls get, that
would put food on the table tomorrow instead of in
some shining doctor\{}’s future five and ten and fif-
teen years away; and that to go on, to cling to the
door, to spend on her own far-off calling the money
and the hours her broken father and her frightened
mother and her hungry brothers needed today —
would be a kind of theft. A selfishness dressed up
as a dream. She knew what she had to do. She had
always known what she had to do; it had been the
easiest thing in the world, her whole life, to know
the right thing and do it. So she did it. She went,
dry-eyed, to the tired kind doctor who had believed
in her, and she said that she was sorry, and that
her family needed her, and that she would not be
continuing; and the doctor looked at her for a long
time with something breaking in her own face, and
did not argue, because she had seen this before, a
hundred times, the poor and the gifted set against
each other, and she knew there was no arguing with
it. And Hana went home, and folded the dream up
small, and put it away, and took the work that was
offered, long hours of it, hard and dull and far be-
neath what she had been made for; and told herself
it was right, because it was right; and waited to feel
the peace that doing the right thing had always, her
whole life, brought her.
   And the peace did not come.
   That was the thing she had not been ready for;
that was the hammer under the hammer. She had
borne the poverty, and the fear, and the long grey

                         333

exhausting days; she had borne her father\{}’s
weeping and her mother\{}’s ageing and the death
of the shining future; she had borne all of it the way
she had been built to bear things, by laying it down
each night before the One who held it all. But this
time, when she knelt in the dark to lay it down, some-
thing in her would not let go of it. It sat in her chest
like a stone that would not be prayed away. And un-
derneath it, for the first time in the whole of her still
and faithful life, a small voice had begun to speak
that was not her own and wore her own exactly, and
it asked her the oldest question there is, the one
the cold thing had crossed the water to plant, and it
asked it gently, and reasonably, and over and over
in the dark: why. Why you. You, who never wanted
anything for yourself; you, who gave away what you
did not have; you, who sat with the dying when ev-
eryone else ran — and the one time, the one single
time, a door opened for you, the one good thing that
was ever going to be yours, He took it. Why would
He do that? You were faithful. You were faithful ev-
ery single day, and this is what faithfulness earns.
Where is He now, your warm Presence, your near
One, on whom you have leaned your whole life like
a fool leaning on a painted wall? Lean now. Go on.
Lean now and see what holds you.
   And the terrible thing, the thing that frightened
her more than the poverty and more than her fa-
ther\{}’s ruined face, was that she had no answer
for it. She had never needed an answer before. The
leaning had always simply worked; she had turned,
and been met, and been eased, as surely as a stone

                          334

dropped from the hand falls down. Now she turned,
in the dark, in the small hours, with the stone in
her chest — and the warmth that had met her ev-
ery day since before she could remember was not
there. Or she could not find it. She did not know
which, and the not-knowing was its own fresh ter-
ror. She said the names on her fingers and they were
only words. She stood for the prayer and her heart
stood off at a cold distance and watched her mouth
move. She read the verses she had loved and they
lay flat on the page like pressed dead flowers. And
the small reasonable voice was there for all of it,
patient, sympathetic, almost kind: you see? There
is nothing there. There never was. You built your
whole life on a warmth that was only ever your own
wishing, and now that you need it, now that you are
truly in the dark, look — it is gone, and you are alone,
as everyone is finally alone, and the only difference
between you and the clever ones who admitted it
long ago is that you were too simple to see it until
now.
   She did not believe the voice, and that disbelief
was the whole of why she did not fall: some deep
grain of her, the turning-toward that had been set
in her before the world and could not be wholly un-
set by any grief, did not believe it, even now, even
empty, even in the worst of the dark. But — and this
was new, and this was the danger, and the cold thing
felt the danger and pressed — she was, for the first
time in her life, listening to it. She was lying in the
dark at the bottom of everything, too tired to weep
and too empty to pray, with the stone in her chest

                          335

and the kind cruel voice in her ear, and she was, just
barely, beginning to let it talk. One degree of turn-
ing. That was all it had ever wanted. From open to
a hairsbreadth less than open; from leaning-toward
to merely standing still; from a heart that gave it-
self away without counting to a heart that had be-
gun, just begun, in its exhaustion and its grief, to
fold its arms and ask to be paid back. It was not
bitterness yet. But it was the first cold edge of the
place where bitterness begins, and she had never
in her life stood so near it, and she did not have the
strength left to step back, and she knew it.

                       •   • •

The unseen ones knew it too.
   The cold thing was close now, closer than it had
ever been permitted to come to her, drawn in by
the opening it had made, leaning over the exhausted
girl in the dark and pouring the patient reasonable
poison into the one small crack that eighteen years
of faithfulness had finally, under enough weight, be-
gun to show. It could not make her turn. It still could
not make her do anything; that law held, as it holds
for all of them, to the very end. But it could talk,
and she was listening, and it had broken stronger
souls than hers with less; and it bent close over the
low note it had once dismissed as no sport, and it
did not gloat, because it was too old and too seri-
ous for gloating when the kill was this near, and it
simply pressed, and pressed, and waited for the one
degree.

                           336

   And the believing ones stood off in the dark and
could not reach her. They had drawn as near as they
were allowed, the small faithful band of them, Layth
rigid with a fury he could do nothing with, Wahb
murmuring, Sadira watching the thing she could not
stop, the Old One grave as stone; and they did the
only thing that was given them to do, which was to
pray for her, and to hope; but they could not put one
word of comfort into her where the cold thing was
putting its poison, because that is not how it works,
because the heart is the one country no creature
may enter uninvited, neither the dark ones nor the
bright, and what happened in her now would hap-
pen by her own turning and no one else\{}’s. Her
own guardian stood nearest of all, the one that had
kept her since her first hour, and it was in an an-
guish that such beings rarely know, because it could
see how close she was, and it loved her, and it could
not save her; it could only stand, and keep its use-
less faithful post, and wait, with the rest of Heaven,
to see which way, at the very bottom of the dark, the
still girl who had never yet failed to turn the right
way would turn.
   She knelt that last night on the cold floor when
the house was asleep, as she had knelt ten thousand
times; and she tried, one more time, to lay it down;
and she could not; and the voice said, softly, almost
tenderly, there now. You see. You can stop pretend-
ing. Let go. Just let go; it will be such a relief; you
have carried it so long. And Hana knelt in the dark
with the stone in her chest and the warmth nowhere
to be found and the kind voice in her ear and the

                         337

whole weight of the ruined year on her bowed shoul-
ders, and she opened her mouth to pray, and noth-
ing came; and she felt herself, at last, at the very
bottom of everything, begin — slowly, exhaustedly,
almost without deciding to — to fold her arms; to
draw the smallest fraction back; to turn, by that one
fatal degree, away.




                        338

###SECTION: Chapter Thirty-Eight — The Chord###
The Chord

 She did not, in the end, turn away. She did some-
thing that, for her, in that hour, was nearer to defeat
than turning: she gave out. The body has its mer-
cies, and one of them is that it cannot kneel at the
bottom of the dark forever; and somewhere past the
worst of the night, mid-prayer, mid-turning, with the
stone still in her chest and the words still dead in her
mouth and the cold voice still murmuring its patient
poison, she simply went down — sank sideways off
her knees onto the thin mat on the cold floor, too
spent to reach her bed, too emptied even to weep,
and fell, between one ragged breath and the next,
into the bottomless sleep of the truly exhausted. The
cold thing, bent close, took it for surrender. It had
seen a thousand souls go down like that, folded and
beaten, and not get up the same; and it eased back,
almost satisfied, to wait for the bitter waking. It had
forgotten — for even it could forget, in its long cer-
tainty, the one law that had undone it once before —
that there is a country the sleeping go to, sometimes,
that no creature of its kind has ever once been per-
mitted to enter.

                         339

   And she went there now.
   It did not feel like falling asleep. It felt like surfac-
ing — like coming up, after a long time held under,
into air. The cold floor went out from under her and
the cold voice thinned to nothing and the dark that
had been pressing on her for a year began, by slow
degrees and then all at once, to fill with light: not a
light that fell from anywhere, for there was nowhere
for it to fall from, but a light that simply was, on ev-
ery side, as the warmth had been on every side at
the very beginning of her, before the streets and the
steep stairs and her own name. And she knew this
place. That was the first impossible thing. She had
never been here in her life and she knew it as you
know your mother\{}’s face, knew it in some part
of her far below memory, below thought, below the
whole grey weight of the forgetting; and the know-
ing broke over her not as a fact but as a homecom-
ing, so that the first thing she did, in the dream, with
no idea why, was to begin helplessly to weep — not
the dry beaten not-weeping of the cold floor, but a
flood, a breaking, the weeping of a child who has
been lost in a crowd for so long that it has stopped
expecting ever to be found, at the instant it is lifted
up into the arms it had given up on.
   For the warmth was there. The warmth she had
lost; the warmth she had begun, in her exhaustion,
to believe had never been real; the near, vast, gen-
tle warmth that had met her at the very beginning
and that she had leaned toward every day of her life
and that had gone, this last terrible year, so silent
and so far — it was here, it had never not been here,

                           340

it folded round her now on every side so completely
that she understood, in the wordless way that things
were understood in this place, that it had not gone
anywhere at all; that it had been here the whole
black year; that the silence had been in her, in the
stone and the grief and the exhaustion, never once
in Him; that He had been exactly this near the entire
time she had knelt on the cold floor and felt nothing,
near as warmth on the face, near as the next breath,
only veiled from her by a grief too heavy for her to
see past. The why she had been asking did not get
answered. It got drowned. It simply dissolved, as
a question about the dark dissolves when the sun
comes up: not refuted, not argued down, only made
suddenly and forever beside the point; and the stone
in her chest, that no prayer had been able to shift,
came loose all at once in the flood of the warmth and
was gone, and she was, for the first time in a year,
and more than she had ever been in her waking life,
light.

                       •   • •

And then she became aware that she was not alone
in the light; and this was the second impossible
thing, and the greater.
  There was another. Near her in the warmth —
though near and far had no real meaning here,
where there was no space for them to measure
— there was another presence, another brightness,
turned toward her exactly as she found, now, that
she was turned toward it; and the instant she be-

                           341

came aware of it, something in her that had been
incomplete for the whole of her life without her
ever once knowing it was incomplete, leapt toward
it with a recognition so total, so glad, so utterly
without doubt, that it made the homecoming of the
warmth itself seem only the half of a thing now sud-
denly made whole. She knew this one too. Not as
she knew the warmth — not as the source, not as the
One — but as a fellow; as the other half of some old
and broken music; as the very someone whose loss
had carved the person-shaped hollow in her that she
had carried, aching and nameless, for a year and
could never explain. Here it was. Here was the miss-
ing one. Not lost at all — only here, only waiting,
only on the far side of a forgetting as deep as her
own.
   It was high where she was low. That was the thing
she felt first and knew longest: that it sounded high
and quick and bright where she sounded low and
still and deep, the two of them as different as two
notes can be — and that the difference was not a dis-
tance but a fitting: a key fitted to its lock, made so
on purpose; the low string and the high string of one
instrument, tuned apart precisely so that, struck to-
gether, they can make the one sound that neither
could ever make alone. And they were struck to-
gether now. She could not have said who moved,
or whether either moved, or whether the moving
was something the warmth itself did to the two of
them, drawing them by the grain of how they had
each been made; she knew only that the low note
in her and the high note in the other leaned, and

                         342

met, and sounded — and the sound they made, the
third sound, the chord, the one that had been struck
once at the beginning of everything and left to ring
and then torn silent for two whole lives — the sound
of it went out through the whole of the lit country
and through the whole of her and woke, all at once,
below every layer of the long forgetting, in a sin-
gle blaze that no grief and no poison and no clever
bolted door could have survived for one instant, the
thing they had both been made to remember and
had both forgotten: the great Yes. The yes she had
said, with her whole self, before she had a self; the
yes to a question she could no longer hear but felt
the shape of in every fibre of her; the yes that was
the truest thing she had ever done or ever would,
sounding again now in the chord as if it had never
stopped, so that she understood — weeping, blaz-
ing, whole — that she had been His before the world,
and was His in the world, and would be His past the
end of the world, and that the someone turned to-
ward her in the light had been promised to her in
the same breath in which she had been promised to
Him, and that none of it, none of it, not one thread
of it, had ever for one instant been in doubt.
   The cold thing was not there, and that absence
was the heart of why the night went as it went: for
all its age and all its patience and all its long cold
cleverness, it stood outside that country and could
not come in, could not so much as see in, could only
rage at the sealed edge of a thing it had no power
to touch — for there are places the whisper cannot
go, and the deepest sleep of a soul that God means

                         343

to comfort is one of them, and the great Yes when it
sounds is another, and the cold thing beat against
the outside of the dream the whole night long like a
moth against a lit window and got nothing for it but
the burning. Everything it had built that year, every
patient grain of the poison, every degree it had so
nearly turned her — all of it was, in the single sound-
ing of that chord, simply washed away, not fought,
not answered, only outshone; and it knew, crouched
at the burning edge, that it had lost her, and worse
than lost her: that she would wake remembering,
somewhere below remembering, that the dark had a
floor, and that the floor held, and that she had been
carried across the worst of it in arms she could not
see; and that a soul which has once known that, all
the way down, in the very bottom of its worst night,
is a soul that has slipped its hook for good.

                       •   • •

She woke on the cold floor in the grey before dawn
with her face wet and the mat creased into her cheek
and the call just beginning to lift from the minaret
up the hill; and she was weeping still, but it was
the other weeping now, the full kind, the kind that
empties nothing; and the stone was gone from her
chest; and the warmth was there, near as her own
breath, exactly where it had always been and always
would be, and she lay on the floor in the grey light
and leaned into it and was met, at once, without a
hairsbreadth of distance, as she had been met ev-
ery day of her life before the hard year and would

                           344

be met every day after it. She did not understand
what had happened. She could not have told you
one clear thing about the dream; it was already go-
ing, as dreams go, the light of it draining even as
she reached for it, until all that was left was the
warmth, and the wet on her face, and a single cer-
tainty with no picture attached to it: that she was
loved, that she had always been loved, that the door
was not closed and had never been closed, and —
strangest of all, the one shard of the dream that
would not drain away — that somewhere in the wide
world there was a someone she had been made for
and need not grieve, because he was real, and he
was kept, and the missing of him was not a wound
after all but a promise. She got up off the floor.
She washed her wet face in the cold water. And
she stood, in the first grey light, with the hard un-
changed year still waiting for her on the other side
of the prayer — the broken father, the closed door,
the long work — all of it exactly as heavy as it had
been; and she turned toward Him, easily, gladly,
wholly, as she had not been able to turn for a year;
and she found that she could carry it now; and she
carried it.

                      •   • •

And two thousand miles to the north, in the cold
clean dark of an apartment over a black canal, the
same dream came to a young man who had bolted
every door he had; and it came in anyway, because
it did not come by any door.

                          345

   He had no warning of it and no defence against it,
because the defences he had spent ten years build-
ing were all built against the things that come from
outside, the arguments and the appeals and the tug
of old custom, and this did not come from outside;
it came up, from underneath, from the one locked
room at the centre of him that he had stopped going
into, the room where a splinter of a sentence and a
nameless ache had lain side by side in the dark for
years. He went down into sleep an ascending man,
brilliant and bolted and dry-eyed as he had trained
himself to be; and in the sleep the floor of the locked
room gave way, and he fell through it into light, into
warmth, into a country he did not know he knew,
and there — helpless, undefended, stripped in an in-
stant of every clever thing he had ever built — he
heard, for the first time since before the world, the
low note answer his own, and felt the chord sound,
and remembered, for the space of one impossible
dream, the great Yes he had spent his whole life rea-
soning his way out of.
   He woke with his face wet.
   He lay rigid in the dark with his heart slamming
and his cheeks soaked and no idea on earth what
had happened to him, and he put his hand to his
own face and felt the wet there and stared at his
wet fingers in the dark as though they belonged to
someone else, because he could not remember the
last time he had wept, could not remember how, had
trained it out of himself at thirteen in a long cold dor-
mitory and had thought it gone for good; and here
it was, his face running with it, in the middle of the

                          346

night, for no reason he could name. He could not
remember the dream. It was gone almost before he
was awake, drained away past every grasping of his
quick clever mind, which scrabbled after it now in
the dark and came up holding nothing — nothing
but a wet face, and a slamming heart, and a grief
with no object so vast and so precise that it dou-
bled him over on the edge of the bed, a grief that
was somehow also, unbearably, a kind of joy, the
grief of a man who has been shown for one instant
something he lost so long ago that he had managed
to forget he had ever had it to lose. There was no
word for it. He, who had a word for everything, who
could argue any case from either side, sat on the
edge of his bed in the dark with the wet drying on
his face and reached for a word and found, for the
first time in his articulate life, that there was sim-
ply none; that whatever had been in the dream was
a thing his whole formidable apparatus of language
could not get the smallest grip on; that it had come
from under the floor of everything he knew how to
say.
   And because he was who the long years had made
him, he did, in the end, the only thing he knew how
to do with a thing he could not solve. He breathed.
He waited for his heart to slow. He told himself it
was a dream, only a dream, that everyone has them,
that the body does strange things in the small hours,
that it meant nothing and would be gone by break-
fast; and he was so practised, and the telling was
so smooth, that by the time the grey northern dawn
came up over the canal he had very nearly put it

                         347

away — had folded it small, as he folded everything,
and carried it to the locked room to file it with the
splinter and the ache. But the door of the locked
room, when he came to it that morning, would not
quite shut again. He pushed at it, as you push at a
drawer that will no longer close, and it gave a little
and held and would not seat; and he stood there a
moment in the dark of himself with his hand against
it, frowning, unsettled in a way his cleverness could
not reach; and then he gave it up, and went out
into the bright hard purposeful day to be formidable,
with the door at the centre of him standing, for the
first time in years, the smallest crack ajar — and a
thin line of warmth, too faint for him to feel yet, com-
ing through it from a country he had spent his whole
life proving did not exist.

                       •   • •

So the chord was struck at last, once, in a single
night, across the whole width of the turning world;
and the two who had been made for it woke on the
same grey dawn with the same wet on their faces
and no memory of why, the one in the steep gold
streets and the other over the black northern water,
each having dreamed the other and neither knowing
it, each marked by it past undoing and neither able
to say how. What it had begun in them would take
years to finish, and would cost more than either of
them could yet imagine, and is the matter of every-
thing that comes after. But the silence was broken.
The note had been answered. And whatever the cold

                           348

things plotted now, and whatever hard roads still
ran ahead through the long world, one thing had
been settled in the dark that night that would not
be unsettled again: that neither of them, however
far they wandered, was alone; and that the missing,
in both of them, had a name; and that the name,
though they could not yet hear it, was a promise.




                       349

###SECTION: Chapter Thirty-Nine — The Bending of the Roads###
The Bending of the Roads

  The hard years did not end because she had
dreamed a dream. That is not how it works, and she
would have been the first to say so.
   Her father did not rise from his chair and walk;
the money did not come; the door that had closed
did not, for a long time, open again. The dream had
changed none of the facts of her life. What it had
changed was her, and that turned out to be the only
change that mattered, because a fact you can carry
is no longer quite the same fact as a fact that is
crushing you, though it weighs exactly the same.
She carried it. She rose before dawn and washed
and turned toward Him and was met, every day, as
she had been met before the bad year, and the meet-
ing held her up; and she went out to the long hard
dull work that fed the family, and she did it well and
without complaint, and she found — because she
had stopped looking for the peace and so the peace
came back — that even there, even in the grinding
grey of it, the warmth was near. She had learned
the thing in the dream that the bad year had tried

                        350

to unteach her: that He had never once left; that
the hardship was real and was not abandonment;
that ease was folded inside the very heart of the dif-
ficulty, as the sweet kernel is folded inside the bitter
shell, and that the patient who waited would taste
it. She waited. And in time, by a road too long and
too winding to tell here, the ease came.
   It came through the tired kind doctor, who had
never quite let her go; who found, two years on, a
way — a training for nurses first, which a poor girl
could do at night and be paid a little for, and which
led, for the rare ones, the ones with the thing that
could not be taught, to a door back into the wider
calling that had seemed closed for good. Hana took
it. She trained, exhausted, in the dark hours after
the long days, and she was not the quickest but she
was, in the one ward where it counted, the best they
had ever seen, because she walked into the rooms
where people were dying and the rooms changed;
and word of her spread, quietly, as word of such a
gift always spreads, from the bedsides outward, un-
til it reached, in time, the people who gather up the
rare ones and send them where the need is greatest.
And the need that called to her, in the end — the call-
ing within the calling — was the care of the dying;
the tending of the ones at the very threshold, with
mercy, and skill, and the refusal to run; and there
was a place, far away, in a great grey northern city
across a sea, that had taught the whole world how
that tending should be done, and that, hearing of
a poor brilliant nurse from a steep eastern quarter
who could sit inside another\{}’s fear and not flinch,

                         351

offered her a fellowship, and a future, and a door
she had long ago stopped letting herself dream of,
standing open at last.
   She wept when it came — not for herself, but for
her grandmother, who had told her, holding her two
small hands in the lamplight a lifetime ago, that she
would one day learn the reason; and she had learned
it, and here was the reason putting out its hand to
lead her into the world. Her family let her go with
their blessing and their tears; her father, who could
not say her name now without great labour, said it,
the whole of it, twice, and would not let her see him
weep. And she went — the still girl from the four
streets, who had been born in a poor back room at
dawn with the call whispered into her ear, who had
buried her grandmother and her own bright future
and very nearly her faith, and had risen from all of
it with the warmth still in her and the gift still in her
hands — she went out at last from the only world she
had ever known, across the water and across a sea,
toward a grey far city and the work she had been
made for; and she did not know, because how could
she have known, that she was being drawn, by the
long invisible bending of the roads, toward the very
place where the other half of an old broken music
had been living, all this while, without her.


                        •   • •


For he was there. He had been there for two years
already, ascending.


                            352

   The dream had not made him good, any more than
it had made her circumstances easy; the dream had
cracked a door, and a thin line of warmth came
through the crack now that had not come through
before, and he could not feel it and did not believe
in it and could not, all the same, quite shut it out.
It changed him in ways he would have denied and
could not have explained. He had become the thing
his father once promised the window he would be —
a lawyer, and a formidable one, rising fast through
the hard bright world of the great cases — but he
had become it crooked, off the line his father had
drawn, for he could not, it turned out, make him-
self fight for the powerful, however they paid; some
flaw in him, some old buried thing that a beating in
a school corridor and a betrayal by a golden friend
had never quite managed to kill, kept turning him,
case after case, toward the ones with no one, the
wronged, the small, the ground-down, the people
the misfiling machine of the world had decided did
not count. He told himself it was strategy; the under-
dog cases made his name; and that was even true,
and it was not the reason, and somewhere under
the locked door he knew it was not the reason. He
fought for the helpless with a strange cold ferocity
that the older men admired and could not account
for, as though he were looking, in every wronged
and frightened face across every cheap table, for
one particular face he had lost and could not name;
and he never found it; and he took the next case,
and the next.



                         353

   He was not at peace. He had everything the
world counts and he carried the old nameless ache
through all of it, sharper since the dream, for the
dream had given the ache a shape it had never had
before — the shape of a someone, a someone real, a
someone missing — and a clever man can do nothing
with the certainty that he is missing a person he has
never met except suffer it, and explain it away, and
suffer it again. He half-looked for her now. That was
the thing he would have died before admitting, the
thing that lived in the locked room with the splin-
ter of an old man\{}’s sentence: that he searched,
without letting himself know he searched, the faces
of every crowd, every courtroom, every grey street,
for one he would know and had never seen. And the
patient cold thing at his shoulder, which had lost
him the great battle in the dream and would not for-
give itself for it, watched him search, and made its
new and colder plan.
   For it had understood, the morning after the
chord, that the war had changed. It could no longer
keep them from remembering; the dream had seen
to that, and the dream was sealed against it, and
what the dream had wakened it could not put back
to sleep. But there was more than one way to ruin a
thing. If they could not be kept from remembering,
and could not, it began to fear, be kept forever from
meeting, then the meeting itself must be made the
weapon: they must be brought together wrong, at
the wrong hour, on the wrong side of some wall, as
adversaries, as strangers who wound one another,
so that the reunion the whole of Heaven was bend-

                        354

ing toward would arrive, when it arrived, as a colli-
sion and not an embrace — so that the two who had
been made to complete one another would meet, at
last, and not know it, and do one another harm, and
part. It was a subtler campaign than the long bright
work of the boy\{}’s forgetting, and a crueller one,
and the cold thing gave the whole of its ancient pa-
tience to it; and it began, that very season, to ar-
range the pieces of the world toward the day the
high note and the low note would stand in the same
room at last — and the day it had chosen to break
them on.

                      •   • •

And so the roads bent in. Hers from the steep gold
east, his already run to ground in the grey north;
her calling carrying her toward the dying, his to-
ward the wronged; mercy and justice, the low note
and the high, the still hands and the fighting tongue,
drawn by every turning of the long slow patient
thing that bends all roads toward itself, toward a
single far city neither had chosen, and a single sea-
son, and a single bridge over a grey river where the
whole hurrying world crossed and recrossed with-
out ever looking up.
  She came on a Tuesday, in the rain, with one case
and a great deal of hope, and was swallowed up at
once into the vast indifferent machinery of the city;
and she did not know he was there; and he did not
know that the air of his own city had changed. But
he felt it. That was the thing none of them — not

                          355

the cold one at his shoulder, which had reckoned
on a slow campaign and many months, not the be-
lieving ones who watched from the fire, not Adel
himself, least of all Adel himself — had reckoned
on: that the chord, once sounded, does not need a
meeting of eyes or an exchange of names to know
its other half has come within reach; that it knows
as the tide knows the moon, across any distance,
through any crowd, under any veil. He was cross-
ing the bridge in the grey afternoon, his collar up,
his mind three cases deep, one stranger among ten
thousand strangers pouring both ways over the grey
river — and the door at the centre of him, the locked
door that had stood for years the smallest crack ajar,
blew suddenly wide, and the thin line of warmth he
had never been able to feel became, between one
step and the next, a flood; and the old nameless ache
rose up in him not as an ache at all now but as a
certainty, vast and wordless and undeniable, that
stopped him dead in the middle of the bridge with
the crowd breaking around him like water around a
stone and his heart slamming against his ribs like a
fist on a door.
   She was near. He did not know how he knew it.
He knew nothing; he had a wet bridge and a grey
river and ten thousand faces and no name, no face,
no reason, nothing at all but the one impossible cer-
tainty roaring up through the broken-open floor of
him after eighteen silent years: that she was here.
That after the whole long forgetting, after the cold
country and the bolted door and the dream he could
not remember and the someone he could not name,

                         356

she was here, now, within the sound of his own
name, somewhere in this hurrying indifferent crowd
on this grey afternoon — and that if he turned, this
instant, and looked, he would find her.
  He turned. And he began to search the faces.




                        357

###SECTION: Chapter Forty — The Turning###
The Turning

 He turned.
   He turned as a man turns who has heard his own
name called from very far off and very long ago, not
with the quick easy motion of a body answering a
sound, but slowly, against the whole weight of eigh-
teen silent years, against the whole architecture of
the careful guarded life he had built to keep exactly
this from happening; he turned the way a key turns
in a lock that has rusted half-shut, with resistance
and then, all at once, with a giving that was almost
pain; and the grey river slid on beneath him, and the
great dome of the cathedral stood pale against the
failing afternoon at the northern end of the bridge,
and the crowd flowed past him in both directions,
the homeward crowd, the indifferent hurrying river
of strangers with their bags and their phones and
their turned-down faces, and Adel stood still in the
middle of all of it, a stone in the stream, and looked.
   He did not know what he was looking for. That
was the unbearable thing, the thing he would turn
over for the rest of his life: that he had no picture

                         358

in his mind, no name on his tongue, no memory to
match against the faces, nothing at all but the roar-
ing in his chest where the silence had been, the roar-
ing that had risen in him on the bridge from some
sealed room under the floor of him that he had not
known was there — the chord, though he had no
word for it, the chord that had slept for eighteen
years and woken now all at once and was sounding
through every bone of him the single unspeakable
note of here, here, here, she is here. He searched
the faces. A businessman. A woman with a pram.
Two students laughing. A tall man in a wet coat.
None of them. All of them wrong, wrong, wrong,
and the roaring climbing, and the afternoon light
going, and the crowd thickening as it does at that
hour when the offices empty all at once into the
streets — and then the crowd, in its blind unknow-
ing motion, parted; some small accident of the flow,
a knot of people stepping left while another stepped
right; a gap opening for the space of three heart-
beats down the length of the bridge; and at the far
end of the gap, perhaps twenty paces off, a woman
had stopped, exactly as he had stopped, a stone in
the same stream, and she was turning, exactly as he
had turned, and she was looking, with a face gone
white and stricken and open, straight back down the
gap at him.
   The world did not stop. That is the lie the books
tell, that in such a moment the world stops; it did
not stop; the river ran and the crowd pushed and
a gull screamed somewhere over the water and a
boat sounded its horn far downstream; the whole

                         359

vast grinding machinery of the indifferent city went
on exactly as before, and that was part of the hor-
ror and part of the glory of it, that the single most
enormous thing that had ever happened to either of
them was happening in the middle of a rush-hour
bridge that did not know and did not care.
   But for the two of them, in the narrow lane of still-
ness that had opened down the moving crowd, ev-
erything that was not the other simply ceased to sig-
nify. He saw her. A woman of about his own age, in a
dark coat, a scarf over her hair, an ordinary woman
whom he had never in his waking life laid eyes on,
a stranger he could not have named or placed or ac-
counted for in any way that would have satisfied the
careful lawyer\{}’s mind he had spent his life per-
fecting — and the careful lawyer\{}’s mind had noth-
ing to say, had been struck dumb and swept clean,
because every other part of him, the deep part, the
sealed-room part, the part that had been waiting
eighteen years and longer, far longer, longer than
he had been alive, had risen up entire and was say-
ing, with a certainty that obliterated argument, with
a recognition that owed nothing to memory and ev-
erything to something older and deeper than mem-
ory: you. It is you. You are the one. You are the miss-
ing thing. You are the name I have never once in
all my life been able to hear, and here you are, and
I know you, I know you, though I have never seen
your face.
   And Hana, twenty paces off, with the bag of small
shopping in her hand and the new cold city all
around her and the strangeness of exile still raw

                          360

on her like a wound that would not skin over, had
felt it come up in her on the bridge the same way —
not as a thought, for there was no thought in it, but
as a rising, a flood, a sudden unsealing, the chord
she had no word for either waking all at once after
its long silence and pouring through her until she
had stopped dead in the middle of the moving crowd
with her heart slamming and her breath gone and
the certainty roaring in her that was not like any-
thing that had ever happened to her in the whole of
her ordered careful faithful life: that someone was
here. That the missing thing she had carried so long,
the absence she had never been able to name, the
hollow that not even the long years of turning to-
ward her Lord had ever quite filled, because it had
not been made to be filled by that, had been made
to be filled by this — was here, now, on this bridge,
in this hour, close, close, unbearably close.
   And she had turned, against the whole weight of
who she was, against all her training in not want-
ing, in accepting, in laying down; she had turned
and looked down the lane of stillness in the crowd;
and she had seen him; a man in a wet dark coat, a
stranger, a man she had never seen, with a face she
did not know turned toward her and gone as white
and open and stricken as she knew her own must
be; and everything in her that was rational and or-
dered and schooled in patience fell silent, and the
deep thing rose, and it said the same word his was
saying, the word that needs no language because it
is older than language: you.



                        361

  Neither of them moved, for the space of a breath;
for the space of another; as two people stand at the
edge of a thing so large that to take the first step
toward it is to fall. The chord between them, drawn
now to its full unbearable tautness across twenty
paces of wet stone and hurrying strangers, sounded
the one note it had been made before the world to
sound, the note of the two who had been turned
toward one another in a place neither could re-
member and had answered, both of them, the same
enormous question with the same enormous Yes —
and the answering was in them now, bodily, total,
though the question and the place and the Yes were
sealed away forever behind the veil that had come
down over both of them at the beginning, and nei-
ther of them knew, and neither of them would ever,
in this life, be given to know, why a stranger\{}’s
face across a bridge should crack the whole world
open like an egg.
  And then — because the body cannot bear such
a thing standing still; because there is a gravity
in it stronger than fear, stronger than the careful
guarded mind, stronger than eighteen years of si-
lence — they began, both of them, in the same in-
stant, without will, without decision, as water moves
downhill, to come toward each other. He took a step.
She took a step. The gap in the crowd held, narrow
and trembling, the lane of stillness down which the
two of them now moved, slowly, through the stream-
ing indifferent river of the city, their eyes locked,
their faces open, the whole of everything that had
ever been between them folded into the closing dis-

                        362

tance — fifteen paces — twelve — and the roaring
in both of them rose toward something that had no
name and needed none, the thing that was about to
happen, the thing that had been coming since be-
fore either of them drew breath, the meeting, the
meeting at last —
   — and a man fell.
   He fell perhaps ten feet from Hana, between her
and Adel, square in the closing lane: an ordinary
man, heavy, grey-suited, walking home like all the
rest, who between one stride and the next simply
folded — his hand going to his chest, his face emp-
tying, his knees unhinging — and went down hard
onto the wet stone of the bridge with the whole loose
finality of a thing whose hour has come. The crowd,
which had not cared and had not noticed and had
flowed indifferent past the largest thing in the world,
cared at once and noticed at once about the smaller,
nearer, more legible catastrophe of a man down on
the stones, and broke, and surged, and closed.
   And Hana — Hana who was made as she was
made, Hana whose whole self had been shaped and
tempered through every hard year into the single
shape of one who goes toward the suffering and
not away from it, Hana the healer, in whom mercy
was not a choice anymore but the very grain of the
wood — Hana turned. Of course she turned. The
deep thing roaring she is here, he is here had to con-
tend in that instant with the other thing, the trained
thing, the holy reflex of nineteen years of running
toward the fallen, and the holy reflex won, because
it was holy, because a man was dying on the stones

                         363

and she had hands that knew what to do, and she did
not even decide it, she was simply already moving,
already down on her knees on the wet stone beside
the grey-suited man, her bag spilling its small shop-
ping into the gutter-stream of the bridge, her fin-
gers at his throat, her voice lifting clear and steady
over the gathering panic — stand back, give him
air, someone call — and the crowd, relieved to be
told, surged in around her and the fallen man in a
tightening knot of backs and shoulders and craning
heads; and the lane of stillness was gone, swallowed,
erased, as though it had never been; and Adel was
on the wrong side of it.
   He fought. He would remember fighting, after-
ward, in the long sleepless reconstructions: that he
had thrown himself forward, shoved, shouldered,
clawed at the wall of strangers that had risen up be-
tween them in the space of three seconds, that he
had shouted something, that he had been shouted
at in turn, that a large man had put a flat hand on
his chest and told him to wait his turn, that someone
had said move back, mate, give them room; that the
crowd, that great mild monstrous indifferent body,
had simply absorbed his desperation as the sea ab-
sorbs a thrown stone, closed over it, and went on
being the sea.
   By the time he won through — and it could not
have been more than a minute, though it was the
longest minute he would ever live — the shape of
things had changed again, as crowds change, fluid
and stupid and absolute. There were uniforms now;
someone in the high-visibility coat of the city, then

                         364

another; the wide authority of the official, the gen-
tle firm hands that move the public back. The grey-
suited man was flat on the stones with a young
paramedic over him and a machine being opened.
And the woman — the woman in the dark coat, the
woman who had been kneeling at his throat, the
woman who was the whole of the world — was being
drawn up and back and aside by a steward\{}’s care-
ful hand, her work done, the professionals arrived,
her place yielded; was being folded, gently, cour-
teously, irresistibly, into the thickening dispersing
churn of the crowd at the bridge\{}’s northern end;
was turning, he could see her turning, her white face
hunting back over the heads and shoulders for the

because he had been swept the other way; and he
flung up his arm, and he called out, the wordless
sound of a man with no name to call; and for one
last instant, across the whole heaving width of the
broken-open crowd, he saw her see him — saw the
white face find him — saw the eyes lock onto his one
final time with everything in them, the recognition,
the grief, the impossible unspeakable knowing —
   — and then the crowd closed, the river of
strangers ran between them thick and final, a bus

                        365

never learned, the voice he had heard speak only
to strangers, the face that was already, even now,
even as he stood there with his arm still half-raised

only thing in all the world he could see.
   High above the bridge, in the light that does not
waver, the one who had kept the watch over the two

fore the river was set in its bed, since before the

the falling of the appointed man in the appointed
hour; the holy reflex; the closing of the crowd; the
last look across the breaking tide. And in the man-

do not doubt, the Archangel asked why the meeting,
so long prepared, so nearly come, must be struck
from their hands in the very last instant by the plain
decree itself, and not even by the malice of the en-
emy but by the simple falling of a man whose time
had come; and he was answered as he was always
answered, not in speech but in the widening of sight:
shown that the nearness had been the mercy and the

real, was here, was findable, was worth the whole of

                          366

a life\{}’s searching — and that they had been given
exactly that, and no more, because more would have
come too soon, before the long road had finished the
slow hard work it had only begun in them; that the
wound of the sundering was itself the seed of the
finding; that the One who had set the term of the
grey-suited man had set also the term of their part-
ing and the term, far off, of their meeting, and that
all the terms were known, and all were just, and all,
though no human eye on the bridge that day could
see it, were merciful.
   Far below, in the cold folded dark, the enemy had
gathered to the bridge as carrion gathers, drawn by

bored eighteen years to keep these two apart and
had felt the whole of that labor tremble on the edge

not his doing; he knew it was not his doing; the
falling of the man was from the other Court entirely,
the Court he hated and could not touch; but he had
been ready, he had been near, he had whispered
into the crowd\{}’s blind surging the small addi-
tions that turn a stumble into a wall — push, hurry,
not your business, move along — and he took the
credit to himself in the way of his kind, who take
credit for the weather. And yet, even in his satisfac-
tion, the old cold worm of his unease turned over
once; for he had seen the two faces in the instant
of the seeing, and he knew, better than any crea-
ture in the three worlds save the watchers above,

                         367

what could not now be undone. They had seen each
other. The recognition was lit. And a thing that has
once been lit, he knew, by long and bitter experi-
ence, can be hidden, can be buried, can be starved
and lied to and walled away for years — but it is the
very devil to put out.
   And at the cold edge of that gathering, unseen,
the small ember that was Qabas had watched it all
with no satisfaction at all, only a grief that startled
him with its size; and he carried it away through
the cracks under the world to the four who kept the
faith in the dark, and Marid al-Qadim heard him, and
bowed his ancient head, and said only: ”Then it has
begun in earnest. They have seen. Now comes the
long part, and the hard part, and the part where ev-
erything we are permitted to do will seem like noth-
ing at all against the weight of it. Keep the watch.
Begin the prayers. And do not, whatever the years
bring, let yourself believe the lie that nothing can
be done. Something can always be done. It is only
small.”
   Adel stood on the bridge until the dusk was full
and the crowd had thinned to nothing and the
paramedics had long since borne the grey-suited
man away under a red blanket to whatever came
next for him, and the lamps burned their patient
orange down the length of the embankment, and
the cold came up off the black water, and still he
stood, at the place where the lane of stillness had
opened and closed, looking north into the city that
had taken her, with his careful ordered life lying
in ruins around him and one single impossible fact

                         368

standing up out of the wreckage entire: that she was
real. That somewhere in the ten thousand streets,
under the same falling dark, breathing the same
cold air, was a living woman with a face he would
now never be able to forget and a name he did not
know, and that the eighteen-year silence in him was
broken past all mending, and that nothing, nothing,
nothing in the whole of his remaining life would mat-
ter beside the finding of her again.
   He did not know her name. He did not know her
street, her work, her country of birth, the sound
of her voice when it spoke to anyone but a dying
stranger. He had nothing. He had a face in the fail-
ing light and a roaring in his chest and a bridge in
the middle of a vast indifferent city, and the dusk
coming down, and the whole long world to search.
   He turned up the collar of his wet coat. And he
began to walk north, into the lights, into the crowd,
into the search that would be the rest of his life;
and far across the dark water, swallowed in another
street, a woman in a dark coat walked on through
the same falling night with her empty hands and her
spilled shopping left behind on the bridge and the
same impossible roaring in her own chest, not know-
ing his name either, not knowing anything, carrying
it the only way she had ever been taught to carry the
unbearable and unanswerable things — toward her
Lord, in the dark, with a patience that was beautiful,
and broken, and would have to be enough.
   They had found each other. And the world had
taken her back in the same breath. And the finding
could never now be unfound, though the whole long

                         369

bending of the road still lay between them, and nei-
ther of them knew, walking away that night into the
two halves of the broken city, whether they would
ever, in all the years that remained, stand so near
again.
